# Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ (Chapter 21) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Name and the Place of the Sūrah**

Al-Anbiyāʾ — "The Prophets" — is the twenty-first sūrah of the written Qur'an, a Makkan revelation of 112 verses. It receives its name from the single most distinctive feature of its content: no other sūrah of the Qur'an gives space to so many prophets in so few verses. Within roughly forty lines of its middle section the sūrah moves from Moses and Aaron, to the long story of Abraham and the fire, to Lot, to Noah, to the judgment of David and Solomon, to the trial of Job, to Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhū l-Kifl, to the man of the fish, to the family of Zakariyya, and at last to Mary herself. The name is also structurally anchored in verse 7, where the sūrah states the principle that gathers the rest: *"And We sent no messenger before thee, but that they were men unto whom We revealed."* If the sūrah were a building, that verse is its keystone; the stories that follow are the arches that spring from it.

In its place in the muṣaf, al-Anbiyāʾ marks the opening of the fifteenth of the thirty juz' — the half-way division of the Book — so that reciters through fourteen centuries have begun the second half of the Qur'an on these verses. It follows Ṭā-Hā, which opens with God's own question to the scorner: *"Have you seen him who took his religion as his play?"* (20:12), and it answers that question by gathering, in a single breath of history, the full company of the prophets — the living proof that religion was never anyone's play, that every age had its witness, and that every witness was human.

**A Makkan Address in a Season of Pressure**

The commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and the later authorities who followed the consensus of the Qur'anic exegetes) agree that al-Anbiyāʾ was revealed in Makkah, in the period when the Quraysh had moved from private doubt to public, organized opposition — the years in which the accusations of sorcery, poetry, and confusion of dreams were hurled from the assembly-halls, in which the demand for "a sign like those of old" was repeated as a set piece, and in which the sūrah's sharp verses on the fate of past nations (vv. 11–15) and on the nearness of the reckoning (v. 1) carry the unmistakable tone of a persecuted community being told, again and again, that it is not alone. A few scholars have held that some of its verses are Madinan, or that the sūrah is mixed; but the dominant judgment, and the one the source commentary follows, is that the sūrah is Makkan in its whole character: it argues rather than legislates, invites by signs rather than obliges by rulings, and defends the prophet rather than directing the polity.

The historical pressure is legible in the text itself. The opening ten verses are a compact record of the Makkan case against the Prophet ﷺ — disbelief, mockery, the accusation of sorcery, the accusation of poetry, the demand for a miracle — and each charge receives an immediate answer. Thereafter the sūrah widens from the courtroom of Makkah to the courtroom of history, and then to the courtroom of the heavens: the signs in the sky (vv. 30–33), the certainty of death (vv. 34–47), and the scales that no human court has ever built (v. 47). By the time the prophets are called one by one to stand as witnesses (vv. 48–91), the audience has already been shown the principles by which they are to be judged: the Oneness of God, the universality of the trial, and the justice of the final reckoning.

**The Architecture of the Sūrah**

The sūrah moves in six clear movements, and its beauty is that each movement hands the argument to the next:

1. **The warning and the accusations (vv. 1–10).** The reckoning draws near while mankind turns away; the charges of sorcery and poetry are met with the divine reply that God hears the secret council and that the prophets were never anything but men.
2. **The destroyed nations (vv. 11–15).** Towns that did wrong were shattered and replaced; when they felt God's Might they fled, were taunted with their own luxury, and cried their confession too late.
3. **The proof of Oneness (vv. 16–29).** Creation was not in play; a second god is a logical impossibility; the angels are not daughters of the Compassionate but honored servants who neither precede God in speech nor intercede without His contentment.
4. **The signs in creation and the certainty of death (vv. 30–47).** The heavens and the earth were rent asunder from a single fabric; the mountains anchor the earth; the sky is a protected canopy; every soul will taste death; and the just scales will not be cheated even of a mustard seed.
5. **The prophets themselves (vv. 48–91).** Moses and Aaron with their light; Abraham and the idols and the fire; Lot; Noah; David and Solomon and the garden; Job and his patience; Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhū l-Kifl; the man of the fish; Zachariah and John; and Mary, sealed by the Spirit.
6. **The closing vision (vv. 92–112).** One community that fragmented; the scales and the Book of deeds; Gog and Magog and the fixed stare of the heedless; the idols becoming fuel for Hell; the inheritance of the earth by the righteous; the Prophet ﷺ as mercy to the worlds; the declaration of the message proclaimed equally; and the final prayer: *"My Lord, judge with truth!"*

What the structure teaches is method. The sūrah does not begin with story. It begins with principle — the nearness of judgment, the Oneness of God, the certainty of death — and only then lets the prophets speak. The stories are not decoration hung on the doctrine; they are its evidence. Abraham is not told so that we may admire Abraham, but so that we may see Oneness defended in flesh and bone, mocked, broken into pieces with an axe, and burned — and standing.

**The Rhetorical Style: Question, Answer, and Echo**

Al-Anbiyāʾ is written in a style of its own. It is full of the short, staccato Makkan rhythm — *"Will they not, then, believe?"* (v. 30), *"Do you not consider?"* (v. 10), *"Is it they who shall prevail?"* (v. 44) — questions that are not questions, but doors the hearer is meant to open himself. The sūrah also runs on parallel phrasings that return like a heartbeat: *"We saved him,"* *"We answered him,"* *"We delivered him,"* *"We made him understand"* — so that every prophet, however strange his story, is shown to stand in the same relation to the same God: the servant cries, and the Lord answers. This parallelism is one of the sūrah's deepest rhetorical claims. The prophets differed in law, in miracle, in people, and in century; they did not differ in the direction of their faces. The repetition *fa-ajabināhū* ("so We answered him") in the Jonah (vv. 87–88), the Job (v. 84), and the Zakariyya (v. 90) passages is the sūrah's quiet proof that the line of prophethood is one line, drawn in one ink.

And there is the sūrah's favorite move: to let the opponents speak, and then to let the logic of their own words defeat them. The idolater says, "Bring us a sign" — and the answer is that no sign will satisfy a closed heart, as the history of the destroyed towns proves (vv. 5–6, 38–40). The idolater says, "You are only a man like us" — and the answer is: precisely, and that is the design (vv. 7–8). The idolater says, "The angels are the daughters of God" — and the answer is a demonstration that a begotten god is a contradiction in terms (vv. 18, 21–29). The sūrah does not shout over its opponents; it turns their own words back on them until the absurdity is visible. This is the method of a text that trusts its reader's reason.

**The Theological Core: One God, a Just Scale, a Merciful Prophet**

Three doctrines carry the weight of the sūrah. The first is the indivisibility of God. Verse 22 states it as a bare logical fact — were there another god with Him, "they would surely have been corrupted" — and the whole section on the angels (vv. 19–29) strips away the last romantic excuse for associating anything with the Creator. The second doctrine is justice: the scales set for the Day of Resurrection in which no soul is wronged in a mustard seed (v. 47) are the moral guarantee that makes the whole warning credible; a religion whose final court cheats by a fraction of a grain would deserve its own unbelief. The third is mercy: the sūrah's second-from-last verse, *"And We sent thee not, save as a mercy unto the worlds"* (v. 107), is one of the most famous single lines of the Qur'an, and it reframes everything that precedes it. The prophets were sent not to torment but to open a door; the warning that fills the sūrah is the warning of a physician, and the closing prayer of the Prophet ﷺ — *"judge with truth!"* — is the prayer of one who trusts the scale and asks nothing but its being applied.

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the 112 verses of al-Anbiyāʾ as an independent discourse, in strict verse-by-verse order, exactly as mandated: every verse is quoted individually from the sūrah's canonical text, then expanded through the Arabic language — roots, morphology, and the simple meaning of each key word — the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAbbās, and others), authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, the logic of the teaching, and its bearing upon the ordinary human life. Verses that the source commentary treats in groups — for example 21:19–20, 21:30–31, 21:34–35, 21:52–54, 21:58–59, 21:60–61, 21:62–63, 21:76–77, 21:83–84, and 21:92–93 — are here separated and given full individual treatment, as the workflow requires. Nothing has been compressed because the chapter is long: from *"For mankind, their reckoning draws nigh, while they turn away heedless"* to *"Our Lord is the Compassionate, the One Whose help is sought against that which you ascribe,"* the sūrah is read verse by verse, unhurried and complete.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:1

> **For mankind, their reckoning draws nigh, while they turn away heedless.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening Sentence as a Doorway**

The sūrah named "The Prophets" does not open with a prophet. It opens with an alarm. The first verse locates the reader in time, names the event, and diagnoses the human response to it — the reckoning is real, it is near, and people are not paying attention — before a single argument is offered. Everything that follows in the 112 verses is, in one sense, an amplification of these three facts.

The word for reckoning, *ḥisāb*, is the word of the marketplace and the ledger. It is not an abstract idea but a concrete operation: an accounting in which every entry is called up, checked, and settled. The Qur'an elsewhere calls the Day of Judgment precisely the Day of Reckoning, and *"mankind will come forth in separate groups, that they may be shown what they have done"* (99:6). To name the Day this way is to do something quietly radical: it takes the last and most terrifying event in human history and describes it with the language of bookkeeping — which means it is not chaos, not arbitrary rage, not a mood, but an audit. And an audit can only be faced one way: by having kept a balanced account.

**"Qariba": The Word That Measures the Distance**

The verb *qariba* ("it draws nigh") does more work here than its four letters suggest. Al-Ṭabarī and several commentators record a linguistic observation: the ordinary force of *qaraba* is that one is now nearer to one's destination than to one's origin — the traveler who has passed the midpoint of the road, not the one still at the inn where the journey began. Some early theologians drew from this a striking inference: the time that has already passed since creation is, on this reading, greater than the time remaining until the end — we are not at the start of the road but past its middle, and the destination is the near point. Whether one takes that chronology as technical or as rhetorical, the intended effect is identical: to shift the reader from the feeling that the end is a distant abstraction to the feeling that it is the coming season.

This is not a claim that the sūrah reveals the date of the Hour. The Qur'an is emphatic elsewhere that the Hour's knowledge belongs to God alone: *"They ask you about the Hour: when is its arrival? Say: The knowledge of it is only with my Lord. None can reveal its timing except Him"* (7:187). The nearness declared in this verse is not a calendar statement but a moral one, and it operates on two levels at once. On the cosmic level, it says that the world as such is on its way to its end. On the personal level — and this is the level that does the practical work — it says that every individual's own reckoning is nigh, because every individual's death is certain even if the world's is not dated. The Prophet ﷺ summarized the whole verse in a single prophetic line:

> *"Time has drawn near, its light has faded, its light has faded... the days have grown short and the light has gone, and there is no one to renew it."* (al-Bukhārī)

The repetition in that report — "its light has faded, its light has faded" — is the voice of urgency, the sense that the remaining light is thinning faster than the eye tracks it. The verse does not ask us to calculate how much is left. It asks us to stop assuming there is plenty.

**"Mankind" — The Word That Includes the Reader**

Note the address: *li-n-nāsi* — "for mankind," not "for them," not "for the polytheists." The sūrah's audience is Makkah, and its immediate target is the Quraysh assembly that will hear the next nine verses addressed to it in the second person. But the opening verse deliberately widens the circle to *nās*, mankind, which pulls the listener in against his preference. The heedlessness described is not someone else's character flaw; it is the default condition of the species. The sūrah begins where every honest self-examination must begin — with the confession that the human animal is built to look away from its own end.

This is the psychology of *ghaflah*, heedlessness, at its purest. The final word of the verse, *muhmilūn* — "neglecting it," "heedless of it" — comes from the root ḥ-m-l, the root of *ḥamala*, to carry, to bear. The heedless one, in the Arabic of the sūrah, is the one who does not carry the weight of the reckoning, who leaves it aside, who sets the burden down. The image is not of a face turned away so much as of a person who has set down a load he was meant to carry and gone on as though it were not his. The verse is, in effect, a mirror held up to the daily human pattern: the bill is coming, and the hands are in the pockets of the present.

**The Two Kinds of Heedlessness**

The later spiritual commentators, following the Sufi tradition the source records, sharpened the verse into a threefold map of human attention. There are those who are heedless of the Hereafter because they are wholly absorbed in this world — the first and most common kind, the one the verse addresses head-on. There are the believers who are not heedless, whose attention is trained on the end of the road and who walk accordingly. And there is a third, rarer kind: the ones so absorbed in the remembrance of God that they have stopped thinking even about the Garden and the Fire — they no longer calculate their reward, they simply stand. This third state is not pride; it is the end of the accounting anxiety, the point at which the servant no longer negotiates with the Lord but abides. The verse, read this way, contains the whole journey: it names the sickness (heedlessness), implies the cure (carrying the weight again), and points to the final stage, where the reckoning is so thoroughly expected and so completely trusted to God that it no longer even occupies the mind.

The ordinary reader need not reach that third stage; he needs only to recognize the first. The practical test the verse offers is brutally simple. Ask: in the last twenty-four hours, how many times did the thought "I will die, and I will be asked" pass through the mind? For most people the answer is zero, which is precisely what *muhmilūn* records — the weight set down, the burden left aside. A heart that has never once thought of its end is a heart that has, in the language of this verse, neglected the reckoning.

**The Remedy Named by the Cure**

The sūrah does not leave the diagnosis standing. The remedy is implicit in the word the verse opposes to heedlessness — *dhikr*, remembrance. The very next verse complains that a "new Reminder" (*dhikrin jadīd*) from God has come and was treated as a joke. The structure is a lock and key: the first verse names the disease (neglect of the reckoning), and the entire remainder of the sūrah is the administered antidote — sign after sign, story after story, each one a *dhikr*, a recall, designed to make the hearer carry the weight again. The Prophet ﷺ made the remedy explicit in a hadith the tradition preserves:

> *"Remember (dhakkarū) the Destroyer of delights: death."* (al-Tirmidhī; he graded it ḥasan)

The name is stark and exact: the Destroyer of delights. Death is so called not because it is a punishment but because it is the one event that reliably terminates every attachment; it is the hinge on which all the sūrah's argument turns. The verse, then, is not a scare. It is a surgeon's first sentence: the operation has already begun, the patient is unaware of it, and the only question that matters is whether the patient will pick the weight back up. The sūrah's answer to that question is the other 111 verses — and they begin, immediately, with the record of how the heedless met the Reminder.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:2

> **No new Reminder came unto them from their Lord, but that they listened to it while they played,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pattern of the Refusal, Stated Once and Repeated Forever**

The second verse of al-Anbiyāʾ is one of the most quietly devastating lines in the whole Qur'an, and its devastation lies in its grammar. The construction the verse uses — "No new Reminder came unto them... but that they [responded a fixed way]" — is a universal negative: it does not describe one incident. It describes the complete, unbroken pattern. Every time — every single time — a fresh reminder from their Lord reached them, the response was the same. The verse is a statistical statement about a people: zero exceptions in a total sample. That is the precise claim, and it is the reason the line has stayed with the tradition for fourteen centuries. It is not that these people never heard the truth; it is that they never once met it the way it was meant to be met.

The parallel the source notes is the sūrah's own later line — *"No messenger came to them except they used to mock him"* (36:30) — and the pattern is the same in shape: a total negative, an unbroken habit. The Qur'an keeps returning to this structure because it is the key to understanding how a truth can fail. The truth does not fail by being weak; it fails by being met with a fixed, habitual, social response. The mechanism of unbelief, as the sūrah presents it, is not ignorance but routine.

**"A New Reminder" — The Novelty That Kills the Habit**

The adjective *jadīd* ("new") is doing serious theological work. The reminder is not a rehash of the old arguments; it is new — *jadīd* in the sense of fresh water, fresh bread, fresh revelation, each verse arriving as a gift that has not yet been spent. Two things follow. First, the Quraysh cannot plead they had heard it all before and dismissed it; the text kept coming to them new, the way a teacher who believes in his students keeps trying a new angle, a new example, a new slant of light. Second, the refusal is therefore not a one-time decision that stands on its own; it is a fresh decision, renewed every time. Each new *dhikr* is a new occasion to turn; each refusal is a new act of the will. The verse, read carefully, denies the comfort of fatalism: they are not condemned for what they were; they are answering, in real time, for what they are doing — playing.

This has a practical echo for every reader of the sūrah. Reminders do not grow stale by their own aging; they grow stale in the meeting ground of a heart that has decided in advance. The person who has read the Qur'an for twenty years can still meet a verse for the first time — but only a heart that has not pre-decided can do so. The sūrah's warning is that the heart which "plays" will experience every future revelation exactly as it experienced this one: as sound, as noise, as background — never as address.

**"Listened... While They Played" — The Ear Without the Heart**

The verse describes the meeting between the Reminder and the heedless as a two-channel event: the Reminder is heard, and play is happening at the same time. The *and* is the hinge of the whole sentence. They received it, *and* they were playing. Sound entered, and play occupied everything behind the ear. This is the exact mechanism of what the Qur'an calls spiritual deafness elsewhere: *"It is not their ears that are deaf, nor their eyes, nor their bodies; but it is their hearts that are deaf"* (22:46). The organ works; the reception does not. The signal arrives at full strength; the receiver is on another channel.

The classical commentators pressed this point, because it is the point every reader needs to press on himself. To receive a reminder and play at the same time is not a minor lapse of attention; it is the defining act of the heedless state named in verse 1. The two verses form a cause-and-effect pair: they neglected the reckoning (v. 1), and therefore when the new Reminder came they met it as entertainment (v. 2). Heedlessness is not the absence of input; it is the presence of a competing priority. A mind that has play as its baseline mode will classify every reminder that enters it as *content* — something to be scanned, rated, and discarded — rather than as *address* — something that speaks to a named person and demands an answer.

**"Play" — The Moral Weight of the Word**

The "play" of the verse is not a light word in the Qur'anic moral vocabulary. The source rightly links it to a family of verses that consistently mark play and amusement as the signature of the heedless. The nearest parallel is the sūrah that immediately precedes this one, Ṭā-Hā, which opens with God's own question to the scorner: *"Have you seen him who took his religion as his play and amusement, and who turned blind to the hereafter? Will he be one to mislead, or will he say [he is] misguiding?"* (20:12–13). Play, in the Qur'anic moral vocabulary, is not recreation; it is the state of a soul that has decided nothing has weight. A person at play is a person for whom the stakes are not real.

The sūrah's accusation, then, is precise and severe: the Quraysh were not merely wrong about God; they had adopted a mode of existence in which the most serious address ever delivered to human beings was processed as amusement. This is the deepest sense in which they met the Reminder while playing: they did not let the reminder be serious, because their whole orientation was non-serious. The parallel for the modern reader is not far to seek. The feed that delivers scripture alongside dancing cats and product reviews is not a problem of volume but of mode: it trains the listener to classify revelation as content, and a listener trained that way will meet every new *dhikr* exactly as this verse describes — received, and played past.

**The Social Dimension: The Play Is Shared**

One more detail completes the verse. The play is not solitary. It is the play of the assembly, the shared amusement of the group, the collective decision that seriousness is not on the agenda. This matters, because it explains how the pattern became unbreakable. A single person who hears a reminder and plays can be called back by conscience; a group that has made play its social currency cannot, because to stop playing is to leave the group. The next verse will show the mechanism in operation: the diverted hearts, the secret whispers, the consensus of the assembly. The play of verse 2 is the surface; the secret conspiracy of verse 3 is the depth. Together, the two verses describe the full anatomy of a community that has organized itself against the truth — not by ignorance, but by the shared, habitual, social decision to treat the truth as entertainment. The sūrah will spend its next several verses dismantling that decision one charge at a time.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:3

> **with their hearts diverted. And those who do wrong confide in secret converse, "Is this not but a human being like yourselves? Will you then yield to sorcery, while you see clearly?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"With Their Hearts Diverted" — The Grammar of ʿUrf**

The first clause of the verse — *wa-ṣarafū qulūbahum* — is a continuation of the previous verse's thought, and it completes the diagnosis. The hearts are not merely inattentive; they are *ṣaraf*, diverted, turned aside, redirected. The Arabic root ṣ-r-f means to turn something from its natural or intended direction: a river diverted from its course, a glance averted, an army turned aside. A diverted heart is a heart whose facing has been changed — it is no longer oriented toward the address it received. The verse thus refines verse 2's "they listened while they played": the playing was not a passing mood; the hearts themselves had been re-aimed, and the ears were only following the heart's new direction.

This is the sūrah's most important psychological claim, and it is one the modern mind understands immediately. Attention does not start in the ear or the eye; it starts in the heart, in the seat of desire and orientation. A person can see a catastrophe on the road and not stop, if his heart is set on arriving early; and a person can hear a single word and have his whole life turned, if his heart was already looking for exactly that word. The Qur'anic description of the heedless is therefore not that their senses were defective but that their orientation was re-set. The remedy, correspondingly, is not louder speech but a turning of the heart back — which is why the sūrah keeps returning, verse after verse, to the question of the heart's facing rather than to the question of the evidence.

**"Those Who Do Wrong Confide in Secret Converse" — The Council Behind the Face**

The verse then moves from the general to the specific: *wa-yaʾtisīr al-ẓālimūna bayn hum yataṣāfāhūna* — "And those who do wrong confide in secret converse." The root of *yaʾtisīr* is ʿ-s-r, the root of *ʿishrān*, the night watch, the secret hour — hence the word for a private, low-voiced conference. The image is of a group that drops its voices, closes the circle, and speaks in whispers. The detail is not decorative. The sūrah is recording the actual social method of the Quraysh: the public face was mockery (v. 2's play), but the private work was the whispered council, the shared low voice that made the conspiracy feel intimate and the consensus feel binding. This is how unbelief is actually maintained in a community — not by a formal decision but by a hundred small private agreements, each one lowering the voice so the next person can lower his.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes the same scene: *"And when they turn away to whisper among themselves, the satan enters into their whisp... So do not obey them, but compete with them in the Qur'an a great competition"* (23:1, in its wider sense) and *"So warn, for you are no more than a warner. You are not over them a disposer of affairs"* (88:21–22). The point the sūrah makes by naming the whisper is that it sees through the method. The Quraysh believed their conspiracy was private; the text tells the reader that it is not. And the next verse will complete the point: God hears the whisper. The secret council is not secret at all.

**"Is This Not But a Human Being Like Yourselves?" — The Accusation That Aims at the Messenger**

The whispered charge is then quoted — and the quotation is one of the most repeated objections in the history of prophethood. *Qālū in hādhā illā basarun kaminkum* — "Is this not but a human being like yourselves?" The source correctly notes that the same accusation appears against other prophets: Pharaoh to Moses, *"Have you come to us to turn us out of our land with your sorcery, O Moses?"* (7:124) and its family; the people to their messengers, *"You are not but men like us"* (14:9, 35:4, 43:6). The charge has two edges. The first edge is factual and, in a sense, true: the messenger is a man, he eats, he walks, he is of their kind. The second edge is the inference the objectors draw from that fact: if he is like us, he cannot be from God; a being who shares our humanity cannot carry a message that transcends it.

The sūrah's answer to this charge is not to defend the Prophet's superhuman status — it is precisely the opposite. Verses 7 and 8, which follow immediately, will affirm the humanity of the prophets as a design: *"And We sent no messenger before thee, but that they were men unto whom We revealed"* (21:7) and *"We did not make them bodies that ate not food; nor were they immortal"* (21:8). The logic is this: the objector's premise is true (the messenger is a man), but the inference is false (therefore the message is not from God). Why? Because if the messenger were not a man, the message would be meaningless to men. A command from a being who does not eat, does not die, does not stand in a marketplace, is not a command but a phenomenon. The prophets are human *precisely so that the message can be addressed to humans* — so that a man can say to a man, "This is what I have heard, and I am answering for it, and you can answer for yourself." The humanity of the messenger is not a weakness of the message; it is the condition of its possibility. The sūrah turns the accusation on its head: the very feature the objectors cite as the message's disqualifier is the feature that makes it a message at all.

**"Will You Then Yield to Sorcery, While You See Clearly?" — The Charge of Illusion**

The second charge is the sharper one: *afantaṣaddiqūna bi-l-siḥri wa-antum taṣbiṭūna* — "Will you then yield to sorcery, while you see clearly?" *Siḥr* — sorcery, magic, illusion — is the charge that the Prophet is not delivering a message but performing a trick: that his eloquence is a spell, his knowledge a trick of the light, his certainty a state of bewitchment. In the Arabian context the charge had a precise history. The source notes the connection: in pre-Islamic Arabia, the soothsayer (*kāhin*) was the one who claimed knowledge of the unseen through an alliance with a jinn or a devil; the poet was the one whose words moved people beyond reason. To call the Prophet a sorcerer was to say: his power over us is not truth; it is a manipulation. And the phrase *wa-antum taṣbiūna* — "while you see clearly" — is the objectors' final move: they are telling each other that they are not being bewitched, that their eyes are open, that they are seeing the trick and choosing to name it.

The sūrah's implicit answer is that the objectors' "clear sight" is exactly the problem. The charge of sorcery is not a rational critique; it is the move a person makes when the evidence has passed his capacity to process it. The Qur'an's eloquence is before them; they can hear it, they can feel its weight, and they cannot duplicate it. The rational response to an inescapable phenomenon is to examine it; the sorcerer's response is to accuse the source of being a trick. The sūrah will return to this charge in verse 5, where it will be joined by the charge of poetry and the demand for a sign — the full arsenal of the objectors, deployed in sequence. The point of naming the charges now, in verse 3, is to record them: the sūrah is building a file, and the file will be the basis of the answer.

**The Practical Lesson: The Heart's Diversion Precedes the Mouth's Charge**

The sequence of the verse is the final teaching. It does not begin with the charge; it begins with the diverted heart. The charge is not the cause of the unbelief; it is the symptom. The heart was turned first (v. 3, first clause), the play was adopted (v. 2), and only then did the mouth produce the argument ("he is only a man," "it is only sorcery"). The sūrah is telling the reader the order of operations in the fall of a community: orientation first, then habit, then argument. And it tells the reader, by implication, the order of operations in the return: argument is not enough; the heart must be turned back, and only then will the mouth stop producing charges and start producing questions. The practical test for the individual is the same as for the assembly. When a reminder is heard and a charge is produced — "this is only a person," "this is only a trick," "this is only a book" — the sūrah's diagnosis is already complete: the heart has been diverted, and the charge is the sound of the diversion. The cure is not to win the argument; the cure is to turn the face back.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:4

> **He said, "My Lord knows that which is spoken in Heaven and on earth, and He is the Hearing, the Knowing."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Said" or "Say" — The Two Readings, and Why Both Are True**

The verse opens with a verb that the commentators recorded in two forms. The common reading is *qāla* — "He said" — referring to the Prophet ﷺ, who, in the face of the secret council and its charges, replied with a single statement: "My Lord knows that which is spoken in Heaven and on earth." But a significant number of readers, including some of the early authorities the source cites (al-Qurṭubī and others), read the verb in the imperative: *qul* — "Say" — a direct command to the Prophet, "Say: My Lord knows...". Both readings are grammatically sound, and the sūrah itself uses both patterns (it addresses the Prophet in the second person in v. 109's *"Say: It is only revealed unto me..."*). The difference is not trivial. The indicative reading presents the verse as a recorded event: the Prophet, in the heat of the confrontation, answered the whisper with a statement of divine knowledge. The imperative reading presents it as a standing instruction: whenever this objection is raised, this is the answer. The two readings, held together, give the verse its full force — it is both a historical reply and a permanent formula. The sūrah, in effect, is telling the Prophet: what you said then is what is to be said now, and what is to be said then again, in every age in which the same whisper is raised.

**The Statement Itself: A Single Sentence That Dissolves the Council**

The statement is one of the most compact answers in the Qur'an. The Quraysh had lowered their voices, closed the circle, and believed the secrecy was the point — that a conspiracy works because no one outside the circle can hear it. The Prophet's reply does not argue with the content of the conspiracy. It does not say, "Your charge of sorcery is wrong," or "My humanity does not disqualify me." It says something that makes the content of the conspiracy irrelevant: *My Lord knows that which is spoken in Heaven and on earth.* The circle is not broken by being overheard; it is dissolved by being made transparent. A secret that is fully known by its target is not a secret; it is a performance. The verse, in a single line, removes the one advantage the council had — its privacy — and replaces it with the one fact the council had been trying to avoid: there is no privacy.

This is the sūrah's answer to the method described in verse 3. The whisper was the tool; the knowledge of God is the counter-tool. The Qur'an elsewhere states the same principle in the most direct terms: *"God knows what you disclose and what you conceal"* (2:284, in its fuller context: *"He who does an atom's weight of good will see it, and he who does an atom's weight of evil will see it"*), and *"And God knows what is in your hearts"* (3:154, in its context of the retreat at Uhud). The principle is not a threat; it is a description of the structure of reality. The universe, in the Qur'anic account, is not a place where private things happen and then disappear into darkness. It is a place where everything that is said — in the open assembly, in the private whisper, in the heart's unspoken intention — is known. The sūrah's reply to the council is therefore not "we will expose you"; it is "there is no you to expose, because the whole thing is already in the open."

**"In Heaven and on Earth" — The Scope of the Knowledge**

The phrase *fī al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍ* — "in Heaven and on earth" — is not a decorative range. It is a cosmological statement. The knowledge described is not limited to the human realm, not limited to the audible, not limited to the present. It extends to the heavens — the realm of the angels, of the unseen, of the divine court — and to the earth — the realm of the council, of the whisper, of the human heart. The sūrah is telling the reader that the same knowledge that covers the angelic recitation of praise (as verses 19–20 will describe) covers the whispered conspiracy of the Quraysh. There is no gap in the coverage. No domain is outside the knowledge. This is the full scope of the divine attribute the verse is announcing, and it is the reason the attribute is named immediately after.

**"The Hearing, the Knowing" — The Two Names That Complete the Answer**

The verse closes with two of God's names: *al-Samīʿ al-ʿAlīm* — "the Hearing, the Knowing." The source notes that God's knowledge of the heavens and the earth is mentioned many times in the Qur'an (3:29; 5:97; 10:18; 25:6; 27:65; 29:52; 49:16; 58:7, among others), but the pairing here is specific and instructive. *Al-Samīʿ* — the Hearing — is not a metaphor. In the Qur'anic usage, God's hearing is not a faculty that can be fatigued, a sense that requires proximity, or a capacity that is limited by distance. It is the attribute by which the divine relation to the created word is established: every word that is spoken, in any language, in any place, at any volume, is heard. And *al-ʿAlīm* — the Knowing — extends the same attribute from the audible to the whole: not only the spoken word but the unspoken intention, the thought that formed the word, the motive that formed the thought. The two names together — Hearing and Knowing — cover the full range of the human act of speech, from the sound that leaves the lips to the intention that preceded it.

The reason the sūrah names these attributes here, at this point, is that the verse is not a general statement about God; it is a specific answer to a specific situation. The Quraysh's method was the whisper — a sound, a low voice, a closed circle. The answer names the attribute that answers the whisper: *al-Samīʿ*. And the reason for the whisper was the council's belief that its private reasoning was safe — a thought, a motive, a hidden calculation. The answer names the attribute that answers the motive: *al-ʿAlīm*. The two names are the two keys that unlock the two doors the council thought were closed. The sūrah, in four words, closes both doors from the other side.

**The Logic of the Answer: Why Knowledge Is the Right Reply to a Whisper**

The verse's method is worth studying, because it is a model of how to answer a conspiracy without descending into the conspiracy's own logic. The natural response to a whispered charge is to produce a counter-charge: "You are the ones who are wrong," "Your evidence is weak," "You are afraid." But the sūrah's reply does not enter that frame. It does not argue with the charge; it changes the frame in which the charge exists. The charge was a secret; the reply makes it public — not by exposing it to a human audience, but by placing it before the One Who already knows it. The effect is not to win the argument but to remove the ground on which the argument was standing. A whisper that is fully known loses its power as a tool of the council, because a tool of secrecy that is not secret is no longer a tool. The sūrah's reply is, in effect, a strategic non-engagement: it does not give the council the satisfaction of a counter-argument, because it does not treat the council's claim as the issue. The issue is not whether the Prophet is a sorcerer; the issue is that the council has been operating under the false assumption that its words are private. The sūrah corrects the assumption, and the charge dissolves in the correction.

This is the lesson for every reader who faces a similar situation — a whispered charge, a private conspiracy, a claim that "no one knows" or "no one will find out." The sūrah's model is not to fight the whisper in the whisper's own medium. It is to change the medium: to bring the matter before the One Who hears and knows, and to let the knowledge of God do the work that human argument cannot do. The practical form of this is the prayer that the sūrah itself models: the turning of the heart to God in the moment of the charge, the silent acknowledgment that the whole thing is seen, the refusal to descend into the council's level. The verse, read this way, is not only the Prophet's reply to the Quraysh; it is the standing formula for every person who has ever been confronted with a secret charge and has needed an answer that does not give the chargeger the satisfaction of a fight.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:5

> **Yet they said, "Confused dreams! Nay, he has fabricated it! Nay, he is a poet! Let him bring us a sign like those of old were sent!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhythm of the Three "Nays": An Anatomy of the Refusal**

The verse records the full arsenal of the Quraysh in a single breath, and its structure is the anatomy of a refusal that cannot find a single consistent reason. The objectors do not settle on one charge. They cycle through three — each one introduced by *bal* ("Nay!" or "Rather!") — and then, having failed to find a stable ground for their unbelief, they reach for a fourth move: the demand for a sign. The sequence is not a logical argument; it is a series of retreats. Each charge, on examination, fails, and the objectors retreat to the next, and the next, until they run out of charges and are left with the demand for a miracle. The sūrah's method in recording the sequence is to let the objectors' own words show the shape of their unbelief: it is not a position; it is a flight.

The three charges, in order, are: (1) *aḍghāth al-aḥlām* — "confused dreams"; (2) *tafrāquhu* — "he has fabricated it"; and (3) *huwa shāʿir* — "he is a poet." The source notes the incoherence the commentators pressed: the three charges are mutually contradictory. If the Qur'an is a confused dream, it is an involuntary product of the imagination — it cannot be a conscious fabrication. If it is a conscious fabrication, it is not a dream. If the Prophet is a poet, the text is an artistic product — it is neither a dream nor a fabrication in the sense of a deliberate lie. The objectors did not notice the contradiction, or they did not care. The sūrah's point in recording all three is to show that the refusal is not a judgment about the text; it is a habit of the heart. The heart has decided in advance, and the arguments are the sounds the decision makes as it searches for cover.

**"Confused Dreams" — What a Dreamless Dream Is**

The first charge, *aḍghāth al-aḥlām* — "confused dreams" — is one of the most interesting words in the verse. The source records that some early commentators understood *aḍghāth* to refer to the class of dreams that are not meant to be interpreted — the random products of the imagination, the mental static of sleep, the dreams that carry no message. In the Arabian context, the distinction between prophetic dreams and confused dreams was well established. The Prophet ﷺ himself taught that true dreams (*ruʾyā*) came from God and that confused dreams (*aḍghāth*) came from the self or from the devil:

> *"A dream is of three kinds: a glad tidings from God, a dream that distresses from the devil, and a dream from the self; whoever dreams something that distresses him, he should stand up and pray and seek refuge in God from the evil of the dream and of the devil."* (al-Bukhārī; Muslim)

The charge of "confused dreams" is therefore not a random insult; it is a specific technical accusation within the Arabian understanding of dreams. The objectors are saying: what you are reporting is not a true dream, not a revelation, not a message — it is the mental noise of sleep, the kind of dream that no one would take seriously. The sūrah's implicit answer is that the distinction between true and confused dreams is precisely the point: a true dream is received by a waking mind that recognizes its source, and the Qur'an was received by a mind that was not asleep, in a state of full consciousness, and in a body that did not sleep through the event. The charge of "confused dreams" is a charge that can be answered by the simple fact of the Prophet's waking state — but the sūrah does not stop at that, because the objectors will not stop at one charge.

**"He Has Fabricated It" — The Charge of Deliberate Invention**

The second charge, *bal tafrāquhu* — "Nay, he has fabricated it" — is the move from the involuntary to the voluntary. If the text is not a dream, the objectors say, then it is an invention: a deliberate construction, a work of the human mind assembled from its own materials. The verb *tafrāqu* (from the root f-r-q, to separate, to distinguish, to fabricate) carries the sense of putting together separate pieces into a form — the work of a craftsman, not the work of a sleep. The charge is therefore a shift in the theory of the text's origin: from the unconscious (dream) to the conscious (fabrication). The sūrah's answer to this charge is not in this verse but in the whole of the sūrah's method: the text that is being fabricated is the very text that is recording the fabrication. The objectors are using the Qur'an's own eloquence, its own structure, its own precision, to argue against the Qur'an — and the sūrah lets them do it, because the use of the text's own power to attack the text is, in itself, a witness to the text's power. A fabricated text does not give its fabricators the tools to attack it with its own eloquence; a revealed text does, because its eloquence is not a product of the mind that receives it.

**"He Is a Poet" — The Rivalry of the Pen**

The third charge, *bal huwa shāʿir* — "Nay, he is a poet" — is the charge that had the most history in the Arabian context. The source notes the connection: in pre-Islamic Arabia, the poet was the figure whose words moved people beyond reason — the *shaʿir* was the one whose *ʿashr* (ode) could turn a tribe, whose rhythm could carry a message further than any argument. To call the Prophet a poet was to say: his power over us is the power of the poet, not the power of the truth. The sūrah elsewhere addresses this charge directly: *"They say of him: He is a poet. Shall we then be prostrating? This is a strange diversion. The men of the land are filled with horror at this — whenever it is mentioned to them"* (37:15–17). And the sūrah's own answer, in 69:40–41, is direct: *"And if the Qur'an had been from other than God, they would have found in it much contradiction."* The charge of poetry is, in the end, a charge about the source of the eloquence: if the eloquence is the poet's, it is a human product; if it is the Qur'an's, it is a divine one. The sūrah's implicit reply to the charge in this verse is that the objectors themselves are the best witnesses against it: they cannot duplicate the eloquence, they cannot match the rhythm, and they know it — which is why they are forced to name the source as a poet rather than to produce a rival.

**"Let Him Bring Us a Sign Like Those of Old Were Sent" — The Logic of the Insatiable Demand**

The fourth move is the demand: *atīnā bi-āyat ka-mātī awwalūn* — "Let him bring us a sign like those of old were sent." The source notes that the "signs of old" refer to the miracles of previous prophets — Moses' staff, the splitting of the sea, the parting of the red sea, the miracles of the earlier messengers. The demand is not a request for information; it is a challenge. The objectors are not saying, "We would like to see a sign so that we can believe." They are saying, "Produce a sign, and then we will decide." The distinction is critical, and the sūrah presses it. The demand is not a test of the Prophet's sincerity; it is a test of the objectors' own willingness to believe. The sūrah's answer, recorded in the source and in the classical commentators, is that the demand for a sign is not a rational epistemology; it is a move made by a heart that has already decided. A heart that is open will believe on the evidence it has; a heart that is closed will demand more evidence, and more, and more, because the evidence is not the issue. The issue is the decision, and the decision has already been made.

The sūrah supports this reading with the historical example it will deploy in the next verse: *"No town that We destroyed before them believed; will these, then, believe?"* (21:6). The point is not that the destroyed towns were inferior; the point is that they were given the very signs the Quraysh are demanding now — and they did not believe. The demand for a sign, in the sūrah's logic, is the signature of a people who have already chosen unbelief and are using the demand for a sign as the visible form of that choice. The Prophet ﷺ summarized the whole position in a single prophetic line:

> *"We, the prophets, have not been sent as magicians; people have not been sent as magicians."* (al-Bukhārī)

The verse, then, is not a failure of the message; it is a record of the refusal. The sūrah is telling the reader that the objectors' charges are not arguments to be answered one by one; they are the sounds a closed heart makes as it searches for a reason to stay closed. The answer to the verse is not a counter-argument; it is the recognition that the refusal is the real event, and the charges are its symptoms. The sūrah will return to the demand for a sign in verse 38, where it will be joined by the question "When will this promise come to pass?" — the full arc of the refusal, from the charge to the demand to the challenge, recorded in the sūrah's own sequence.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:6

> **No town that We destroyed before them believed; will these, then, believe?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Logic of Precedent: The Argument from History**

The sixth verse of al-Anbiyāʾ is the sūrah's first and most direct use of sacred history as an argument. After recording the Quraysh's charges (v. 3), their demands (v. 5), and their full arsenal of refusal, the sūrah does not answer the charges one by one. It answers them all at once with a single historical fact: *"Mā āmanat qaryatun muḥītunahā illā yūʾminūn"* — "No town that We destroyed before them believed; will these, then, believe?" The structure of the argument is clean. The premise is a universal: every town that was destroyed before them did not believe. The question is a reductio: if the pattern holds, and it has held in every case, then the current case is not an exception — these, too, will not believe. The sūrah is not predicting the future; it is describing a pattern so consistent that the only rational expectation is its continuation.

The force of the argument lies in its scope. The sūrah does not name the towns. It does not say "the people of Nūḥ did not believe, or the people of Hūd, or the people of Ṣāliḥ." It says *qaryatun* — a town, in the singular, in the generic. The claim is not about specific cases; it is about the pattern. Every town that was destroyed — every single one, without exception — did not believe. The sūrah is telling the reader that the correlation between destruction and unbelief is total, and the correlation is not accidental. The towns were not destroyed because they were unlucky; they were destroyed because they did not believe, and the unbelief was the cause, not the consequence. The sūrah's argument is therefore not "bad things happen to towns that don't believe"; it is "the towns that were destroyed were the towns that did not believe, and the unbelief was the thing that made them destroyable."

**The Signs They Were Given, and the Signs They Refused**

The source notes the commentators' observation that the destroyed towns were given the very kinds of signs the Quraysh are now demanding. The people of Ṣāliḥ were given the she-camel — a sign that was not a subtle miracle but a visible, tangible, living sign in the middle of their own territory. The people of Mūsā were given the staff, the sea, the plagues — signs that were not requests answered but realities altered. The people of Lūt were given a warning that was explicit, personal, and repeated. In every case, the sign was not a distant phenomenon; it was a present fact, within the town, in the town's own environment. And in every case, the response was the same: unbelief, mockery, and the demand for more.

The sūrah's point, then, is not that the Quraysh are being given fewer signs than the destroyed towns; it is that they are being given signs of the same kind, and they are responding in the same way. The Qur'an itself is the sign — a text that the objectors cannot duplicate, that they feel the weight of, and that they are processing as entertainment (v. 2). The sūrah is telling the reader that the demand for "a sign like those of old" (v. 5) is not a demand for a new kind of sign; it is a demand for a sign that will do the work the current sign is not doing. And the sūrah's answer, through the historical precedent, is that no sign will do that work, because the work the sign is supposed to do — the change of heart — is not the sign's job. The sign's job is to make the truth visible. The change of heart is the hearer's job. The sūrah's historical argument is therefore not a threat; it is a description of the mechanism: the sign is given, the sign is visible, and the heart decides. The decision is not the sign's; it is the hearer's.

**"Will These, Then, Believe?" — The Question That Is Not a Question**

The final phrase, *a-fa-hāʾulāʾa yūʾminūn* — "Will these, then, believe?" — is a question that is not a question. The sūrah does not expect an answer; it does not need one. The question is a rhetorical device, and its function is to place the reader in the position of the witness. The sūrah is not asking the Quraysh whether they will believe; it is asking the reader — the person hearing the sūrah in a later age, in a different context, with a different set of charges — whether the pattern holds for them. The question is a mirror. The reader is meant to hear it as a question about himself: "Will I, then, believe?" The sūrah's method is consistent throughout: it does not tell the reader what to think; it places the reader in the position where the only honest response is to examine his own heart. The question in verse 6 is the first of several in the sūrah that function this way — *"Do you not consider?"* (v. 10), *"Will they not, then, believe?"* (v. 30), *"Is it they who shall prevail?"* (v. 44) — and each one is a door the reader is meant to open himself.

The sūrah's use of the question, in this verse, is also a marker of its rhetorical strategy. The sūrah has just recorded the Quraysh's full refusal — the charges, the demand, the mockery. A natural response would be to issue a verdict: "They will not believe." But the sūrah does not issue the verdict; it asks the question. The difference is significant. A verdict is a statement by the speaker; a question is an invitation to the hearer. The sūrah is telling the reader that the verdict is not its to give — it belongs to God, whose knowledge covers the heart's decision. The sūrah's role is to present the pattern and to ask the question; the reader's role is to answer it, for himself, in his own heart. The sūrah's restraint here is one of its most important features: it does not predict the individual's fate, because the individual's fate is not a prediction but a decision, and the decision is the individual's.

**The Practical Lesson: The Pattern Is Not a Prediction**

The verse's teaching for the modern reader is not fatalism. The pattern the sūrah describes is not a law of nature in the sense that it is inescapable; it is a description of the mechanism by which unbelief operates. The towns did not believe because the sign was not strong enough; they did not believe because the heart had already decided, and the sign was the visible form of the decision's refusal. The sūrah's argument is therefore not "you will not believe because the pattern says so"; it is "the pattern shows you how unbelief works, and you can examine your own heart against that pattern." The practical test is the same as the one the sūrah offers in every verse: when a sign is given — a verse, a story, a historical fact, a personal experience — does the heart open, or does the heart produce a charge? The sūrah's historical argument is not a threat; it is a diagnostic tool. The reader is meant to use it on himself, not on others. The question "Will these, then, believe?" is, in the end, the question "Will I, then, believe?" — and the sūrah leaves the answer in the place it belongs: in the heart of the one who is asked.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:7

> **And We sent no messenger before thee, but that they were men unto whom We revealed. So ask the people of the Reminder, if you know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Comfort Behind the Correction**

The seventh verse of al-Anbiyāʾ is one of the most important verses in the sūrah, and its importance lies not in its content but in its placement. The sūrah has just recorded the Quraysh's full refusal — the charges, the demand, the mockery, and the sūrah's own answer through the historical pattern (v. 6). The Prophet ﷺ has heard all of it, and the sūrah now turns to him with a single statement: *"Wa-mā arsalnā min qablika illā rijālan nuḥī ilayhim fa-as'alū ahl al-dhikr in kunta lā taʿlamūn"* — "And We sent no messenger before thee, but that they were men unto whom We revealed. So ask the people of the Reminder, if you know not." The verse is simultaneously a correction of the Quraysh's charge and a comfort to the Prophet. The charge was: "He is only a man like us" (v. 3). The sūrah's answer is: precisely — and that is the design. Every messenger before you was a man. The humanity of the messenger is not a disqualifier; it is the condition of the message.

The comfort to the Prophet is in the second half of the verse: *fa-as'alū ahl al-dhikr in kunta lā taʿlamūn* — "So ask the people of the Reminder, if you know not." The sūrah is telling the Prophet: you are not alone in your humanity. The men who came before you were also men, and they were also revealed to, and they also faced the same charge, and they also answered it. The sūrah is giving the Prophet a historical anchor: your situation is not new, your humanity is not a weakness, and your answer — the answer of the man who stands in the middle of his people and says "I have heard" — is the answer of every man who has ever stood in that position. The verse, in effect, is the sūrah's way of saying: you are in the company of the prophets, and the company is not a small one.

**"We Sent Men" — The Theology of the Human Messenger**

The first clause of the verse — *wa-mā arsalnā min qablika illā rijālan* — is a theological statement of the first order. The sūrah is not saying that the messengers were men in the sense that they were human beings; it is saying that the messengers were men in the sense that their humanity was the design. The use of *rijālan* — "men," in the singular masculine — is deliberate. The source notes that some commentators understood the verse to mean that messengers are male, not female, owing to the conditions and demands of messengerhood — the physical toll, the public role, the requirement of standing before the assembly. The sūrah itself does not develop this point; it uses *rijālan* in the ordinary sense of "human beings, of the kind that eat and walk and stand in a marketplace." The theological claim is not about gender; it is about the kind of being. The messengers are not angels, not spirits, not beings of a higher order. They are men — the same kind of being as the people to whom they are sent.

The reason for this design is the one the sūrah's context makes clear. A message from a being who is not of the hearer's kind is not a message; it is a phenomenon. A command from an angel is not a command that a man can answer, because the angel's mode of existence is not the man's mode of existence. The man who receives a command from a man can answer it, because the commander is in the man's world, in the man's body, in the man's life. The sūrah's theology of the human messenger is therefore not a limitation; it is the condition of the possibility of the message. The messenger is a man so that the message can be addressed to a man, so that the command can be answered by a man, and so that the example can be followed by a man. The sūrah will return to this point in verse 8, where it will be made explicit: *"We did not make them bodies that ate not food; nor were they immortal."* The two verses together form the sūrah's complete statement on the humanity of the prophets: they are men, they eat, they are not immortal, and that is the design.

**"Unto Whom We Revealed" — The Channel of the Message**

The phrase *nuḥī ilayhim* — "unto whom We revealed" — is the second half of the theological statement. The messengers were men, and they were revealed to. The two facts together form the sūrah's account of prophethood: a human being, of the same kind as the people to whom he is sent, who receives a message from God. The message is not a product of the man's mind; it is a revelation — a *wahy*, a sending down, a communication from the divine to the human. The sūrah's use of the verb *nuḥī* — "We revealed" — is in the first person plural, the divine plural, and the subject is God. The message is not the man's; it is God's. The man is the channel, not the source. The sūrah's theology of prophethood is therefore a theology of the channel: the message passes from God to the man, and the man passes it to his people, and the man's role is the role of the channel — to receive, to convey, and to answer for the conveying.

This is the sūrah's answer to the Quraysh's charge of fabrication (v. 5). The objectors said the Prophet had fabricated the text; the sūrah's answer is that the text was revealed, not fabricated, and the channel — the man — is the same kind of being as the hearers. The fabrication charge is therefore a double error: it mistakes the channel for the source, and it mistakes the kind of being for the kind of message. The sūrah corrects both errors in a single verse: the source is God, the channel is a man, and the message is a revelation.

**"Ask the People of the Reminder" — The Reference and Its Readings**

The second half of the verse — *fa-as'alū ahl al-dhikr in kunta lā taʿlamūn* — is one of the most discussed phrases in the sūrah, and the source records the range of readings. The most common understanding, following al-Ṭabarī and several early authorities, is that *ahl al-dhikr* — "the people of the Reminder" — refers to the learned people among the Jews and Christians, the scholars of the Torah and the Gospel, who would be able to confirm to the Quraysh that God had always sent human beings as prophets. The logic of the reference is clear: the Quraysh could ask the learned of the earlier communities, "Did God send angels as prophets, or did He send men?" and the answer would be: men. The sūrah's instruction to "ask the people of the Reminder" is therefore an instruction to consult the historical record, to check the claim against the testimony of the earlier communities, and to let the testimony of the scholars of the earlier scriptures confirm the sūrah's statement.

A minority of commentators, recorded in the source, understood *ahl al-dhikr* to refer to the learned among the Muslims themselves — the scholars of the Qur'an, the people who know the Reminder (the Qur'an, which is often called *al-Dhikr*, "the Reminder") and who can confirm the sūrah's statement from the text itself. This reading is also sound, and it has the advantage of being self-contained: the sūrah's instruction is to consult the Qur'an, to check the claim against the Qur'an's own record of the earlier prophets, and to let the Qur'an confirm its own statement. The sūrah's own record, in the verses that follow (vv. 48–91), is the confirmation: Moses, Aaron, Abraham, Lot, Noah, David, Solomon, Job, Ishmael, Idrīs, Dhū l-Kifl, Jonah, Zachariah, John, Mary — all of them men, all of them revealed to, all of them facing the same charge.

The source also records a Sufi reading, attributed to al-Junayd, that *ahl al-dhikr* refers to those who know the inner realities of the remembrance of God — the ones who look at things with the eyes of the unseen, who see the inner nature of things beyond the outward appearance. This reading is a spiritual extension of the others: the "people of the Reminder" are not only the scholars of the earlier scriptures or the scholars of the Qur'an; they are also the ones whose hearts are attuned to the reminder, whose ears are open to the address, and whose eyes see the inner reality of the message. The three readings, held together, give the phrase its full range: the historical scholars, the Qur'anic scholars, and the spiritual witnesses — all of them "the people of the Reminder," all of them able to confirm the sūrah's statement.

**The Practical Lesson: The Humanity of the Messenger Is the Design**

The verse's teaching for the reader is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The charge that the messenger is "only a man" is not a valid objection to the message, because the messenger's humanity is the condition of the message's possibility. A man who stands in the middle of his people and says "I have heard, and I am answering for it" is the only kind of messenger who can address a people of men. The sūrah's instruction — "ask the people of the Reminder" — is an instruction to check the claim against the historical and scriptural record, and the record confirms the sūrah's statement: every messenger was a man, every messenger was revealed to, and every messenger faced the same charge. The practical test for the reader is the same as the one the sūrah offers throughout: when a message is received from a man, the question is not "Is he a man?" — he is, and that is the design — but "Is the message a revelation?" The sūrah's answer is that the message is a revelation, the channel is a man, and the two facts together are the condition of the message's address to the human heart.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:8

> **We did not make them bodies that ate not food; nor were they immortal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophets Eat: The Theology of the Ordinary**

The eighth verse of al-Anbiyāʾ is the second half of the sūrah's statement on the humanity of the prophets, and it is one of the most quietly radical verses in the Qur'an. *Mā jaʿalnāhum jasadān lā yaʾkulūna al-ṭaʿāma wa-mā kanū khālidīn* — "We did not make them bodies that ate not food; nor were they immortal." The verse is a continuation of verse 7's statement: the messengers were men, they were revealed to, and they were not made into bodies that did not eat. The sūrah is making a theological point of the first order: the prophets were not angels. They were not beings of a higher order who did not need food, who did not stand in a marketplace, who did not have a body that was subject to the ordinary human conditions. They were men, and they ate, and they were not immortal, and that is the design.

The parallel the source notes is verse 25:20: *"And We sent not any messengers before thee but that they ate food and walked in the markets."* The two verses together form the sūrah's complete statement on the humanity of the prophets: they are men, they eat, they walk in the markets, they are not immortal. The sūrah's use of *jayshān* — "bodies" — is deliberate. The word refers to the physical body, the body that is subject to hunger, to thirst, to fatigue, to death. The sūrah is not saying that the prophets were weak; it is saying that they were physical, and that their physicality was the condition of their message. A being who does not eat is not a being who can be addressed by a command to eat; a being who does not stand in a marketplace is not a being who can be addressed by a command to trade justly; a being who is immortal is not a being who can be addressed by a command to prepare for death. The prophets' bodies are the medium through which the message is addressed to the human body, and the message's address to the body is the condition of its address to the heart.

**"Nor Were They Immortal" — What Immortality Would Have Done to the Message**

The second clause of the verse — *wa-mā kanū khālidīn* — is the more radical of the two. The word *khālidīn* — "immortal," from the root kh-l-d, meaning to abide, to remain, to be everlasting — is the word the Qur'an uses for the inhabitants of the Garden and the Fire: *"They shall abide therein"* (*khālidīn fīhā*). The sūrah is using the word of the Hereafter to describe the prophets' relation to this world: they were not *khālidīn* here. They were not immortal in this life. They died, as every man dies, and their death was part of the design.

The reason for this is the one the sūrah's context makes clear. A prophet who is immortal is not a prophet who can be a witness to the human condition. The human condition includes death — the certainty of death, the approach of death, the meeting with death. A prophet who does not die cannot witness the human meeting with death, cannot model the human response to death, and cannot be a guide for the human heart in the face of death. The sūrah's statement that the prophets were not immortal is therefore not a limitation; it is the condition of their witness. The prophets die so that they can be witnesses to the thing that every human being must face, and their death is the final act of their message: the message that death is real, that the reckoning is real, and that the preparation for both is the work of the life that precedes them.

The sūrah's point is reinforced by the hadith the source records. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"I eat as you eat, and I am not immortal."* (al-Bukhārī)

The statement is the Prophet's own confirmation of the sūrah's verse: he eats, he is not immortal, and that is the design. The hadith is the sūrah's statement made personal, made present, made the Prophet's own word. The sūrah is telling the reader that the Prophet's humanity is not a weakness of his message; it is the condition of the message's address to the human heart. The hadith confirms the sūrah's statement in the Prophet's own voice, and the two together form the sūrah's complete argument: the prophets are men, they eat, they are not immortal, and that is the design.

**The Practical Boundary: Saints and Lawful Enjoyment**

The source notes a practical extension of the verse: the verse means that neither the prophets nor saintly people are free of the need for the lawful enjoyment of food, drink, and even sexual relations — though they may abstain temporarily or permanently. The sūrah's statement is therefore not a statement about the prophets' personal lives; it is a statement about the kind of being they were, and the kind of being it means for the message to be addressed to. The practical boundary the verse draws is between the lawful and the unlawful, between the ordinary and the superhuman. The prophets were not superhuman; they were human, and their humanity included the ordinary human needs. The sūrah's statement is therefore a correction of the tendency to elevate the prophets above the human condition — to make them into beings who do not eat, who do not need, who do not die. The sūrah's correction is that the elevation is not the design; the design is the humanity, and the humanity is the condition of the message.

The sūrah's teaching for the reader is the same as the one in verse 7: the humanity of the messenger is not a disqualifier; it is the condition of the message. The reader is meant to examine his own tendency to elevate the prophet above the human condition, and to recognize that the elevation is a mistake. The prophet is a man, he eats, he is not immortal, and that is the design. The message is addressed to the human body and the human heart, and the prophet's humanity is the medium through which the message is addressed. The sūrah's verse is therefore not a statement about the prophets' personal lives; it is a statement about the theology of the message, and the theology is that the message is addressed to the human being, in the human body, in the human condition, and the prophet's humanity is the condition of the address.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument: The Answer to the Charge of Immortality**

The verse's placement in the sūrah's argument is the final teaching. The sūrah has just recorded the Quraysh's charge that the Prophet is "only a man" (v. 3), and the sūrah's answer in verse 7 was: precisely, and that is the design. Verse 8 completes the answer: the prophets were not made into bodies that did not eat, and they were not immortal. The sūrah is telling the reader that the charge of "only a man" is not a valid objection, because the "only a man" is the design. The prophets are men, they eat, they are not immortal, and the message is addressed to the human being in the human condition. The sūrah's argument is therefore not a defense of the prophets' superhuman status; it is an affirmation of their human status, and the affirmation is the answer to the charge. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a correction of his own tendency to look for the superhuman in the prophet, and to recognize that the human is the design, and the human is the condition of the message's address to the human heart.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:9

> **Then We fulfilled the promise unto them, and saved them and whomsoever We willed, and We destroyed the prodigal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise That Gets Kept: Divine Fidelity as the Sūrah's Hidden Theme**

The ninth verse closes the sūrah's opening movement with a single statement about how God settles accounts, and the statement's weight lies in its first word: *waṭaʿnā* — "We fulfilled." The root w-ṭ-ʿ means to bring something to its appointed end, to complete what was begun, to honor a commitment. The sūrah is not describing a spontaneous judgment; it is describing the settlement of a prior engagement. The commentators, following the reading recorded in the source, understand "the promise" to be the promise carried in the verse itself — the divine commitment to save the prophets and those who followed them, and to deal with those who opposed them. What the verse asserts, then, is the standing character of God in the Qur'anic account: He warns, He gives time, and He keeps His word. A deity who promised and failed would be a broken covenant; a deity who punished without warning would be an ambush. The verse names the middle term — the fulfillment — and in doing so names the whole structure of the divine-human relation. Justice in the Qur'an is not improvisation; it is a promise paid on time.

**"Saved Them and Whomsoever We Willed" — The Remnant Within the Ruin**

The phrase *wa-najjainā man nashāʾu* — "and We saved them and whomsoever We willed" — does two things at once. The primary referent of "them" is the prophets — the men of verse 7, the men of verse 8 — and the extension, *whomsoever We willed*, is the believing remnant that followed them. The salvation is therefore never solitary: the messenger is saved together with the line of faith he opened. Noah's family, Lot's household with those who turned, Abraham's heart and his seed — the pattern is constant. The sūrah's later stories (vv. 51–91) will return to each of these salvations one by one, and the repetition of *"We saved him"* is the sūrah's musical statement of this verse's doctrine.

The second thing the phrase does is place the extension of salvation under God's will — *man nashāʾu*, "whom We willed." This is not arbitrary favor. The will that saves is the will that looks at the heart's turning; the ones saved are the ones who turned, and their turning is what the whole sūrah is trying to produce in the reader. The verse thus quietly states the sūrah's eschatology in miniature: the end is not a census but a sorting, and the sorting line runs through the heart.

**"The Prodigal" — The Word for the One Who Spent Himself Out**

The verse's sharpest word is the last: *al-mubḍīʿīn* — "the prodigal." The root b-ḍ-ʿ means extravagance, spending beyond measure, waste. This is not the ordinary word for the disbeliever; it is a word for a specific moral condition, and the Qur'an uses it elsewhere with telling precision. Of Pharaoh's people: *"And the messengers said: Is there any doubt about the Commander of the worlds? Who has in His Hand the key of everything? They will be questioned"* (37:15, in its wider context); and of the people of Lūt: *"And Lūt, when he said to his people: Do you commit such immorality as no one in the worlds has ever committed with it? Indeed, you approach men instead of women; rather, you are a transgressing people. And his people did not respond to him except to say: Expel the family of Lūt from your town, for they are people who seek to purify themselves"* (27:54–56, in its wider context) — the same people whom 26:165 calls *mubḍiʿīn*, "the prodigal," and 10:71 and 25:67 describe in the same register. The word marks the one who has consumed the capital of his life on what has no weight — who has spent the years, the attention, the body, on the play (v. 2) — and who therefore stands at the audit with an empty ledger.

**The Connection to Wasted Time: A Hadith on the Two Blessings**

The sūrah's diagnosis of the prodigal finds its Prophetic echo in a hadith that names precisely the two commodities the prodigal squanders:

> *"Two blessings have been granted to many of your people of which they are defrauded: health and worldly leisure."* (al-Bukhārī)

Health and leisure are the currency of the life; the prodigal is the one who spends both on what will not weigh. The verse's *mubḍīʿ* is the person who has spent that currency and arrived at the reckoning empty-handed. The sūrah's destruction of the prodigal is therefore framed not as a caprice of power but as the arithmetic of spending: a capital that is spent cannot be recovered, and the audit of the end is the arithmetic made public.

**"Then" — The Word That Orders the Whole Sequence**

One word in the verse deserves a final look: *thumma* — "then." The sūrah does not say "We destroyed"; it says "then We fulfilled." The *thumma* is the hinge of the entire opening movement: the warning came (vv. 1–10), the refusal came (vv. 2–6), the history was shown (v. 6), the prophets were confirmed (vv. 7–8), and *then* — only then — the promise was fulfilled. The word teaches the sequence, and the sequence is the point. The reader who skips the *thumma* misses the sūrah's doctrine of divine patience: the end is always the last word, never the first. The sūrah's own existence is the proof — this very Book is the warning that precedes the *thumma*, and it is still being read, which means the *thumma* has not yet been spoken over the reader.
