The emphasis falls on *each* — *kullun*. The regularity is not general but particular: every one of these bodies has its own orbit, and the orbits do not interfere. This is the same point the sūrah made at verse 22 from a different direction, where it argued that a plurality of gods would produce corruption in the heavens and the earth. The observed order of the celestial bodies is the evidence for that argument.

The verse therefore completes the sequence begun at verse 30. Having surveyed the earth, the sky, and now the moving bodies, the sūrah has presented the whole visible world as a single ordered system. The next verse turns from the world to the Prophet ﷺ and to death, and the turn is sharper for the order that precedes it.

**The Turn to Mortality**

Verse 33 describes a cosmos of immense duration — the night and the day alternating, the sun and the moon gliding in orbits without interruption. Verse 34 begins: *We have not ordained perpetual life for any human being before thee.* The juxtaposition is the sūrah's method. The permanence of the celestial order is set against the brevity of the human life that observes it.

The contrast is not designed to diminish the reader. The celestial bodies are created and sustained; the human being is created and returns. Both are creatures, and the difference in duration does not make one divine and the other merely mortal. What the contrast establishes is that permanence belongs to the One who ordained both, and to nothing within the creation.

This is the same argument the sūrah has been making since verse 21, approached from a new direction. The gods taken from the earth cannot resurrect; the angels are servants under sanction; the sky is a preserved canopy; the celestial bodies glide in assigned orbits. In each case the point is that nothing within the world has the permanence that divinity requires, and verse 34 applies the conclusion to the reader.

---

**Orbits and the Argument of Verse 22**

The verse's statement that each body glides in its own orbit completes an argument the sūrah began at verse 22. There the claim was that if there were gods other than God in the heavens and the earth, both would have been corrupted. The evidence offered was the observed coherence of the world. Verse 33 supplies the specific case: the celestial bodies move in assigned paths without interference.

The commentators' cosmological discussion does not disturb this argument, which is why they can record both the standard view of ethereal spheres and Ḍaḥḥāk's simpler reading of orbits as mere trajectories. The argument requires ordered motion, not a particular account of its mechanism. Whether the bodies swim in spheres or simply travel, each keeps its own course.

Al-Qurṭubī's and al-Rāzī's concluding observation — that no one can have knowledge of these matters except through revelation — protects the argument from being read as a cosmological claim. The verse asserts the order and leaves the mechanism to revelation, which means it cannot be falsified by a later cosmology and was never intended to compete with one.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:34

> **We have not ordained perpetual life for any human being before thee. So if thou diest, will they abide forever?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Perpetual Life Denied**

*We have not ordained perpetual life for any human being before thee.* The word rendered *perpetual life* is *khuld*, which the commentary notes is used in the frequent refrain *they shall abide therein* of the state of those in the Hereafter, and elsewhere to connote perpetuity or everlastingness. Al-Ṭabarī is explicit that in this verse the word refers specifically to this world, where every human being will die.

The distinction is essential. The sūrah is not denying that there is perpetuity; it is denying that perpetuity is available in this life. The same word that describes the abiding of the people of the Hereafter is used here of what no human being has been given on earth, and the contrast between the two uses is the verse's point.

The phrase *before thee* is also significant. The Prophet ﷺ is not exempted from the general rule but included in it, and the rule is stated as a fact about all human beings rather than as a warning to him. The verse's addressee is the audience as much as the Prophet, and the audience is being told that the man they oppose is subject to the same condition they are.

**"Will They Abide Forever?"**

*So if thou diest, will they abide forever?* Al-Ṭabarī explains that the question is asked of the Prophet and signifies that if he should die, it will not change the fundamental situation of the idolaters who oppose him — they too will die. The verse contains the verb form of the noun rendered *perpetual life*, so the question turns on the same word the first clause denies.

The logic is exact and worth spelling out. The opponents appear to suppose that the death of the Prophet ﷺ would end his message and vindicate their opposition. The verse observes that his death would leave them exactly where they are: mortal, accountable, and awaiting the same end. Nothing would be settled by it, and the question exposes the supposition as incoherent.

Al-Rāzī records that some mention the verse was revealed in relation to those who believed the Prophet would never die, and the commentary notes an alternative occasion as well. The ambiguity about the occasion does not affect the argument, which holds whether the opponents expected his death or his immortality — in either case the verse observes that their own mortality is unchanged.

**Tasting Death**

Verse 35 will state the general rule: *Every soul shall taste death.* The commentators note that for some, *to taste death* — with cross-references to 3:185 and 29:57 — refers not to death itself but to the sufferings that precede it. The reading narrows the phrase from an event to an experience, and the narrowing is theologically useful.

If what is tasted is the suffering rather than the cessation, then the verse is describing something that begins before the end. This is the ground of the reading the commentators record from the Sufi Junayd: *Whosoever is between the two vanishings is now vanishing*, implying that between the nonexistence preceding earthly life and the death ending it, one is in a constant state of dying.

On that reading verse 34 is not only a statement about the future but a description of the present. The Prophet ﷺ and his opponents are alike in being mortal, and the mortality is not a future event awaiting them but a condition they inhabit. The verse's force is therefore not merely that everyone dies but that everyone is dying, and the difference between the two claims is considerable.

**The Verse's Function in the Argument**

Verse 34 comes immediately after the survey of the cosmos at verses 30 to 33, and the placement is deliberate. The sūrah has just described the night, the day, the sun, and the moon — phenomena of immense and apparently permanent regularity — and it now turns to the brevity of the life that observes them. The contrast gives the cosmic survey its purpose.

The verse also answers an objection that the preceding arguments do not address. An audience might grant that the gods taken from the earth cannot resurrect, that the angels are servants, and that the sky is preserved, and still suppose that none of this concerns them personally. Verse 34 removes that distance by applying the conclusion to the reader's own duration.

This is the sūrah's characteristic movement: establish a general truth, then close the gap between it and the hearer. Verse 21 asked whether the idols resurrect; verse 23 said that they shall be questioned; verse 30 asked whether they would not then believe; verse 34 tells them that they will not abide. Each step reduces the distance between the proposition and the person considering it.

---

**The Verse and Its Occasions**

Al-Rāzī records two occasions for the verse. Some mention that it was revealed in relation to those who believed the Prophet would never die; others give an alternative occasion connected with the opponents' expectation that his death would end his mission. The commentators preserve both without adjudicating, and the argument holds under either.

Under the first, the verse corrects a belief held by some of the Prophet's own followers — that he was exempt from the general rule. Under the second, it answers an expectation held by his opponents — that his death would settle the question. In both cases the verse makes the same observation: no human being before him was given perpetual life in this world, and his death would change nothing about the mortality of anyone else.

The ambiguity of the occasion is therefore not a weakness. The verse states a rule and applies it, and the rule applies regardless of which error prompted the revelation. This is characteristic of the sūrah's method throughout: the general statement carries the argument, and the particular occasion explains why the statement was made at this point rather than another.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:35

> **Every soul shall taste death. We try you with evil and with good, as a test, and unto Us shall you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Soul Shall Taste Death**

*Every soul shall taste death.* The verse states the rule that verse 34 applied to the Prophet ﷺ and his opponents, and it states it universally. Al-Ṭabarī's note that *khuld* in the preceding verse refers specifically to this world, where every human being will die, is the premise from which this clause follows. The two verses are a single argument in two steps.

The verb *taste* is chosen rather than a plainer term, and the commentators attend to it. For some it refers not to death itself but to the sufferings that precede it, with cross-references to 3:185 and 29:57. The reading makes the verse descriptive of experience rather than predictive of an event, and it is what allows Junayd's gloss to be drawn from the same words.

Junayd's statement — *Whosoever is between the two vanishings is now vanishing* — treats the interval between the nonexistence before life and the death at its end as itself a state of dying. The commentators record this as a spiritual reading rather than a rival translation, and it depends on the verse's choice of *taste*, which implies something undergone rather than merely arrived at.

**Tried With Evil and With Good**

*We try you with evil and with good, as a test.* The clause is the verse's most consequential statement, and its symmetry is exact. The test is not only adversity; prosperity tests as well. The commentators do not elaborate the point here, but the symmetry is the verse's method — it forecloses the assumption that difficulty is the only form of trial, which is the assumption most people operate with.

The pairing also answers an objection that arises from verse 34. If human life is brief and ends in death, the distribution of good and evil within it might appear arbitrary. The verse presents both as instruments of a single test, which does not explain the distribution but reframes it: neither condition is a verdict, and both are occasions.

The word *fitnah* — test, trial — is used elsewhere in the Qur'an of both persecution and temptation, and the verse's use of it for both evil and good is consistent with that range. What is being tested is not endurance under suffering alone but the use made of either condition, and the verse leaves unspecified what a successful response would look like.

**"Unto Us Shall You Be Returned"**

The verse closes: *and unto Us shall you be returned.* The clause completes the movement begun in the first half. Death is not the end of the sequence; it is a transition to an account. The return is to God, and the preposition — *unto Us* — is emphatic, indicating that the return is to the One who conducted the test rather than to an impersonal end.

This is the same structure the sūrah established at verse 23, where it was said that God shall not be questioned about what He does but they shall be questioned. The two verses are separated by twelve others but make a single point: the test has an examiner, and the examination follows. The brevity of life stated at verse 34 is therefore not a diminishment but a limitation of the period in which the test is taken.

The verse's three clauses form a complete sequence: everyone dies, everyone is tested in the interval, everyone returns. Nothing is left outside the sequence, and the universality of each clause is stated rather than argued. The sūrah's method here is to lay out the structure of the human situation without comment and let the reader locate himself within it.

**Why the Verse Is Here**

Verse 35 follows the cosmic survey of verses 30 to 33 and the mortality of verse 34, and its placement gives those verses their point. A cosmos of immense regularity, observed by creatures of brief duration, would be a merely melancholy picture if the sequence ended there. The verse supplies what makes it an account rather than a spectacle.

It also prepares the verses that follow. From verse 36 the sūrah returns to the Prophet's opponents and their mockery, and the return is informed by what has just been said. Those who mock are themselves within the sequence — mortal, tested, returning — and their mockery does not place them outside it.

The verse is thus a hinge. It closes the passage on the physical world and mortality and opens the passage on the opponents' response. The sūrah does not announce the transition; it states the universal rule and then resumes the particular case, leaving the reader to see that the particular case falls under the rule.

---

**The Test and the Return**

The verse's three clauses are usually treated separately, and they are stronger together. Everyone dies; everyone is tested with evil and good in the interval; everyone returns. The middle clause is what connects the first to the third, and without it the sequence would be a bare statement of mortality followed by an unrelated assertion about return.

The symmetry of *with evil and with good* is the clause's most important feature. It forecloses the common assumption that hardship is the test and ease is the reward. On the verse's account both are instruments, and neither is a verdict. A person in prosperity is not thereby passing, and a person in adversity is not thereby failing; the test is the use made of whichever condition has been given.

The closing clause — *unto Us shall you be returned* — supplies the examiner. This connects the verse to verse 23, where the sūrah established that God shall not be questioned about what He does but they shall be questioned. The two verses make a single point across a gap of twelve others: the test is conducted by the One to whom the return is made, and the examination is not optional.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:36

> **And whenever those who disbelieve see thee, they take thee in naught but mockery, "Is this the one who makes mention of your gods?" And it is they who are disbelievers in the remembrance of the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mockery and Its Object**

The verse opens by describing the encounter: when the disbelievers see the Prophet ﷺ, mockery is the only register they bring to it, and the taunt they frame concerns his speaking about their gods. Al-Ṭabarī explains that *the one who makes mention of your gods* refers to the Prophet when he disparaged and condemned the idolaters, which they took as an insult. The mockery is therefore not general contempt but a response to something specific he had said.

The quoted question is worth reading carefully. It is phrased as though mention of the gods were itself the offence, and the commentators note that *mention* can imply either praise or blame depending on context. The opponents' formulation suppresses the distinction, treating any reference to their gods as an indignity rather than engaging with what was actually said about them.

This is a characteristic move in polemic and the verse identifies it precisely. By reducing a criticism to a mention, the opponents avoid answering it. The sūrah records the formulation rather than rebutting it, because the formulation is itself the evidence of what the verse is describing — a refusal to engage that presents itself as offence.

**The Verb and the Irony**

*Makes mention* renders *yadhkuru*, which the commentary notes is the verb form of *remembrance* (*dhikr*). The implication is that the idolaters censure the Prophet for remembering their idols while they themselves refuse to remember and worship God. The irony is structural rather than rhetorical: the word they use as an accusation names the very thing they neglect.

The connection to *dhikr* also links the verse to verse 24, where the Prophet ﷺ was told to say *This is the Reminder for those who are with me, and the Reminder for those before me*. The same root carries both uses, and the sūrah's argument throughout has been that remembrance of God is what the messengers bring and what the opponents decline.

The verse's closing clause makes the point explicit: *And it is they who are disbelievers in the remembrance of the Compassionate.* The accusation is reversed. Those who censure the Prophet for mentioning their gods are themselves in a state of disbelief regarding the remembrance that matters, and the reversal is achieved by the same word both parties are using.

**Remembrance of the Compassionate**

Al-Qurṭubī records that for some, *remembrance of the Compassionate* refers to the Qur'an — one of whose names is *al-Dhikr*, the Reminder — rather than to remembering God or being reminded of God. Both readings are preserved, and both make the same point about the opponents' position: they are in a state of disbelief with respect to the thing that has been given to them.

The use of *al-Raḥmān* here continues a pattern in this sūrah. The name appeared at verse 26 in the report of the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child, and it appears again at verse 42 in the question *Who will protect you, night and day, from the Compassionate?* The recurrence is deliberate, and each occurrence attaches the name to a specific refusal.

The theological point of using this particular name is recorded by al-Rāzī at verse 42: it causes one to reflect upon the fact that God encompasses all things with His Mercy. The opponents' mockery is therefore directed at the One whose name denotes mercy, and the verse's choice of that name sharpens rather than softens the description of their position.

**The Verse as Comfort**

The verse records the mockery without rebutting it, and the recording is itself the point. The sūrah has already established at verse 41 — which the commentators cross-reference to 6:10 — that messengers were mocked before the Prophet ﷺ and that those who scoffed were beset by what they mocked. Verse 36 states the present case; verse 41 states the pattern.

The function is pastoral as well as polemical. A man being publicly ridiculed is told that the ridicule is not evidence against his message but a recurring feature of the office, and that the recurrence has a consistent outcome. The sūrah does not promise that the mockery will stop; it states what has happened to those who mocked before.

This is the same form of comfort the sūrah offered at verse 25, where the Prophet ﷺ was told that every messenger before him was given the same message. There the comfort was that he was not innovating; here it is that he is not alone in being mocked. Both are forms of the same reassurance — that his situation is the standard shape of prophetic history rather than a personal anomaly.

---

**The Root Dh-K-R Across the Sūrah**

The verse's use of *yadhkuru* — makes mention — as the verb form of *dhikr* connects it to several earlier passages in this sūrah. At verse 24 the Prophet ﷺ was told to say *This is the Reminder for those who are with me, and the Reminder for those before me*. At verse 42 the sūrah will say *they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord*. The same root carries all three uses.

The pattern is argumentative rather than incidental. The sūrah establishes at verse 24 that remembrance is what the messengers bring; at verse 36 it shows the opponents accusing the Prophet of the very thing they neglect; at verse 42 it names the neglect directly. The root ties the three together, and the irony at verse 36 depends on the reader having the earlier use in mind.

Al-Qurṭubī's note that *remembrance of the Compassionate* may refer to the Qur'an, one of whose names is *al-Dhikr*, reinforces the connection. If the remembrance the opponents disbelieve in is the revelation itself, then their accusation against the Prophet is a rejection of the thing he was given, and the reversal the verse effects is complete rather than merely verbal.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:37

> **Man was created of haste. Soon shall I show you My signs; so seek not to hasten Me!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Man Was Created of Haste**

*Man was created of haste.* The commentators record two readings. On the first, people were created possessing the trait of being hasty, which al-Qurṭubī compares to 30:54, *God is He Who created you from weakness*, meaning created weak, and to 4:28, *Man was created weak*. On the second, the verse alludes to the rapidity of creation itself, as at 2:117: *He only says to it, "Be!" and it is.*

The first reading is the one the following verses require. Verse 38 records the opponents asking *When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?*, and al-Rāzī's note on that verse explains that it is not the mere fact of being hasty that is condemned, since haste is part of human nature, but challenging God to be so, in which case people display an excess of haste.

The verse therefore states a feature of human nature in order to distinguish it from a misuse of that feature. Haste is not the fault; the demand that God conform to one's timetable is. The distinction is precise, and the verse's brevity is possible because the following verse supplies the case that illustrates it.

**The Second Reading and Its Use**

The alternative reading — that the verse concerns the rapidity of creation — is not simply discarded by the commentators, and it has a use. If man was created of haste in the sense that his coming into being was immediate, then haste is not merely a trait he acquired but a feature of his origin. The cross-reference to 2:117 supports this: the Divine command is *Be!* and the thing is.

The two readings are compatible rather than competing. A creature brought into being instantaneously may be constituted for immediacy, and the trait described in the first reading would then be a consequence of the manner of creation described in the second. The commentators record both without choosing because both serve the verse's purpose.

What matters for the argument is that haste is presented as created rather than chosen. This is what allows verse 38's condemnation to be precise: the fault is not having the trait but directing it at God. A person cannot be blamed for a constitution he did not select, but he can be blamed for the use he makes of it, and that is the distinction the two verses draw together.

**"Seek Not to Hasten Me"**

The command in the verse's second half follows directly from the statement of human haste, and the sequence is the verse's method. Having said that man was created of haste, the verse addresses the haste directly and forbids one specific form of it — the demand that God act on a schedule set by the one making the demand.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the question at verse 38 follows immediately upon God's command not to seek to hasten the promise of punishment for the disbelievers in the Hereafter. The juxtaposition is the point: the command is given, and the very next verse records the demand that violates it. The sūrah does not comment on the violation; it simply places the two side by side.

The promise that the signs will soon be shown is a commitment rather than a schedule. It affirms that the signs will be shown without specifying when, which is precisely what the opponents' demand at verse 38 seeks to extract. The verse gives what can be given — the certainty of the signs — and withholds what is being demanded, which is the date.

**The Verse and the Cross-References**

The commentators cite 17:11 in connection with this verse: *Man prays for evil as he prays for good, and man is ever hasty.* The cross-reference extends the point beyond the demand for punishment. Haste is not only impatience for God's justice; it is a general disorder of desire in which a person asks for what will harm him with the same urgency he brings to what will benefit him.

This broadens the verse's application considerably. The opponents at verse 38 are a specific case of a general condition, and the condition is not confined to them. The sūrah's statement that man was created of haste is addressed to the reader as much as to the opponents, and the command *seek not to hasten Me* applies to anyone who finds himself demanding that God conform to his expectations.

The verse's placement after the survey of the cosmos and the statement of mortality is therefore purposeful. Verses 30 to 35 established that the world is ordered, that human life is brief, that it is a test, and that everyone returns. Verse 37 identifies the disposition that makes those facts hard to accept, and verse 38 shows that disposition in action.

---

**Haste as Created and Haste as Chosen**

The verse's two readings — that man possesses the trait of haste, and that his creation was rapid — are not competing accounts but two aspects of one claim. What both establish is that haste belongs to the constitution rather than to the choice. A person is not responsible for his constitution, and the verse does not condemn the constitution.

Al-Rāzī's note on verse 38 makes the distinction explicit: the trait itself is not what draws condemnation, since the previous verse attests that it belongs to human nature; what does is turning it against God, and thereby displaying haste in excess. The fault is the direction of the haste, not its presence.

The cross-reference to 17:11 — *Man prays for evil as he prays for good, and man is ever hasty* — extends the diagnosis beyond the opponents. Haste is not only impatience for God's justice; it is a general disorder of desire in which a person seeks what will harm him with the same urgency he brings to what will benefit him. The command *seek not to hasten Me* is therefore addressed to the reader as much as to those who demand a date.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:38

> **And they say, "When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for a Date**

*And they say, "When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?"* The question is a challenge rather than an inquiry. The opponents are not asking for information they intend to act on; they are demanding that the promise be dated, on the assumption that an undated promise is an empty one. The conditional *if you are truthful* makes the demand's purpose explicit — it is a test of the Prophet's credibility framed as a request for a schedule.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that this question is a frequent challenge in the Qur'an, often associated with the notion of seeking to hasten, and that in this context it follows immediately upon God's command not to seek to hasten the promise of punishment. The juxtaposition is the verse's force: the command is given at verse 37, and the demand that violates it is recorded at verse 38 without comment.

The commentators cite parallel instances at 10:48, 27:71, 34:29, and 67:25, indicating that this is a recurring pattern rather than a single incident. The recurrence matters because it shows the demand to be a standard move in the opponents' repertoire, and the sūrah's answer to it is therefore addressed to the move rather than to the occasion.

**Haste Condemned and Haste Excused**

Al-Rāzī's note draws the distinction on which the two verses turn. It is not the mere fact of being hasty that is condemned, since haste is part of human nature as the previous verse attests; what is condemned is challenging God to be so, in which case people display an excess of haste. The trait is created; the demand is chosen.

This is a precise piece of moral psychology and it deserves attention. The sūrah does not ask human beings to be without impatience, which would be to ask them to be other than they were created. It asks them not to convert impatience into a claim against God — not to say that a promise is false because it has not yet been dated.

The distinction also explains why the verse records the demand rather than rebutting it. There is nothing to rebut. The demand does not contain a false proposition; it contains an illegitimate inference, from the absence of a date to the absence of a promise. The sūrah's method is to let the inference stand exposed rather than to argue against it.

**Why the Date Is Withheld**

The sūrah does not answer the question, and the withholding is deliberate. Verse 37 promised that the signs would soon be shown without specifying when, and verse 39 will describe what the opponents do not know. A date would convert the promise into a prediction that could be checked and then dismissed, which is precisely what the opponents are seeking.

There is a further reason, connected to verse 40's statement that the punishment will come suddenly. Al-Qurṭubī and others note that the suddenness of the Hour is connected with the fact that its time is known only to God, with a cross-reference to 79:44. If the time were known, the coming would not be sudden, and the moral function of the promise would be lost.

The withholding therefore serves the promise rather than undermining it. An undated warning that may come at any moment requires a different response from a dated prediction: not calculation but readiness. The opponents' demand for a date is a request to be released from that readiness, and the sūrah declines to grant it.

**The Question as a Test of the Questioner**

The demand reverses the burden the sūrah has been assigning. At verse 24 the opponents were told *Bring me your proof!* — a demand for evidence they had never supplied. At verse 38 they respond by demanding evidence of a different kind: not a demonstration but a date. The substitution is itself revealing.

A date is not evidence. It would not establish that the promise is true, only that it has been scheduled, and a scheduled promise that failed to arrive would be worse for the one who made it. The opponents' demand therefore does not actually seek what would satisfy an inquirer; it seeks what would allow them to declare the matter closed.

The sūrah's refusal to engage on these terms is consistent throughout. It offers observation at verse 30, the structure of mortality at verses 34–35, and the promise of signs at verse 37. It does not offer a timetable, because a timetable would answer a question nobody should be asking and leave the real question — what one does while waiting — untouched.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

Verse 38 follows verse 37's statement that man was created of haste, and the connection is causal as well as sequential. The verse does not merely record a demand; it exhibits the disposition described in the preceding verse. The sūrah states the trait and then shows it in operation, which is more effective than either move alone.

It also prepares verses 39 to 41, which describe what the opponents do not know, the suddenness of the coming, and the fate of those who mocked earlier messengers. The sequence moves from the demand to the answer, and the answer is not a date but a description of what will happen when the date arrives.

The pastoral function is worth noting as well. The Prophet ﷺ is being challenged publicly on the timing of a promise he did not make and cannot date. The verses that follow reassure him not by supplying the date but by showing that the challenge is a recurring feature of the office and that its outcome has been consistent.

---

**What the Question Reveals**

The demand for a date is worth examining as evidence about the questioners. A person who intends to act on a warning does not need its date; he needs its content. The request for a schedule indicates that the opponents are not considering what to do but how to dismiss the warning, and the sūrah's refusal to supply the date is a refusal to assist in the dismissal.

This is why verse 39 follows with a description rather than an answer. The opponents asked when; they are told what. The substitution is the sūrah's method throughout — it does not meet illegitimate demands on their own terms but supplies what the situation actually requires, and leaves the demand unmet.

The pattern recurs at verse 24, where the demand was reversed: instead of answering the opponents' implicit challenge, the Prophet ﷺ was told to demand proof from them. In both cases the sūrah declines the framing it is given and substitutes the framing the subject requires.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:39

> **If those who disbelieved but knew of the time when they shall not be able to hold back the Fire from their faces or from their backs, nor be helped!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Unfinished Conditional**

*If those who disbelieved but knew of the time when they shall not be able to hold back the Fire from their faces or from their backs, nor be helped!* The verse is a conditional clause whose completion is implicit. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī both note that the apodosis is understood: *if they but knew, they would do otherwise*. The sentence breaks off, and the breaking is the point.

The grammatical incompleteness does specific work. A completed conditional would state what they would do, and the statement would be information about them. An unfinished conditional leaves the consequence to be supplied by the reader, who supplies it from his own understanding of what knowledge of that moment would produce. The verse thereby implicates the reader rather than describing a third party.

The commentators' unanimity on the ellipsis indicates that they read it as deliberate rather than as an accident of style. The Qur'an uses incomplete conditionals elsewhere for the same effect, and the convention would have been familiar to the audience, who would supply the missing clause without being told to.

**Faces and Backs**

The verse describes a moment when the Fire cannot be held back *from their faces or from their backs*, and the commentators direct the reader to 8:50 for the symbolism of punishment visited on faces and backs. The pairing denotes totality: there is no direction from which the punishment can be averted, and no posture that avoids it.

The image also carries a moral sense. The face is what one presents; the back is what one cannot see. Punishment reaching both indicates that nothing is shielded — neither the visible self nor the hidden one. The commentators read this as a description of exposure rather than merely of pain.

The clause *nor be helped* completes the picture. It is not only that the punishment cannot be deflected but that no assistance will be available. This connects to verse 43, where the sūrah will ask whether the idols can defend their worshippers, and answers that they cannot help themselves. The two verses make the same point from opposite ends.

**What They Do Not Know**

The verse's force rests on the gap between what the opponents assume and what the verse describes. At verse 38 they demanded a date, which presupposes that the promise is the kind of thing that can be scheduled and awaited. Verse 39 describes a moment that cannot be prepared for in that way, and the description is what they do not know.

The gap is not about information. The opponents have been told repeatedly that the Hour is coming; what they lack is the understanding of what its coming will be like. The verse supplies that understanding in a single image, and the image is what makes the demand for a date appear in its true light.

This is the sūrah's method at verse 39 as elsewhere: it does not argue against the opponents' position but describes the reality their position ignores. The description does the work that an argument would otherwise have to do, and it does so more efficiently because it appeals to imagination rather than to inference.

**The Verse as Answer to Verse 38**

Read against the preceding verse, verse 39 is the answer to the demand for a date. The answer is not that the date is unknown but that the question is malformed. What the opponents need is not a schedule but an understanding of what is scheduled, and the verse supplies the second while withholding the first.

The withholding and the supplying are complementary. If the date were given, the description would be less urgent, because a known future can be postponed in the mind. If the description were withheld, the date would be the only thing to consider. The verse gives the description and denies the date, which produces the response the sūrah wants — not calculation but readiness.

Al-Rāzī's note that the apodosis is *they would do otherwise* identifies the intended response. Knowledge of that moment would change behaviour. The verse therefore functions as a provision of that knowledge in the only form available before the moment arrives, which is description rather than experience.

**The Conditional and the Reader**

The unfinished conditional has a further effect on the reader, who is not among those described. The verse addresses *those who disbelieve*, but the ellipsis invites anyone reading to complete it, and completing it requires imagining oneself in the situation described. The verse thus converts a statement about others into an exercise for the reader.

This is characteristic of the sūrah's method from verse 34 onward, where the general rule of mortality was applied to the reader as much as to the Prophet's opponents. The movement is consistent: establish a truth about a third party, then close the distance between the third party and the reader.

The verse's brevity supports this. At twenty-odd words it is among the shortest in the passage, and the brevity leaves space for the reader's completion. A longer verse would have filled the space with description; this one leaves it open, and what fills it is the reader's own understanding of what he would do if he knew.

---

**The Image and Its Work**

The verse's image — the Fire that cannot be held back from faces or from backs — does more than describe pain. It describes exposure. A person who can shield his face has retained some agency; one who cannot shield either side has none. The commentators' cross-reference to 8:50 for the symbolism of faces and backs indicates that they read the pairing as total rather than merely graphic.

The clause *nor be helped* completes the exposure by removing the social dimension. It is not only that the individual cannot defend himself but that no one will assist him. This anticipates verse 43's question about gods who defend, and the two verses together exclude both self-help and help from others.

The brevity of the verse is functional. At this length it does not overwhelm the reader with description; it presents one image and leaves the implication to be drawn. The unfinished conditional works the same way, and the two devices together make the verse one of the most economical in the passage.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:40

> **Nay, but it will come upon them suddenly, and confound them. Then they will not be able to repel it, nor will they be granted respite.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**It Will Come Suddenly**

*Nay, but it will come upon them suddenly, and confound them.* The verse answers the demand for a date by describing the manner of the coming. The commentators note that the suddenness of God's punishment is mentioned at 6:31, 7:95, 7:187, 12:107, 22:55, 26:202, 29:53, 39:55, 43:66, and 47:18 — a list long enough to indicate that this is a settled feature of Qur'anic eschatology rather than a local claim.

The suddenness is connected to the fact that the Hour is known only to God, with a cross-reference to 79:44. The commentators draw the inference explicitly: because the time is unknown, the coming is sudden by definition. It is not that God chooses to surprise; it is that a time known only to God cannot be anticipated by anyone else.

The word rendered *confound* — *tabhatuhum* — carries the sense of being overwhelmed or dumbfounded. The commentators gloss it as a state in which one cannot respond. The verse therefore describes not only an unexpected arrival but an arrival that disables the capacity to deal with it, which is what distinguishes it from an ordinary surprise.

**No Repelling, No Respite**

*Then they will not be able to repel it, nor will they be granted respite.* The two clauses exclude the two responses available to someone confronted by a threat: resistance and delay. Neither will be available, and the verse states both exclusions rather than one, so that no evasion remains.

The clause about respite connects to verse 38's demand. The opponents asked when the promise would come, which presupposes that knowing the time would give them room to prepare. The verse removes the presupposition by stating that even when it comes there will be no respite — no interval between the coming and its effect.

This is the answer to the demand in its most direct form. A date would not help, because the coming would not wait for preparation. The opponents' question assumes a kind of time in which things can be scheduled and prepared for; the verse describes a coming that operates outside that kind of time.

**Al-Sulamī on Confusion**

The commentary records a gloss from al-Sulamī that shifts the verse's register: people are confounded by something to the degree that they are heedless of its Creator, but whoever is always in the Presence of God will not be confounded by anything of the world. The reading makes confusion a function of heedlessness rather than of surprise.

On this account the verse is not only describing a future event but diagnosing a present condition. A person who is heedless is already in the state the verse describes, and the sudden coming will not introduce confusion but reveal it. The one who is in God's presence will not be confounded, not because he expected the event but because his relation to its source is already established.

The reading is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the angels at verses 19 to 28, whose worship was described as continuous and whose wariness was described as constant. The contrast between the angels' posture and the heedless person's is the contrast between being unconfounded and being confounded, and al-Sulamī's gloss draws it explicitly.

**The Verse and Verse 39**

Verses 39 and 40 form a pair. Verse 39 describes what the opponents do not know — the moment when the Fire cannot be held back; verse 40 describes how that moment will arrive — suddenly, and without respite. The first supplies the content, the second the manner, and together they answer the demand at verse 38 completely.

The pairing also explains why the sūrah withholds the date. A date would allow the opponents to locate the moment in their own timeline and to arrange their affairs around it. The two verses describe a moment that cannot be so located, and the description is what makes the withholding meaningful rather than merely evasive.

The sequence from verse 37 to verse 40 is thus complete: man is hasty; they demand a date; the date is withheld; the coming is described as sudden and irresistible. The sūrah has answered the challenge without conceding its terms, and the answer is more informative than a date would have been.

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader**

The verse's practical implication is readiness rather than calculation. If the coming cannot be dated and will not wait, then the only available preparation is a state rather than an arrangement. This is what al-Sulamī's gloss identifies, and it is what the sūrah's withholding of the date is designed to produce.

The implication is not that the reader should live in fear of a surprise. It is that the response to an undatable event is different in kind from the response to a scheduled one: not planning but disposition. The sūrah has been describing that disposition throughout — the angels' continuous worship, the wariness of verse 28, the reverence of verse 49.

The verse therefore connects the eschatological material to the practical material of the sūrah. What is said about the Hour is not separate from what is said about worship; the second is the response the first requires, and the sūrah presents them together rather than in isolation.

---

**Suddenness as a Moral Category**

The suddenness described in the verse is not incidental to the eschatology but constitutive of it. A dated event can be prepared for and, in the preparing, postponed in the mind. An undatable event cannot be postponed; it requires a standing readiness rather than a scheduled response. The commentators' connection of suddenness to the Hour's being known only to God makes this structural rather than accidental.

Al-Sulamī's gloss identifies the corresponding disposition: whoever is always in the Presence of God will not be confounded by anything of the world. The readiness required is therefore not vigilance about a future event but a present relation, and the relation is what the sūrah has been describing since verse 19 in the angels' continuous worship.

The verse thus connects eschatology to practice without stating the connection. What is said about the manner of the coming implies what is required of the one awaiting it, and the implication is left for the reader to draw rather than spelled out — which is the sūrah's consistent method.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:41

> **Messengers were certainly mocked before thee. Then those who scoffed at them were beset by that which they used to mock.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mocked Before Thee**

*Messengers were certainly mocked before thee.* The verse turns from the description of the coming to the Prophet's own situation, and the turn is consolatory. The commentators note that he is reminded here, as elsewhere, that he was not the first messenger to be mocked by his people, with cross-references to 13:32, 15:11, 36:30, and 43:7.

The reminder answers the situation described at verse 36, where the disbelievers took the Prophet ﷺ in naught but mockery. Verse 36 recorded the mockery; verse 41 places it in a pattern. The pattern is what converts an insult into a datum, and the conversion is the verse's consolation.

The commentators also cite 6:10, which the verse echoes. The recurrence across sūrahs indicates that this reassurance was needed repeatedly, which is itself information about the Prophet's situation — the mockery was not occasional but sustained, and the reassurance was correspondingly repeated.

**Beset by What They Mocked**

*Then those who scoffed at them were beset by that which they used to mock.* The commentators note that the idea of being beset by what one mocked recurs at 6:10, 11:8, 16:34, 39:48, 40:83, 45:33, and 46:26, and that the word connotes being both surrounded and overcome by something. The double sense is important: the mockers are not merely confronted by what they mocked but enclosed by it.

Al-Ṭabarī records the standard interpretation: those who ridiculed the idea of punishment in the Hereafter for their misdeeds in this life will eventually suffer that punishment and will not be able to escape it. The reading connects the verse directly to verses 39 and 40, which described the coming as sudden and inescapable.

The irony is exact and the verse leaves it uncommented. What was mocked was the punishment; what besets the mockers is the punishment. The thing dismissed as unreal becomes the thing that surrounds them, and the sūrah states the reversal in a single clause without drawing attention to it.

**The Pattern and Its Evidence**

The verse's consolation depends on the pattern being evidential rather than merely recurrent. That earlier messengers were mocked shows the Prophet ﷺ that he is not alone; that the mockers were beset shows what the pattern concludes. Both halves are needed, and the verse gives both.

This distinguishes the verse from simple reassurance. It does not say that things will be better, or that the mockery will stop. It says that the mockery has a consistent outcome, and that the outcome has been observed repeatedly. The consolation is empirical rather than promissory, which makes it stronger.

The sūrah's method here matches its method elsewhere. At verse 25 the Prophet ﷺ was told that every messenger before him was given the same message; here he is told that every messenger before him was mocked and that the mockers were beset. Both are genealogies, and both draw their force from completeness rather than from selection.

**The Verse as Answer to Verse 38**

Verse 41 also answers the demand at verse 38 from a different direction. The opponents asked when the promise would come; the verse replies that the question has been asked before, of earlier messengers, and that the answer was the same — the coming was not dated, and it came.

The historical answer is more effective than a doctrinal one because it does not require the opponents to accept anything. It reports what happened, and the report is available to anyone who knows the accounts of the earlier peoples, which the audience did. The verse appeals to shared knowledge rather than to revelation.

This is why the verse is placed after verses 39 and 40 rather than before them. The description of the coming establishes what is at stake; the historical pattern establishes that the description is not speculative. The order matters, and reversing it would weaken both moves.

**The Consolation and Its Limits**

The verse does not promise that the Prophet ﷺ will see the outcome. The mockers were beset, but the verse does not say when, and verse 34 has already established that he will die as every human being dies. The consolation is that the pattern holds, not that he will witness its completion.

This is a sober form of reassurance and it is characteristic of the sūrah. The comfort offered throughout is the removal of isolation rather than the promise of improvement — at verse 25 that he is not innovating, at verse 36 that he is not alone in being mocked, here that the mockery has a consistent end.

The limits are what make the consolation credible. A promise of victory within the Prophet's lifetime would have been falsifiable and, in the event, falsified. A statement about the pattern is not falsifiable in that way, and the sūrah offers the statement that can bear the weight rather than the one that sounds better.

---

**Why the Pattern Consoles**

The consolation offered by the verse depends on the pattern being complete rather than selective. *Messengers were certainly mocked before thee* is a universal claim, and the commentators' cross-references to 13:32, 15:11, 36:30, and 43:7 indicate that the Qur'an makes it repeatedly. The Prophet ﷺ is not being told that some messengers faced mockery but that mockery accompanies the office.

The second half is equally universal: those who scoffed were beset by what they mocked. The commentators note the recurrence at 6:10, 11:8, 16:34, 39:48, 40:83, 45:33, and 46:26. Two complete patterns, stated together, produce a consolation that does not depend on the outcome of any particular case.

This is why the verse can console without promising. It does not say the Prophet ﷺ will be vindicated in his lifetime, and verse 34 has already established that he will die. It says that the pattern holds, and a pattern that holds does not require the observer to witness its completion.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:42

> **Say, "Who will protect you, night and day, from the Compassionate?" Nay, but they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Protection Night and Day**

*Say, "Who will protect you, night and day, from the Compassionate?"* The question is rhetorical and expects no answer, because none exists. Al-Rāzī notes that the verse serves as an implicit reminder that God is aware of the disbelievers and powerful over them by night and by day, but that they are nonetheless oblivious.

Al-Qurṭubī draws out the practical implication: while human beings are going about their daily affairs or sleeping, God is able to unleash His punishment upon them at any time. The pairing of night and day covers both states — waking activity and unconsciousness — so that no interval is exempt.

The phrase echoes the idiom used at verse 20 of the angels, who glorify *night and day* without tiring. The same span of time is used of the angels' worship and of God's power over the disbelievers, and the echo is deliberate: the period is fully occupied in both cases, by worship in the one and by sovereignty in the other.

**Why the Name al-Raḥmān**

Al-Rāzī records that the name *the Compassionate* is used here because it would cause one to reflect upon the fact that God encompasses all things with His Mercy. The choice is not incidental, and its force depends on the verse's context: the question is about protection from punishment, and the name invoked is the name of mercy.

The tension is the point. The One from whom protection would be needed is the One whose name denotes compassion, which means that the punishment is not arbitrary but stands in a relation to mercy that the verse leaves for the reader to work out. The sūrah does not resolve the tension; it names it.

The name has appeared twice before in this sūrah. At verse 26 it was the name against which the claim of Divine offspring was made; at verse 36 the opponents were described as disbelievers in the remembrance of the Compassionate. Each occurrence attaches the name to a specific refusal, and this verse continues the pattern.

**Turning Away From Remembrance**

*Nay, but they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord.* The clause repeats the diagnosis given at verse 24 — *most of them do not know the truth; so they turn away* — and at verse 32, where the idolaters turn away from the signs of the sky. The same verb recurs, and the recurrence identifies a single condition underlying the various refusals.

The object here is *the remembrance of their Lord*, which connects the verse to verse 36's use of the same root. The opponents accused the Prophet ﷺ of mentioning their gods while refusing to remember God; this verse states the refusal directly. The irony recorded at verse 36 is here made explicit.

The clause also explains why the question has no answer. The opponents cannot say who will protect them because they have turned away from the only One who could. Their inability to answer is not a gap in their information but a consequence of the turning away the verse describes.

**The Verse and Verses 39-41**

Verse 42 completes the sequence begun at verse 39. Those verses described the coming as sudden, inescapable, and without respite; this verse asks who will protect against it. The question makes explicit what the description implied — that no protection is available — and it does so in the form of a challenge rather than a statement.

The challenge is addressed through the Prophet: *Say*. The instruction places the question in his mouth, which makes it public and direct, and which continues the pattern established at verse 24 where he was told to say *Bring me your proof!* Both are demands issued through him rather than assertions made about the audience.

The sequence from verse 38 to verse 42 is thus complete. The demand for a date; the description of what they do not know; the suddenness of the coming; the pattern of earlier mockers; and now the question about protection. Each step removes one of the assumptions on which the demand rested, and the last removes the hope of help.

**The Verse as Comfort**

The verse also functions as reassurance to the Prophet ﷺ, though it is addressed to the opponents. The statement that God is aware of them and powerful over them by night and day is a statement that the matter is not in his hands and need not be. Al-Rāzī's gloss makes this explicit by noting their obliviousness alongside His awareness.

The contrast between His awareness and their obliviousness is the verse's comfort. The Prophet ﷺ is being mocked by people who do not know what he knows, and the verse observes that their ignorance does not diminish God's knowledge of them. The asymmetry is what allows him to leave the matter there.

This is the same form of comfort the sūrah has offered throughout. At verse 41 the consolation was the pattern of earlier mockers; here it is the sovereignty that surrounds the mockers. Neither promises intervention; both remove the burden of having to secure the outcome.

---

**The Name and the Question**

The use of *al-Raḥmān* in a question about protection from punishment is the verse's most deliberate feature. Al-Rāzī's note — that the name causes one to reflect that God encompasses all things with His Mercy — identifies the intended effect: the reader is asked to hold mercy and punishment together rather than to choose between them.

The sūrah does not resolve the relation. It names the Compassionate as the One from whom protection would be needed, and leaves the tension standing. This is consistent with its treatment elsewhere: verse 23 established that God is not questioned about what He does, and the present verse assumes that establishing rather than repeating it.

The closing clause supplies the human side. *They turn away from the remembrance of their Lord* identifies the refusal that makes the question unanswerable. The opponents cannot say who will protect them, not because the question is difficult but because they have turned away from the only possible answer, and the verse notes the fact without comment.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:43

> **Or do they have gods to defend them apart from Us? They cannot help themselves; nor are they given protection against Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gods to Defend Them**

*Or do they have gods to defend them apart from Us?* The question returns to the idols, which the sūrah has not addressed directly since verse 24. The return is timed: after four verses describing an inescapable coming, the question about the idols' usefulness can be asked with maximum force.

The commentators note that the powerlessness of the idolaters' gods, for both themselves and their worshippers, is also mentioned at 7:192 and 7:197. The recurrence indicates that this is a settled point in the Qur'an's treatment of idolatry, and the verse does not need to argue it at length.

The phrase *apart from Us* is important. The question is not whether they have gods but whether they have gods other than God who can defend them. The formulation assumes the answer: any defence would have to come from God, and no other source is available. The idols are excluded not by argument but by the structure of the question.

**They Cannot Help Themselves**

*They cannot help themselves.* The clause is the verse's central observation and its most economical argument. Before considering whether the idols can help their worshippers, the verse notes that they cannot help themselves. The inference is immediate: what cannot act on its own behalf cannot act on another's.

Al-Rāzī draws out the consequence: the inability of the idols to defend the idolaters implies that the idolaters will have no defence against God's punishment when it comes, either in this world in the form of defeat and death, or in the Hereafter in the form of punishment. The verse covers both, and the commentators note both.

The observation connects to verse 21's question about gods taken from the earth who resurrect. There the test was whether the idols could perform the defining function of a god; here the test is whether they can perform the most basic function of an agent. Both questions have the same answer, and the second is the simpler.

**"Nor Are They Given Protection Against Us"**

The closing clause — *wa-lā hum minnā yuṣḥabūn* — is rendered by the commentators in two ways. The standard reading is *nor are they given protection against Us*. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī record an alternative: *They will not have Us as a Companion*. Both readings are preserved, and both make the same point about the absence of divine accompaniment.

The alternative reading is worth attending to because it shifts the emphasis from protection to relationship. On that reading the verse is not only saying that the idols will not be shielded but that they will not have God with them, which is a different and more fundamental deprivation. The commentators preserve both without preferring either.

The clause completes the verse's argument. The idols cannot defend their worshippers; they cannot defend themselves; and they will not have divine accompaniment. The three exclusions leave nothing, and the verse's brevity reflects the fact that the case requires no elaboration.

**The Verse in the Argument**

Verse 43 closes the sequence that began at verse 38. The demand for a date was met with a description of an inescapable coming; the hope of protection is met with the observation that the protectors cannot protect themselves. The sequence removes both the demand's premise and its fallback.

The verse also returns the sūrah to its original subject. From verse 21 to verse 29 the argument concerned the gods taken from the earth and the angels; verses 30 to 37 turned to the physical world and mortality; verses 38 to 43 return to the idols in the light of what has been established. The return is not a repetition but a conclusion.

What has been established makes the conclusion available in a single verse. The idols cannot resurrect; the sky is a preserved canopy; human life is brief and tested; the coming is sudden; no respite will be granted. Against that background the question about the idols' usefulness answers itself, and the verse simply asks it.

**The Verse and the Khuzāʿah**

The verse has a specific application to the practice described at verse 26. Al-Qurṭubī recorded that the tribe of Khuzāʿah worshipped the angels in order to obtain their intercession, which presupposed that the beings worshipped could act on their worshippers' behalf. Verse 43 removes the presupposition for the idols, and verses 27 to 28 removed it for the angels.

The two cases are handled differently and the difference is instructive. The angels can intercede, but only for one with whom God is content; the idols cannot act at all. The sūrah distinguishes between a limited capacity exercised under command and an absence of capacity, and it does not conflate the two.

The distinction matters for how the verse should be read. It is not a blanket denial that any being other than God has any role. It is a denial that the idols have one, and the denial rests on the observation that they cannot help themselves. The angels' case was settled differently, at greater length, and on different grounds.

---

**The Simplest Argument**

Of all the arguments the sūrah makes against idolatry, this is the simplest. Verse 21 asked whether gods taken from the earth can resurrect — a demanding test. Verse 43 asks whether they can help themselves — a test any observer can apply. The movement from the first to the second is a movement toward the undeniable.

Al-Rāzī's gloss draws the consequence for the worshippers: the idols' inability to defend them means the idolaters will have no defence when the punishment comes, in this world through defeat and death, or in the Hereafter through punishment. The verse covers both, and the double coverage leaves no interval in which the idols might be useful.

The alternative reading of the closing clause — *they will not have Us as a Companion* — adds a further dimension. It is not only that the idols cannot defend but that they will lack divine accompaniment, which is a deprivation more fundamental than the loss of protection. The commentators preserve both readings, and both close the verse's argument completely.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:44

> **Nay, but We granted enjoyment to them and their fathers till life grew long for them. Do they not consider how We come upon the land, reducing it of its outlying regions? Is it they who shall prevail?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Enjoyment Granted**

*Nay, but We granted enjoyment to them and their fathers till life grew long for them.* The commentators gloss the opening simply: the idolaters were granted enjoyment, meaning God provided them with the goods of this world. The provision is stated as a fact and not as a reward, and the distinction matters for everything that follows in the verse.

The phrase *till life grew long for them* is the verse's hinge. The enjoyment was not brief; it extended across generations, until duration itself became the evidence the opponents took as vindication. A people who have prospered for a long time naturally infer that their way is sound, and the verse identifies the inference as the error.

The verse therefore answers an argument the opponents have not stated aloud. Their position is not merely that the idols are their ancestors' gods but that the ancestors' prosperity testifies on their behalf. The first clause grants the prosperity and the second removes its evidential force.

**Reducing the Land of Its Outlying Regions**

*Do they not consider how We come upon the land, reducing it of its outlying regions?* The commentators record three readings. Some understand the verse to refer to the conquest of a territory, which begins on its frontiers. For others it refers to the loss of a land's peoples and its blessings. For others still, it refers to the loss of the learned sages of a community.

The third reading has a special resonance with a ḥadīth the commentators cite: *God does not take away knowledge by removing it from His servants, but rather takes it away by removing the learned, till, when no learned people remain, people rely on ignorant leaders who are consulted and who make judgments without knowledge. They are thus misled and mislead.* On this reading the reduction is intellectual before it is territorial.

Al-Rāzī notes that reading the statement as a reference to conquest and loss fits more closely with the remainder of the verse — *Is it they who shall prevail?* — and the observation is sound. The closing question concerns political outcome, and the territorial reading supplies the most direct antecedent.

**The Argument From the Frontiers**

The image of a land being reduced from its edges is precise and would have been familiar to an audience living on the margins of two empires. Conquest does not usually strike the centre first; it takes the periphery and works inward. The verse asks the audience to observe a process they could see occurring around them.

The rhetorical force lies in the direction of the movement. If the reduction were random it would prove nothing, but a reduction that proceeds from the borders inward has the character of a deliberate approach. The verse asks *Do they not consider* — the same appeal to observation used at verses 30 and 32 — and the observation is of a pattern rather than an event.

This connects the verse to the sūrah's method throughout. The evidence offered is not revelation the audience must accept but a process they can watch. What the verse adds is the interpretation: the reduction is attributed to God, and the attribution is what the audience is being asked to consider rather than merely notice.

**"Is It They Who Shall Prevail?"**

The closing question is short and expects a negative answer. Al-Rāzī notes that it fits the territorial reading of the preceding clause, and the fit is exact: a land being reduced from its frontiers is not a land that is prevailing. The question asks the audience to reconcile their assumption with the process in front of them.

The question also answers the opponents' implicit argument from duration. Long enjoyment was taken as evidence of favour; the reduction of the frontiers is evidence of the opposite. The verse sets the two observations against each other and asks which the audience will read.

There is an ambiguity the verse leaves open. *Is it they who shall prevail?* could refer to the idolaters or to the Prophet ﷺ and his followers. The commentators do not resolve it, and the ambiguity serves the verse: on either reading the answer is the same, and the audience is left to supply the reference.

**The Verse and the Preceding Sequence**

Verse 44 follows the sequence at verses 38 to 43, which removed every fallback the opponents had: no date, no escape, no respite, no protecting gods. This verse addresses the last remaining ground — the evidence of their own prosperity — and removes it as well.

The placement is therefore argumentative rather than incidental. Having closed off the future, the sūrah turns to the past and present, where the opponents still have an argument. The verse grants the facts of that argument and denies its conclusion, which is the most economical way to dispose of it.

It also prepares the verses that follow. Verse 45 will say that the Prophet ﷺ warns only through revelation and that the deaf do not hear; verse 46 will describe the recognition that comes too late. The sequence moves from the evidence available now to the response it fails to produce.

**Duration as Evidence and Duration as Test**

The verse's treatment of long enjoyment reverses a common reading of prosperity. On the opponents' account, duration testifies to favour; on the verse's account, duration is the period in which the test is taken. This connects directly to verse 35, where the sūrah said *We try you with evil and with good, as a test*.

The connection is important because it explains why the enjoyment was granted at all. It was not withheld because the recipients were unworthy, nor granted because they were worthy; it was granted as the condition of the test. The verse's first clause states the grant and its second clause states the reduction, and between them lies the period in which the response was to be made.

The pastoral implication is available to any reader. Prosperity is not a verdict, and its duration is not evidence. The verse says this about the idolaters, but it says it in terms general enough to apply to anyone who has taken a long run of good fortune as a sign that his position is sound.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:45

> **Say, "I only warn you through a revelation." But the deaf do not hear the call however much they are warned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"I Only Warn You Through a Revelation"**

*Say, "I only warn you through a revelation."* The verse commands the Prophet to remind his audience that what he says is the result of revelation (*waḥy*). The commentators cite 53:3–4 — *Nor does he speak out of caprice. It is naught but a revelation revealed* — and direct the reader to 28:7 and 16:68 on the nature of *waḥy*.

The word *only* is the verse's emphasis. The Prophet ﷺ is not warning on his own authority, from his own reasoning, or in pursuit of his own interest. The limitation is stated as a restriction on his role rather than as a claim about its importance, and the restriction is what makes the warning credible.

The instruction *Say* places the statement in his mouth, continuing the pattern established at verses 24 and 42. The sūrah repeatedly has him speak rather than being spoken about, and each occasion addresses a different objection — at verse 24 the demand for proof, at verse 42 the question about protection, here the question about his authority.

**The Deaf Who Do Not Hear**

*But the deaf do not hear the call however much they are warned.* Al-Rāzī explains that they are deaf not in the literal sense but insofar as they listen to the revelation and do not act upon what is said; one is effectively deaf to a message if one ignores its significance. The deafness is functional rather than physical.

The commentators note that spiritual deafness is an idea evoked throughout the Qur'an, with cross-references to 2:18, 5:71, 6:25, and 31:7. The recurrence indicates a settled category rather than a local metaphor, and the audience would have recognised the description as applying to a condition rather than to a disability.

The clause *however much they are warned* is important. The deafness is not curable by repetition, which means the obstacle is not a shortage of warning. This connects to verse 24's diagnosis — *most of them do not know the truth; so they turn away* — and to verse 42's *they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord*.

**The Variant Reading**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī record that some read the verb *hear* in the second person, so that the verse would be addressed to the Prophet: *Thou canst not cause the deaf to hear when they are warned.* The variant shifts the addressee and, with it, the verse's function.

On the third-person reading the verse describes the opponents; on the second-person reading it addresses the Prophet's limitation. The second is the more pastorally pointed, since it tells him directly that the failure to be heard is not a failure of his effort. The commentators preserve both without preferring either.

The two readings are compatible. If the deaf cannot be made to hear, then the one warning them cannot cause them to hear, and both statements describe the same situation from different ends. The variant supplies the personal application of the general description.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not say that the opponents are incapable of hearing. It says they do not hear, and al-Rāzī's gloss locates the deafness in the failure to act rather than in the failure to perceive. They hear the call; they do not act on it, and the non-action is what constitutes the deafness.

This distinction preserves responsibility. A person who cannot hear is not blameworthy for not responding; a person who hears and does not act is. The verse's description is therefore not an excuse for the opponents but an indictment, and the indictment is precise about where the failure lies.

It also explains why the warning is issued at all. If the deafness were constitutive, warning would be pointless. The verse records that the warning continues *however much* it is repeated, which presupposes that hearing remains possible and that the obstacle is not in the faculty but in the will.

**The Verse and Verse 44**

Verse 45 follows verse 44's appeal to observation — *Do they not consider how We come upon the land?* — and records the failure of that appeal. The evidence was available; it was not taken up. The verse describes the condition that makes the evidence ineffective.

The sequence is diagnostic. Verse 44 presents the evidence and asks the audience to consider it; verse 45 states that the audience will not consider it, and identifies why. The sūrah does not move from evidence to conclusion but from evidence to the obstacle that blocks the conclusion.

This is a recurring structure in the passage. Verse 30 asked *Will they not, then, believe?*; verse 32 said they turn away from the signs; verse 44 asks them to consider; verse 45 says they do not hear. The pattern of appeal and refusal is stated repeatedly, and each statement adds a detail about the refusal.

**The Limit on the Prophet's Role**

The verse's two halves together define the Prophet's role precisely. He warns through revelation; he does not cause the deaf to hear. The first clause establishes the source of his authority, the second its limit, and the limit is not a deficiency but a specification.

This is the same limit established at verse 23, where the sūrah said that God shall not be questioned about what He does but they shall be questioned. The outcome belongs to God; the delivery belongs to the messenger. The verse at 24 used the term *al-balāgh al-mubīn* in a related sūrah for precisely this limit.

The specification is consolatory. A man whose warnings are not heard is told that the hearing was never within his remit, and that his task was the warning. The sūrah has offered this form of comfort repeatedly — at verse 25, verse 36, verse 41 — and each time it has done so by defining the role rather than by promising an outcome.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:46

> **And if but a breath of thy Lord's Punishment were to touch them, they would surely say, "Oh, woe unto us. Truly we were wrongdoers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Breath of Punishment**

*And if but a breath of thy Lord's Punishment were to touch them, they would surely say, "Oh, woe unto us. Truly we were wrongdoers."* The word rendered *breath* is *nafḥah*, which the commentators note can also mean breeze or fragrance. Al-Rāzī explains that it conveys something light and very slight, in the sense that one does not come into contact with the thing itself but with what emanates from it.

The lexical choice is the verse's central device. A *nafḥah* is not the punishment but its edge — a whiff, an emanation, the smallest possible contact. The verse does not describe the punishment arriving; it describes the minimum conceivable degree of contact with it, and then reports the response that minimum produces.

The effect is to make the point about proportion. If a breath produces the confession, what will the thing itself produce? The verse does not ask the question, and does not need to. The comparison is set up by the word choice and left for the reader to complete.

**The Confession and Its Timing**

*Oh, woe unto us. Truly we were wrongdoers.* Al-Qurṭubī notes that the recognition will come when it is too late for it to do them any good, with a cross-reference to 67:10–11. The confession is accurate and useless, and the verse's force lies in the combination.

The accuracy matters. The opponents are not described as confessing under duress something they do not believe; they are described as recognising what they had declined to recognise. The verse therefore does not portray a coerced admission but a belated understanding, and the belatedness is what makes it tragic rather than merely dramatic.

The timing connects to verses 39 and 40, which described the coming as sudden and without respite. A recognition that arrives with the punishment cannot inform a response, because no response remains available. The verse supplies the content of the recognition and the preceding verses supply its futility.

**The Conditional and Its Implication**

The verse is a conditional: *if but a breath were to touch them, they would surely say*. The certainty of the apodosis — *they would surely say* — indicates that the confession is not in doubt. What is in doubt is whether it will come in time to matter, and verses 39 and 40 have already answered that.

The conditional form has a further use. It describes a future situation in terms that allow the reader to test himself now. A person who would confess on contact with a breath of the punishment is a person who already knows what he is avoiding, and the verse's hypothetical is therefore a probe rather than a prediction.
