This is the same device used at verse 39, where the unfinished conditional invited the reader to supply the apodosis. Both verses describe the opponents in order to reach the reader, and both use the conditional form to make the description applicable rather than merely reportorial.

**What the Verse Reveals About the Opponents**

The verse implies that the opponents already possess the knowledge their confession expresses. A confession is not new information; it is the acknowledgement of what was known and declined. The verse therefore depicts the opponents not as ignorant but as avoidant, which is a more serious condition.

This is consistent with the diagnosis running through the passage. At verse 24 they turn away because they do not know the truth; at verse 42 they turn away from remembrance; at verse 45 they hear and do not act. The cumulative picture is of people who have the material and do not use it.

Al-Rāzī's gloss on verse 45 supports this: one is effectively deaf to a message if one ignores its significance. The deafness is not a failure of reception but of application, and verse 46 shows what the application would consist in — the confession they will eventually make.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

Verse 46 follows verse 45's statement that the deaf do not hear however much they are warned, and it supplies the counterfactual: there is a degree of contact that would produce hearing. The warning does not reach them; a breath of the punishment would. The comparison is the verse's argument.

The argument is not that punishment is more effective than warning in principle. It is that the opponents' deafness is specifically a refusal to hear warning, and that what overcomes the refusal is not better argument but direct contact. The verse identifies the limit of the Prophet's task, which verse 45 had just defined.

The placement also prepares verse 47, which describes the just scales and the weight of a mustard seed. Verse 46 shows the recognition arriving too late; verse 47 shows the reckoning that follows. The sequence moves from the moment of recognition to the assessment that makes it necessary.

**The Verse as Warning to the Reader**

The verse's practical use is to identify the point at which recognition becomes worthless. The confession *Truly we were wrongdoers* is the correct judgment; the verse's warning is that a correct judgment delivered after the fact has no value. What matters is not eventually agreeing but agreeing in time to act.

This connects to verse 40's description of the coming as sudden and without respite. If there is no interval between the coming and its effect, then the recognition the verse describes cannot be the beginning of a response. The two verses together define the urgency without ever using the word.

The sūrah's method throughout has been to describe rather than to exhort. Verse 46 is a case in point: it does not say *repent now*, but describes a moment when repentance is accurate and useless, and leaves the reader to draw the conclusion about the present.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:47

> **We shall set the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul shall be wronged in aught. Even if it be the weight of a mustard seed, We shall bring it. And We suffice as Reckoner.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Just Scales**

*We shall set the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul shall be wronged in aught.* The commentators note a grammatical ambiguity in the phrase rendered *for the Day of Resurrection*: al-Rāzī reads it as *on the Day of Resurrection*, while al-Qurṭubī reads it as *for the people of the Day of Resurrection*. Both are recorded, and the difference is minor.

The emphasis falls on *just* — *al-qisṭ*. The scales are not merely instruments of measurement but instruments of equity, and the verse's concern is that the reckoning will not be arbitrary. The clause *no soul shall be wronged in aught* states the same point negatively, so that both the instrument and its operation are covered.

The verse follows verse 46's description of a recognition that arrives too late, and the connection is important. The recognition is useless because the reckoning is exact; there is no margin in which a belated confession could alter the outcome. The justice of the scales is what makes the timing decisive.

**The Weight of a Mustard Seed**

*Even if it be the weight of a mustard seed, We shall bring it.* The commentators note that God's knowledge of the tiniest deed is symbolised elsewhere by the weight of a mustard seed at 31:16, and by other very small objects at 6:59, 10:61, 34:3, and 99:7–8. The recurrence indicates a settled idiom rather than a local image.

The mustard seed functions as a limit case. It is not merely small but proverbially small, and the verse's claim is that nothing falls below the threshold of the reckoning. The image therefore does quantitative work: it establishes that the scales operate at every scale rather than only at the level of significant acts.

The verb rendered *We shall bring it* is important. The deed is not merely recorded but produced — brought forward and presented. The commentators' cross-references to verses describing God's knowledge of small things support this reading: what is known will be brought, and the bringing is what the reckoning consists in.

**"We Suffice as Reckoner"**

The verse closes: *And We suffice as Reckoner.* The word *ḥasīb* denotes one who reckons, and the claim of sufficiency excludes any other assessor. There will be no appeal, no second opinion, no alternative accounting, and the verse states this in a single clause.

The sufficiency connects to verse 23's statement that God shall not be questioned about what He does but they shall be questioned. The two verses make a single point across a gap of twenty-four others: the reckoning runs in one direction, and the one who conducts it is not subject to review.

The clause also completes the verse's argument. The scales are just; nothing is omitted, however small; and the one who operates them requires no assistance. The three claims together leave no ground on which the outcome could be contested, which is what the verse needs given what verse 46 has just described.

**The Verse and Verse 46**

Verses 46 and 47 form a pair and are best read together. Verse 46 describes the confession that arrives too late; verse 47 describes the reckoning that makes the timing decisive. The first shows the recognition, the second shows why it cannot help.

The pairing also answers a question verse 46 leaves open. If the opponents will eventually recognise the truth, why does the recognition not avail them? The answer is that the reckoning is exact and complete — nothing is omitted, and nothing can be added after the fact. The confession adds nothing to the account.

The sūrah's method here is characteristic. It does not say *the confession will be too late*; it describes the scales, the mustard seed, and the sufficiency of the Reckoner, and leaves the reader to see why a late confession is worthless. The description does the work that an assertion would otherwise have to do.

**The Verse and Verse 35**

Verse 47 completes a movement begun at verse 35, where the sūrah said *We try you with evil and with good, as a test, and unto Us shall you be returned*. The test was stated there; the assessment is stated here. The two verses are separated by twelve others but belong to a single argument.

The connection clarifies the test's nature. What is tested is not endurance under adversity alone but the whole of a person's conduct, including the small acts symbolised by the mustard seed. The verse's insistence on minuteness indicates that the test covers everything, not merely the significant choices.

This also explains the symmetry of verse 35's *with evil and with good*. Both conditions are occasions for acts that will be weighed, and neither is exempt from the reckoning. The verse at 47 supplies the assessment that gives the test at 35 its meaning.

**What the Verse Requires**

The verse's practical implication is attention to the small. If nothing falls below the threshold, then the moral life is not composed only of significant decisions but of the accumulation of minor acts. The mustard seed is not a reassuring image in this respect; it is a demanding one.

The commentators' cross-references support this reading. The verses cited — 6:59, 10:61, 34:3, 99:7–8 — all concern God's knowledge of what is small or hidden, and the recurrence indicates that the Qur'an returns to this point because it is easily overlooked.

The verse therefore connects the eschatological material to daily practice without stating the connection. What is said about the Day of Resurrection implies something about the conduct of ordinary life, and the implication is left for the reader to draw — which is the sūrah's consistent method throughout.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:48

> **We indeed gave unto Moses and Aaron the Criterion, and a radiant light and a reminder for the reverent,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Criterion Given to Moses and Aaron**

*We indeed gave unto Moses and Aaron the Criterion, and a radiant light and a reminder for the reverent.* The verse turns from the reckoning to the history of earlier revelation, and the turn is deliberate: after describing the assessment, the sūrah describes what was given to those who will be assessed.

Al-Ṭabarī records that the *Criterion* here is understood to be the Torah, with a cross-reference to 2:53 where Moses is described as being given the Criterion. Al-Rāzī offers an alternative: the Criterion is the help God gave him in his mission. Both readings are preserved, and the commentators direct the reader to 25:1 for the term's fuller treatment.

The pairing of Moses and Aaron is worth noting. The sūrah has been describing the Prophet ﷺ as one among a line of messengers, and here it names two who served together. The joint mention anticipates the account of their mission in the verses that follow.

**The Radiant Light**

The commentators record a disagreement about the relation between the Criterion and the radiant light. Some interpret the light as referring to the Torah and consequently take the Criterion to be something else, as al-Ṭabarī notes. The commentary observes, however, that 5:44 and 6:91 treat the light and the book as one and the same.

The disagreement is instructive about how the classical tradition handled such pairs. Where two terms are conjoined, some commentators read them as distinct gifts and others as two descriptions of one gift, and the choice affects what the verse is said to confer. The commentary preserves both without resolving the question.

On either reading the verse confers illumination. Whether the light is the Torah itself or a further gift alongside it, the recipients are described as having been given the means of discernment, and the discernment is what the term *Criterion* denotes.

**A Reminder for the Reverent**

The third item — *a reminder for the reverent* — specifies the recipients. The reminder is not for everyone indiscriminately but for the reverent, and the specification is developed in verse 49, which describes those who fear their Lord unseen and are wary of the Hour.

The word rendered *reminder* is *dhikr*, the same root the sūrah used at verses 24, 36, and 42. The recurrence ties this verse to the earlier argument: the Reminder is what the messengers bring, it is what the opponents turn away from, and here it is what was given to Moses and Aaron.

The specification *for the reverent* indicates that the reminder's effectiveness depends on the disposition of its recipient. This is consistent with verse 45's description of the deaf, who hear and do not act. The reminder is given; whether it functions as a reminder depends on the one receiving it.

**Why the Verse Is Here**

Verse 48 follows the eschatological material of verses 38 to 47 and precedes the account of Moses' mission. Its function is transitional: it establishes that revelation has been given before, to messengers who faced what the Prophet ﷺ faces, and that what was given included the means of discernment.

The transition answers an objection implicit in the preceding verses. If the reckoning is exact and the recognition comes too late, what is available to those still living? The verse supplies the answer: the Criterion, the light, the reminder. These are given in this life, and they are what the reckoning will assess.

The placement also connects to verse 25, where the sūrah said that every messenger before the Prophet ﷺ was given the same message. Verse 48 gives the claim a specific instance, naming two messengers and three gifts, and the specificity is what makes the earlier general claim concrete.

**The Three Gifts**

The verse lists three things: the Criterion, a radiant light, and a reminder. Whether the first two are distinct or identical, the list covers discernment, illumination, and recollection — the faculties required to respond to revelation. The verse does not explain how they differ, and the commentators' disagreement about the first two reflects that.

The three together describe what a community needs in order to be assessed fairly. It needs to distinguish right from wrong (the Criterion), to see its situation clearly (the light), and to be recalled to what it already knows (the reminder). The reckoning at verse 47 presupposes all three.

This is why the verse is placed where it is. Having described the assessment, the sūrah describes the provision that makes the assessment just. A reckoning that weighed acts without having supplied discernment would not be the just reckoning the verse at 47 describes, and the connection between the two verses is what makes the provision at verse 48 necessary rather than merely generous.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse contributes to the sūrah's central argument by supplying an instance of the pattern it has been asserting. At verse 25 the claim was that every messenger was given the same message; at verse 41, that every messenger was mocked; here, that a specific pair of messengers was given specific gifts.

The instance matters because it is not abstract. Moses and Aaron are named, the gifts are enumerated, and the recipients of the reminder are specified. The sūrah's argument from history depends on such particulars, and the verse supplies them.

The verse also prepares the reader for what follows. The account of Moses' mission will occupy the next several verses, and this verse establishes what he was given before describing what he did. The order is deliberate: the gifts precede the mission, and the mission is conducted with them. Nothing in the account that follows will describe Moses acting from his own resources, and the verse has already established what his resources were.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:49

> **who fear their Lord unseen, and who are wary of the Hour.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fearing the Lord Unseen**

*Who fear their Lord unseen.* The verse continues the description begun at verse 48, specifying who the reverent are. The word rendered *unseen* is *ghāʾib*, which the commentators note literally means absent or unseen, and which is related to *ghayb*, the unseen reality.

Al-Rāzī records one reading: the reverent are absent from other people and pray to God in seclusion. On this reading *unseen* describes the circumstance of the worship — no audience, no social reinforcement, nothing to be gained in reputation. The fear is what remains when the audience is removed.

Al-Qurṭubī offers a second reading: the worshippers are not present before God and the realities of the Hereafter, meaning they cannot perceive God directly but know Him through faith, reflection, and contemplation. Al-Rāzī adds that this knowledge keeps them from disobedience. The two readings are preserved together.

**The Two Readings and What They Share**

The readings differ in what *unseen* modifies — the worshipper's audience or the worshipper's object — but they converge on the same point. In both, the fear of God operates without direct perception: without other people watching, or without seeing God. What sustains the fear in either case is not sight.

This is what makes the verse a fitting description of the reverent. A fear that depends on an audience is a fear of the audience; a fear that depends on seeing is a fear of what is seen. The verse describes a fear that persists in the absence of both, and the persistence is what qualifies it as reverence.

The commentators' cross-reference to *ghayb* supports this. The unseen is a central Qur'anic category, and belief in it is repeatedly presented as the distinguishing mark of the reverent. The verse uses the same root to describe the circumstance of their fear, tying the two together.

**Wary of the Hour**

*And who are wary of the Hour.* The second clause completes the description, and the word rendered *wary* is the same the sūrah used at verse 28 of the angels — *They are wary, for fear of Him.* The echo is deliberate and places the reverent in the same posture as the highest created beings.

The wariness connects to the eschatological material of verses 38 to 47. The Hour was described there as sudden, inescapable, and without respite; the reckoning was described as exact down to the mustard seed. Wariness is the disposition those descriptions require, and the verse names it.

The connection to verse 28 is theologically significant. The angels' wariness was offered there as evidence that they are creatures rather than divine beings. The reverent's wariness here is evidence of the same recognition — that they are accountable and that the account is coming.

**The Verse as Definition**

Verse 49 functions as a definition of the term used at verse 48. The reminder was said to be *for the reverent*; this verse states who the reverent are. The definition has two clauses — fearing the Lord unseen, and wariness of the Hour — and together they cover the present and the future.

The first clause concerns the worshipper's current relation to God; the second concerns his expectation of the reckoning. Both are required, and the verse's brevity reflects the fact that the two belong together: a fear of God without expectation of the Hour would lack urgency, and expectation of the Hour without fear of God would lack content.

The definition also answers verse 45's description of the deaf. Those who hear and do not act are contrasted with those who fear without seeing and are wary without knowing the time. The contrast is between two dispositions toward the same revelation, and the verse identifies the one that functions.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

Verses 48 and 49 form a unit that closes the eschatological passage and opens the historical one. Verse 47 described the reckoning; verse 48 described what was given to Moses and Aaron; verse 49 describes who benefits from what was given. The three verses move from assessment to provision to recipient.

The unit also prepares the account of Moses that follows. Having described the gifts and the disposition required to use them, the sūrah turns to the mission in which they were employed. The order is deliberate: the reader is told what Moses was given and what is required of those who receive it before being told what he did.

This structure is characteristic of the sūrah. It does not narrate for its own sake but establishes the conditions under which the narrative is to be read. The account of Moses will be an account of a man given the Criterion, the light, and the reminder, addressing people whose response verse 45 has already characterised.

**The Description and the Reader**

The verse's description is available to the reader as a standard. To fear the Lord unseen and to be wary of the Hour are not achievements reserved for prophets; they are dispositions the verse presents as definitional of reverence. The reader is thereby invited to locate himself in relation to them.

The first clause is the more demanding. Fear in seclusion requires that the fear be genuine rather than performed, and the verse's use of *unseen* makes the test explicit. What a person does when nobody is watching is the measure of whether the fear is of God or of the audience.

The second clause supplies the urgency. Wariness of the Hour is not a mood but a settled expectation, and verses 38 to 47 have supplied its grounds: the coming is sudden, there is no respite, and the reckoning omits nothing. The verse names the disposition those grounds require without exhorting the reader to adopt it, and the absence of exhortation is itself a feature of the description: what is being defined is a state, not a task, and a state is recognised rather than undertaken.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:50

> **This is a blessed Reminder that We have sent down. Will you then deny it?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Blessed Reminder**

*This is a blessed Reminder that We have sent down.* The commentators note that the *Reminder* — *dhikr* — refers here to the Qur'an, and that the term is used several times throughout this sūrah in the sense of a Divine book, at verses 2, 7, 10, 24, 48, 84, and 105. The recurrence makes this verse a summary of what the sūrah has been saying about its own nature.

The adjective *blessed* — *mubārak* — denotes something whose benefit extends and increases. The verse does not say that the Reminder is true or authoritative, though both are implied elsewhere; it says that it is a source of blessing, which is a claim about its effect rather than its status.

The clause *that We have sent down* establishes the source without argument. The sūrah has already made the case for its origin at verse 45, where the Prophet ﷺ was told to say that he warns only through a revelation. This verse states the same fact declaratively rather than defensively.

**The Question That Follows**

*Will you then deny it?* The question is short and expects no answer, because there is none available. The verse has described the Reminder as blessed and sent down; the question asks whether the audience will deny what has been so described.

The particle *then* — *fa-* — indicates consequence. The denial is presented not as a possibility among others but as the illogical alternative to what has just been stated. The verse does not argue against denial; it asks whether denial is coherent given the description.

This is the sūrah's method in its most compressed form. A description, then a question that exposes the gap between the description and the audience's position. The same structure appeared at verse 30 — *Will they not, then, believe?* — and at verse 44 — *Is it they who shall prevail?*

**The Verse as a Hinge**

Verse 50 closes the passage that began at verse 38 and opens the historical section that runs to the end of the sūrah. Its position is therefore structural: it summarises what has been said about the revelation and prepares the accounts of the messengers that follow.

The summarising function is evident in the word *dhikr* itself, which the sūrah has used seven times by this point. The verse gathers those uses into a single statement and then asks the question they collectively pose.

The transitional function is equally clear. Verse 48 named Moses and Aaron and the gifts given to them; verse 49 described the reverent who benefit; verse 50 describes the Reminder itself; verse 51 turns to Abraham. The sequence moves from the general to the particular, and this verse is the point of turning.

**Blessing as a Category**

The choice of *blessed* rather than a term denoting truth or authority is worth attending to. The sūrah could have said *this is a true Reminder* or *an authoritative Reminder*; it says *a blessed Reminder*, which locates the Reminder's significance in what it does rather than in what it is.

The distinction matters for the question that follows. A claim about truth invites dispute about evidence; a claim about blessing invites observation of effect. The verse's question — *Will you then deny it?* — is therefore not asking whether the audience accepts a proposition but whether they will refuse a benefit.

This is consistent with the sūrah's method throughout. It appeals to observation rather than to authority, at verse 30 with water, at verse 32 with the sky, at verse 44 with the reduction of the frontiers. The blessing of the Reminder is offered on the same terms.

**The Verse and Verse 24**

At verse 24 the Prophet ﷺ was told to say: *This is the Reminder for those who are with me, and the Reminder for those before me.* Verse 50 returns to the same word and makes a further claim about it — that it is blessed and sent down. The two verses together describe the Reminder's continuity and its character.

The connection answers an objection the sūrah has been addressing. If the Reminder is continuous with what preceded, it is not an innovation; if it is blessed and sent down, it is not a human composition. Both claims are needed, and the sūrah makes them at different points rather than together.

The recurrence of *dhikr* across the sūrah — at verses 2, 7, 10, 24, 48, 84, and 105 — makes this verse the natural place for a summary. The commentators' note listing those instances indicates that they read the term as a sustained thread rather than as an occasional synonym.

**What the Verse Asks**

The verse's question is addressed to the audience, and it asks about their response rather than their belief. *Will you then deny it?* concerns an act, not a state, and the act is one the audience is in a position to perform or refrain from.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the opponents throughout. They are not described as incapable of belief but as turning away, not hearing, and denying. Each description locates the obstacle in the response rather than in the capacity, which preserves responsibility.

The verse therefore leaves the matter with the audience. It describes the Reminder, states its source, and asks the question. Nothing further is added, and the absence of further argument is the verse's confidence: the description is sufficient, and what remains is the response.

---

**The Reminder and the Accounts That Follow**

The verse's description of the Reminder as blessed and sent down is what licenses the historical section that follows. The accounts of Abraham, Lot, David, Solomon, Job, and the rest are not illustrations appended to the revelation; they are part of what was sent down, and their presence is one of the ways the blessing is delivered.

The commentators' list of the sūrah's uses of *dhikr* — at verses 2, 7, 10, 24, 48, 84, and 105 — shows the term spanning the whole text. This verse gathers those uses into a single claim, and the claim explains why the sūrah can move from eschatology to narrative without announcement: both are the same Reminder.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:51

> **And We indeed gave unto Abraham his sound judgment aforetime, and We knew him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sound Judgment Given Aforetime**

*And We indeed gave unto Abraham his sound judgment aforetime, and We knew him.* The word rendered *sound judgment* is *rushd*, which the commentators discuss at 2:256 and 72:2. Al-Ṭabarī explains that *aforetime* means before Moses and Aaron, which places Abraham in the sequence the sūrah has been establishing.

The placement is significant. Verse 48 named Moses and Aaron and the gifts given to them; verse 51 goes behind them to Abraham. The sūrah is tracing the line of messengers backwards, and the movement establishes that the gifts are not recent provisions but have accompanied the office from its earlier instances.

The phrase *We indeed gave* is emphatic, and the emphasis falls on the giving rather than on the recipient. The sound judgment is presented as conferred rather than acquired, which is the same point the sūrah made at verse 48 about the Criterion and the light.

**The Commentators' Readings of "Aforetime"**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that some take *aforetime* to mean Abraham was rightly guided even as a child. Al-Qurṭubī adds that this was before he was endowed with the office of prophethood. Al-Rāzī notes further readings: that it refers to prophethood itself, or to the fact that God knew He would make Abraham a prophet.

The plurality of readings is instructive. The commentators do not settle on one because the phrase supports several, and each has a different implication for when Abraham's guidance began. The child reading makes the guidance precede prophethood; the prophethood reading identifies them.

What the readings share is that the guidance was given rather than earned. Whether it came in childhood or with the office, it is presented as conferred by God, and the verse's emphasis on *We gave* supports all the readings equally.

**"And We Knew Him"**

The closing clause is brief and has attracted considerable comment. *And We knew him* — *wa-kunnā bihi ʿālimīn* — states God's knowledge of Abraham without specifying its content or its consequence.

Al-Rāzī's reading that the phrase refers to God's knowing He would make Abraham a prophet gives the clause a forward reference: the knowledge concerns what Abraham will become. On this reading the clause explains the giving — the sound judgment was conferred because God knew what Abraham would do with it.

The alternative is to read the clause as a simple statement of divine knowledge, parallel to verse 28's *He knows that which is before them and that which is behind them*. On this reading the clause places Abraham within God's knowledge, as the angels were placed there, and the placing is what qualifies him.

**Why Abraham Comes Here**

The sūrah turns to Abraham after summarising the Reminder at verse 50, and the turn is purposeful. Abraham is the figure the sūrah needs for the argument that follows: he confronted his father and his people over their idols, and the confrontation is the pattern the Prophet ﷺ is living.

The commentators note that Abraham's encounter with his father and his people is recounted at 6:76–83, 19:42–48, 26:69–87, 37:84–96, and 43:26–28. The recurrence across five other sūrahs indicates that this is a central Qur'anic narrative, and its appearance here is not incidental.

The verse's function is to establish Abraham's qualification before recounting his action. He was given sound judgment; God knew him. What follows is therefore not the report of an opinion but the exercise of a conferred capacity, and the qualification is what gives the confrontation its weight.

**Rushd as a Category**

The term *rushd* denotes sound judgment, right guidance, the capacity to discern and act correctly. The commentators' cross-references to 2:256 — *there is no compulsion in religion, sound judgment is distinct from error* — and 72:2 indicate that it is a technical term in the Qur'an rather than a general word for intelligence.

The distinction between *rushd* and mere knowledge is important. Sound judgment is knowledge applied correctly, and the verse's claim is that Abraham was given the capacity to apply what he knew. This is what the confrontation in the following verses exhibits.

The connection to 2:256 is particularly apt. That verse pairs sound judgment with the absence of compulsion, and Abraham's confrontation with his people is conducted entirely by argument. He does not compel; he asks, and the asking is the exercise of the judgment he was given.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 51 contributes to the sūrah's central argument by supplying a second instance of the pattern asserted at verse 25. There the claim was that every messenger before the Prophet ﷺ was given the same message; here a specific messenger is named and the gift he received is described.

The instance is chosen well. Abraham is the figure whose creed Islam presents itself as renewing, and the commentators note elsewhere in the Qur'an that Muslims are enjoined to follow the creed of Abraham. Naming him establishes continuity with the most authoritative precedent available.

The verse also prepares the confrontation that follows. Having established that Abraham was given sound judgment and known by God, the sūrah can recount his challenge to his people as the exercise of a conferred capacity rather than as personal rebellion, which is how his people will characterise it.

---

**The Gift and the Task**

The verse pairs the gift with the knowledge: sound judgment was given, and God knew him. The pairing indicates that the gift was not distributed indiscriminately but conferred on one known to the Giver, and the knowledge is what makes the conferral purposeful rather than arbitrary.

What follows confirms the pairing. Abraham's challenge to his father and his people is conducted entirely by question, without compulsion, and the conduct is the exercise of the judgment he was given. The verse establishes the capacity; the narrative exhibits it in use.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:52

> **when he said unto his father and his people, "What are these images to which you are cleaving?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question to His Father and His People**

*When he said unto his father and his people, "What are these images to which you are cleaving?"* The verse places Abraham's challenge at the outset of his mission, addressed jointly to his father and his people. The joint address is significant: the confrontation is not a private family matter but a public challenge to a communal practice.

The word rendered *images* — *tamāthīl* — denotes statues or representations. Abraham's question does not deny that they are images; it asks what they are, which is a question about their significance rather than their existence. The question is framed so that no answer is available.

The verb rendered *cleaving* — *ʿākifūn* — denotes devoted attendance, the posture of one who remains fixed in devotion. The word choice identifies the practice as worship rather than mere respect, and Abraham's question is therefore directed at the devotion rather than at the objects.

**The Form of the Question**

Abraham does not say *these images are false* or *you are wrong*. He asks *What are these images to which you are cleaving?* The interrogative form places the burden of explanation on his audience, and the burden is one they cannot discharge.

This is the same method the sūrah used at verse 24, where the Prophet ﷺ was told to say *Bring me your proof!* In both cases the challenge takes the form of a demand for justification rather than an assertion of falsehood. The method is more effective because it does not give the audience anything to rebut.

The question also has a diagnostic function. By asking what the images are, Abraham invites his people to state what they believe about them, and their answer in the following verse — *We found our fathers worshipping them* — reveals that they have no account of the objects at all.

**The Parallel Accounts**

The commentators note that Abraham's encounter with his father and his people is recounted at 6:76–83, 19:42–48, 26:69–87, 37:84–96, and 43:26–28. The recurrence across five other sūrahs indicates that this is one of the Qur'an's most frequently told narratives, and each account has its own emphasis.

The present account is the most compressed. It gives the question, the answer, the judgment, and the response in a few verses, whereas 6:76–83 gives Abraham's reasoning at length. The compression serves the sūrah's purpose here, which is to establish the pattern rather than to narrate the argument.

The recurrence also serves the sūrah's argument about continuity. If the same confrontation appears in multiple sūrahs, the audience cannot treat it as a local or novel claim. The narrative is part of the shared scriptural heritage, and its repetition establishes that.

**The Charge of Following the Fathers**

The commentators direct the reader to 5:104 on the notion of idolaters blindly following the ways of their forefathers: *Sufficient for us is that which we have found our fathers practicing. What! Even if their fathers knew naught and were not rightly guided?* The cross-reference identifies the answer Abraham will receive as a standard position.

Similar injunctions are addressed to the Children of Adam at 7:28, to the Egyptians at 10:78 and 28:36, and to people in general at 31:21 and 43:24. The list indicates that the appeal to ancestral practice is treated throughout the Qur'an as a distinct and recurring error, not as a local failing.

The error is identified precisely in the 5:104 cross-reference: the appeal to the fathers is offered as sufficient without any examination of whether the fathers knew anything. The sufficiency is claimed for the fact of inheritance rather than for the content inherited.

**Why the Father Is Named**

The verse specifies that Abraham addressed his father as well as his people, and the specification raises the stakes. A challenge to a community is a public dispute; a challenge that includes one's father is a rupture with the most immediate form of inheritance.

The sūrah's other accounts of this confrontation, particularly 19:42–48, develop the exchange between Abraham and his father at length. The present verse's joint address compresses that into a single clause, but the compression retains the point: the challenge reaches the nearest relation.

This is why the answer in the following verse is so revealing. When the people respond *We found our fathers worshipping them*, they are appealing to the very authority Abraham has just challenged in person. The answer invokes the father while Abraham stands before his own.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The account functions as a parallel to the Prophet's own situation, and the sūrah has prepared the parallel throughout. Verse 36 described the mockery he receives; verse 41 placed it in the pattern of earlier messengers; verse 51 introduced Abraham as one who was given sound judgment.

The confrontation Abraham opens here is the one the Prophet ﷺ is conducting. Both address a community attached to ancestral practice; both are met with the appeal to the fathers; both are accused of jesting, as verse 55 will record. The sūrah does not state the parallel, but the sequence makes it available.

The function is consolatory in the same way verse 41 was. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the confrontation he is conducting has a precedent, that the precedent was conducted by one given sound judgment and known by God, and that the response he is receiving is the response the precedent received.

---

**The Question and the Practice**

Abraham's question is directed at a practice rather than at a doctrine. He does not ask what his people believe about the images but what the images are, and the form of the question identifies the devotion as the thing requiring explanation. A practice sustained without an account is what he is asking about.

The commentators' cross-references — 5:104 on following the fathers, and the similar injunctions at 7:28, 10:78, 28:36, 31:21, and 43:24 — show the Qur'an treating this as a recurring condition rather than a local failing. The question is designed to expose the condition wherever it appears.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:53

> **They said, "We found our fathers worshipping them."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer**

*They said, "We found our fathers worshipping them."* The answer is the verse's whole content, and its brevity is the point. Abraham asked what the images are; the answer does not describe them but reports an inheritance.

The answer is not a claim about the images' nature, power, or origin. It is a statement about how the practice came to be held: it was found, already in place, among the fathers. The verse records the answer without comment, and the absence of comment is the commentary.

The commentators' cross-reference to 5:104 identifies the answer as a standard formula: *Sufficient for us is that which we have found our fathers practicing.* The parallel shows that the response is not specific to Abraham's people but is the characteristic reply to prophetic challenge.

**What the Answer Concedes**

The answer concedes the point Abraham was making. He asked what the images are; the reply supplies no account of them. The absence of an account is itself the answer to his question, and the verse lets the absence stand.

The commentators' note on 5:104 makes the concession explicit: *What! Even if their fathers knew naught and were not rightly guided?* The appeal to the fathers assumes the fathers knew, and the assumption is not examined. Abraham's question was designed to expose precisely this.

The verse's economy is therefore argumentative. By recording the answer in a single clause and adding nothing, it shows that there is nothing to add. A longer treatment would have supplied the analysis; the verse leaves the analysis to be performed by the reader on the answer as it stands.

**Inheritance as Justification**

The answer treats inheritance as sufficient justification. The commentators note that similar injunctions against this position are addressed to the Children of Adam at 7:28, to the Egyptians at 10:78 and 28:36, and to people in general at 31:21 and 43:24. The recurrence indicates that the Qur'an treats the appeal to ancestry as a distinct error requiring repeated address.

The error is not that the fathers are invoked but that their practice is taken as establishing its own correctness. An inherited practice may be sound or unsound; the fact of inheritance does not determine which. The answer offers the fact as though it determined the question.

This connects to verse 24's demand for proof. There the opponents were asked to bring their demonstration and could not; here Abraham's people are asked what the images are and offer an inheritance. In both cases the response substitutes something other than the justification requested.

**The Verse and Verse 52**

Verses 52 and 53 form a single exchange and are best read together. The question asks for an account of the objects of devotion; the answer supplies an account of the devotion's history. The mismatch between question and answer is the exchange's content.

The mismatch is what allows Abraham's judgment in the following verse. He does not need to refute an argument about the images because no argument was offered. His declaration that they are in manifest error rests on the absence of any account, which the answer has just supplied.

The sūrah's method here is economical. Two short verses establish the entire epistemic situation: a question that cannot be answered and an answer that does not address it. The judgment that follows requires no further support.

**The Answer and the Father**

There is an irony in the answer that the verse leaves unstated. Abraham addressed his father along with his people, and the people respond by appealing to their fathers. The authority they invoke is the authority Abraham has just challenged in the person of his own father.

The irony is not merely rhetorical. It exposes the appeal to the fathers as generic rather than examined: the respondents are not defending particular fathers on particular grounds but invoking fatherhood as a category. Abraham's presence before his own father makes the generic character visible.

The commentators do not draw this out, but the juxtaposition is the verse's own doing. By placing the joint address in the preceding verse and the appeal to the fathers in this one, the sūrah sets the two side by side and leaves the comparison to the reader.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes the epistemic position of Abraham's people, and the position is the one the sūrah has attributed to the Prophet's opponents throughout. At verse 24 they could not bring proof; at verse 36 they reduced criticism to mention; at verse 38 they demanded a date. Each response substitutes something for justification.

The parallel is what gives the narrative its function. The account of Abraham is not included for its own sake but to exhibit a pattern that the audience can recognise in its own conduct. The verse's single clause is sufficient because the audience supplies the recognition.

This is the sūrah's consistent method: state the case briefly and let the reader make the application. Verse 53 does not say *so it is with you*; it records the answer and moves on, and the application is available to anyone who has been asked what he believes and has answered with an inheritance.

---

**The Answer as a Diagnostic**

The single clause records everything needed to assess the position. No account of the images is offered, no claim about their power is made, no reason is given for the devotion beyond the fact that it was found already in place. The verse's brevity is not economy but sufficiency.

Read against verse 24, where the opponents were asked to bring their proof, the answer is the same response in a different setting. A demonstration was requested and an inheritance was offered. The sūrah records both without comment, and the recording is the commentary — a reader who supplies the account himself reaches the conclusion without being led to it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:54

> **He said, "Certainly you and your fathers have been in manifest error."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Judgment**

*He said, "Certainly you and your fathers have been in manifest error."* Abraham's response is a declaration rather than an argument, and the declaration is warranted by the exchange that precedes it. He asked for an account; none was given; the judgment follows from the absence.

The word rendered *manifest* — *mubīn* — denotes what is clear, evident, open to inspection. The error is not described as hidden or debatable but as plain, and the plainness is what the preceding exchange established. An inherited practice with no account behind it is visibly without foundation.

The emphasis particle — *la-qad* — conveys certainty. Abraham is not offering an opinion but stating a conclusion he takes to be established, and the certainty is appropriate given that his question has gone unanswered.

**Including the Fathers**

The judgment includes the fathers as well as the sons, and the inclusion is the verse's most consequential feature. The appeal to the fathers was the people's justification; Abraham's response denies that the fathers' practice establishes anything, by declaring them in error too.

This directly answers the appeal recorded at verse 53. If the fathers were in manifest error, then finding them worshipping the images establishes nothing about the images. The judgment removes the ground of the answer rather than disputing its accuracy.

The commentators' cross-reference to 5:104 makes the same point: *What! Even if their fathers knew naught and were not rightly guided?* The question there is the judgment here, and both identify the appeal to ancestry as resting on an unexamined assumption.

**Why No Argument Is Given**

Abraham does not argue for the judgment, and the absence of argument is deliberate. He has already asked the question that would elicit an argument, and the answer supplied none. To argue further would be to grant that there is something to argue about.

This is the same method the sūrah used at verse 24, where the demand for proof was issued and not followed by a counter-argument. In both cases the party making the positive claim is asked to support it, and the failure to do so settles the matter.

The method is more effective than argument because it does not elevate the position being rejected. An argument implies that the opposing view has content worth engaging; a declaration following an unanswered question implies that it does not.

**The Judgment and Its Risk**

The declaration is not safe. Abraham is telling his father and his community that they are in manifest error, and the response recorded in the following verse — *Have you brought us the truth, or are you among those who jest?* — indicates that it was received as either insolence or absurdity.

The sūrah does not soften the judgment or explain it away. It records the declaration in its full directness and then records the response, and the juxtaposition shows what the judgment cost. This is the pattern verse 41 described: messengers are mocked, and the mockery follows the declaration.

The risk is what makes the declaration meaningful. A judgment that costs nothing is not a judgment but an observation, and the sūrah's insistence on recording the cost is part of how it establishes the declaration's weight. Abraham's declaration places him against his father and his people, and the sūrah records the placement without comment, leaving its cost to be seen rather than stated.

**The Verse and the Parallel Accounts**

The commentators note that this encounter is recounted at 6:76–83, 19:42–48, 26:69–87, 37:84–96, and 43:26–28. In those accounts Abraham's reasoning is given at length — the observation of the star, the moon, and the sun at 6:76–79, the destruction of the idols at 37:91–96. The present account omits the reasoning entirely.

The omission is a choice about what this sūrah needs. It requires the pattern — question, appeal to the fathers, judgment, response — not the argument. The other accounts supply the argument for readers who need it; this one supplies the shape of the confrontation.

The choice serves the sūrah's purpose, which is to console the Prophet ﷺ by showing the pattern rather than to instruct by reproducing the reasoning. Verse 41 established that messengers are mocked; this account shows the exchange that produces the mockery.

**The Judgment and the Reader**

The verse's application to the reader is direct. Anyone who holds a practice because his fathers held it is in the position of Abraham's people, and Abraham's judgment applies to him as it applied to them. The verse does not make the application explicit, but it does not need to.

The application is not that ancestral practice is always wrong. It is that ancestral practice is not self-justifying — that the fact of inheritance does not establish the soundness of what is inherited. The judgment of manifest error follows from the absence of an account, not from the fact of inheritance.

This is why the exchange is recorded at this length. The two verses establish the epistemic situation completely, and the judgment follows from it. A reader who supplies an account of his practice is not in the position the judgment addresses; a reader who cannot is.

---

**The Declaration and What Supports It**

Abraham's judgment rests on the exchange rather than on an argument, and the exchange is complete: a question was asked, an appeal to the fathers was offered in reply, and no account of the objects was given. The declaration of manifest error follows from that sequence and requires nothing further.

The inclusion of the fathers is what makes the judgment effective. It removes the ground of the answer rather than disputing the answer's accuracy, and the removal is complete: if the fathers were in error, their practice establishes nothing about the images, and the appeal collapses without any further step being required.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:55

> **They said, "Have you brought us the truth, or are you among those who jest?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Truth or Jest**

*They said, "Have you brought us the truth, or are you among those who jest?"* The response offers two alternatives, and both are dismissive. If Abraham is speaking the truth, the claim is too large to be credited; if he is jesting, the exchange is over. The question is framed so that neither answer allows the challenge to proceed.

The alternative between truth and jest is not a genuine dichotomy. It excludes the possibility that Abraham is speaking truly *and* is to be taken seriously — which is what he claims. The question therefore does not seek clarification but offers an escape from the judgment he has just delivered.

The commentators note the parallel to the Israelites' response to Moses at 2:67: *And when Moses said to his people, "God commands you to slaughter a cow," they said, "Do you take us in mockery?"* The recurrence indicates a standard response to prophetic commands.

**The Parallel With Moses**

The cross-reference to 2:67 is instructive about the response's character. The Israelites were not accused of idolatry; they were given a command, and their response was to ask whether Moses was mocking them. The parallel shows that the response is not specific to the content of the challenge.

In both cases the response treats a serious communication as potentially frivolous, and the treatment is a way of avoiding it. If the communication might be a jest, it need not be answered; the question about jesting therefore functions as a refusal dressed as an inquiry.

The sūrah's placement of this parallel is significant. It has just recounted Abraham's confrontation and is about to continue with further accounts of the messengers. The parallel to Moses links the two narratives and establishes the response as a pattern rather than an incident.

**What the Question Reveals**

The question reveals that Abraham's judgment has not been answered. He declared that they and their fathers were in manifest error; they did not dispute the declaration but asked whether he was serious. The absence of a substantive response is the verse's implicit content.

This continues the pattern established at verses 52 and 53. Abraham asked what the images are; no account was given. He declared the error; the declaration was not addressed. Each exchange shows the same thing — that there is nothing on the other side to engage with.

The sūrah's method is consistent: record the exchange and let the absence speak. The verse does not say *they had no answer*; it records a question that functions as one, and the function is visible in the question's form.

**Jesting as a Category**

The accusation of jesting is worth examining. To jest is to speak without commitment, and the accusation implies that Abraham's words carry no weight. The implication is not that he is lying but that he is not serious, which is a different and in some ways more dismissive charge.

The charge is available because the judgment is large. To tell one's father and one's community that they are in manifest error is either the most serious thing that can be said or a joke; the respondents cannot occupy the middle ground, because the middle ground would require them to consider the claim.

The dichotomy therefore protects them. If the claim is serious it must be faced, and facing it would require the account they could not give at verse 53. Treating it as possibly a jest avoids the requirement, and the question is the device by which the avoidance is managed.

**The Verse and Verse 36**

The response parallels the mockery recorded at verse 36, where the disbelievers took the Prophet ﷺ in naught but mockery and asked *Is this the one who makes mention of your gods?* Both responses reduce a challenge to a social offence and both use a question to do it.

The parallel is what the sūrah intends. Verse 41 established that messengers were mocked before the Prophet ﷺ; the account of Abraham supplies the instance. The reader is shown the pattern operating in a narrative he recognises, and the recognition transfers to his own situation.

The function is consolatory in the way verse 41 was. The Prophet ﷺ is not being told that the mockery will stop but that it is the standard response to the standard challenge, and that the challenge is not thereby undermined. Abraham's judgment stands despite the question about jesting.

**What Follows From the Question**

The verse leaves the question unanswered, and the absence of an answer is significant. Abraham does not say *I am serious*; the narrative moves on to the destruction of the idols, which answers the question by action rather than by assertion.

This is characteristic of the sūrah's method. Where a question is posed that cannot be answered, it is not answered; where a challenge requires support, the support is supplied by what happens next. The account of Abraham will continue with the breaking of the idols, which is the demonstration the question implicitly demands.

The structure is therefore not incomplete. The question is recorded, and the answer follows in the narrative rather than in the dialogue. The sūrah's compression here is a choice about where the answer belongs, and the choice places it in the action rather than in the exchange.

---

**The Question and the Narrative's Answer**

The respondents' alternative between truth and jest is not answered in dialogue, and the omission is deliberate. The narrative continues with what Abraham does, and the doing answers the question in the only register that can settle it. A claim about seriousness is demonstrated rather than asserted.

The parallel to 2:67 reinforces the point. The Israelites asked whether Moses was mocking them when he gave a command; the command stood regardless of the question. Abraham's judgment stands regardless of this question, and the sūrah's refusal to record a reply indicates that none was needed.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:56

> **He said, "Nay, but your Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, Who originated them. And I am among those who bear witness unto this."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lord of the Heavens and the Earth**

*He said, "Nay, but your Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, Who originated them."* Abraham's reply does not dispute his people's account of the idols; it supplies the account they failed to give. Where verse 53 recorded an inheritance, this verse states an origin, and the substitution is the whole of the argument.

The commentators note that Abraham points out that it is not the idols who originated the heavens and the earth. The observation is minimal and decisive: whatever else the idols may be credited with, origination is not among their capacities, and origination is what the term *Lord* denotes.

The title *Lord of the heavens and the earth* echoes verse 22, where the sūrah argued that if there were gods other than God in the heavens and the earth, both would have been corrupted. Abraham's declaration applies that argument to his own case, and the application requires no further support.

**Who Originated Them**

The relative clause — *Who originated them* — is the verse's evidential core. It identifies the criterion by which lordship is to be assigned, and the criterion is one the idols demonstrably fail. A thing that was made cannot be the maker of what contains it.

The verb *faṭara* — to originate, to bring into being from nothing — is used elsewhere in the Qur'an for the act of creation, and its use here connects Abraham's argument to the sūrah's treatment of creation at verses 30 and 31. The origination of the heavens and the earth is the fact on which both arguments rest.

The verse therefore does not introduce new evidence. It applies evidence the sūrah has already established — the separation of the heavens and the earth, the dependence of living things on water — to the question of who is to be worshipped, and the application settles it.

**"I Am Among Those Who Bear Witness"**

The closing clause places Abraham within a class: *And I am among those who bear witness unto this.* He does not claim to be the first or the only witness but one among others, and the phrasing indicates that the testimony has been given before.

The clause answers the appeal to the fathers recorded at verse 53. His people invoked the practice of their ancestors; Abraham invokes the testimony of those who have borne witness to the origination. The two appeals are set against each other, and the second has content where the first had only inheritance.

The modesty of the formulation is worth noting. Abraham does not say *I alone know this* but *I am among those who bear witness*, which locates him in a tradition of testimony rather than in a position of private insight. The sūrah has been establishing exactly such a tradition since verse 25.

**The Structure of the Argument**

The verse has three movements: the denial, the positive claim, and the testimony. *Nay* rejects the position; *your Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, Who originated them* states the alternative; *I am among those who bear witness* commits the speaker to it. Each movement is necessary and none is elaborated.

The compression is characteristic of the sūrah's treatment of Abraham. Unlike 6:76–83, which gives his reasoning at length through the star, the moon, and the sun, this account moves directly to the conclusion. The sūrah requires the shape of the argument rather than its development.

The choice serves the sūrah's purpose, which is to exhibit the pattern of prophetic confrontation rather than to instruct in its method. Readers who need the reasoning have it elsewhere; this account supplies the conclusion and the commitment.

**The Verse and Verse 21**

Abraham's declaration applies the test the sūrah posed at verse 21: *Or have they taken gods from the earth who resurrect?* The idols were taken from the earth and cannot give life; the Lord of the heavens and the earth originated them. The two verses make the same argument across a gap of thirty-five others.

The connection is what gives the Abrahamic narrative its function in this sūrah. The account is not included as a story but as an instance of the argument the sūrah has been making since its opening section, and the instance is chosen because it is the most authoritative precedent available.

The commentators' note that Islam presents itself as the renewal of Abraham's creed supports this reading. The narrative is doing theological work, and the work is to show that the demand the Prophet ﷺ is making is the demand Abraham made.

**Bearing Witness as a Category**

The verse's use of testimony is significant. Abraham does not claim knowledge in the abstract but commits himself as a witness, and testimony is a public act with consequences. The following verses will show those consequences: the breaking of the idols, the accusation, and the demand that he be brought before the people.

The connection between testimony and risk is what distinguishes it from opinion. An opinion can be held privately; testimony is given before others and exposes the one who gives it. Abraham's declaration is therefore not merely a statement of belief but an act with a social cost, and the cost is recorded in the narrative that follows.

This is the same structure the sūrah described at verse 54, where Abraham's judgment was noted to carry risk. The testimony here continues that pattern: each declaration places him further outside his community, and the sūrah records the placements without softening them.

**What the Verse Leaves Open**

The verse states the conclusion and the commitment but does not say what Abraham will do about the idols. The following verse supplies that: *And by God, I shall scheme against your idols after you have turned your backs.* The sequence moves from declaration to action, and the action is announced rather than concealed.

The announcement is significant. Abraham does not plan in secret; he states his intention, and the commentators note that he says it to himself. The distinction matters: the plan is not a conspiracy against his people but a resolve concerning the objects of their worship, and it is formed after the verbal argument has failed.

The verse therefore closes the argumentative phase of the account. The question was asked, the appeal to the fathers was given, the judgment was declared, and the positive claim has now been stated. What follows is demonstration rather than discussion, and the verse marks the transition.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:57

> **And by God, I shall scheme against your idols after you have turned your backs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath and the Scheme**

*And by God, I shall scheme against your idols after you have turned your backs.* The commentators record that al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī read the verse as Abraham speaking to himself: *I shall scheme against you concerning your idols.* The address is internal, and the reading affects how the verse is understood.

The oath — *by God* — indicates that what follows is undertaken deliberately and with a sense of obligation. This is not an impulsive act but a resolution sworn before God, and the oath distinguishes it from the destruction of property for its own sake.

The phrase *after you have turned your backs* specifies the timing. The scheme will be executed when the people are absent, which indicates that the demonstration is directed at the idols rather than at a confrontation with the worshippers. The absence is a condition of the demonstration, not an evasion.

**A Different Mode of Demonstration**

Al-Qurṭubī's note identifies the verse's function precisely: after having tried verbal argument, Abraham was now planning to use a different mode of demonstration. The sequence is important — argument first, then demonstration — and the demonstration is not a substitute for argument but a supplement to it.

The verbal argument has been recorded in the preceding verses. The question was asked at verse 52, the appeal to the fathers was given at verse 53, the judgment was declared at verse 54, and the positive claim was stated at verse 56. The argument has been conducted completely and has produced no response.

The demonstration therefore addresses what argument could not reach. A people who answer a question about their gods with an inheritance are not engaged in reasoning, and a demonstration is addressed to a different faculty. The commentators' note identifies this shift explicitly.

**Scheme as a Term**

The word rendered *scheme* — *akīda* — carries connotations of planning and contrivance, and its use by a prophet requires explanation. The commentators' glosses supply it: the planning concerns the idols, and its object is demonstration rather than harm.

The term is appropriate to the action described. What Abraham intends is not destruction but a setup — the breaking of the idols and the leaving of the largest, which verse 58 will describe. A setup is a scheme in the literal sense, and the verse's word choice anticipates the method.

The commentators' reading that he speaks to himself supports this. A scheme spoken aloud to the intended victims would be a threat; a scheme spoken to oneself is a plan. The verse records the plan, and the plan is what the following verses execute.

**Why the Idols Rather Than the People**

The verse specifies that the scheme concerns the idols, and the specification is theologically significant. Abraham's confrontation is with the objects of worship, not with the worshippers. He does not plan to harm his people but to demonstrate something about their gods.

This distinguishes the account from a narrative of conflict. The demonstration is designed to be legible: when the people return and find the idols broken and the largest intact, they will be confronted with a question about the gods' capacity. The demonstration is addressed to their understanding rather than to their persons.

The commentators' note that he lagged behind on a festival day supports this reading. The timing ensures the people are absent, which means the demonstration will be discovered rather than witnessed, and discovery invites inference in a way that witnessing does not.

**The Verse and Verse 56**

Verses 56 and 57 form a unit: the declaration and the resolution. Having stated who the Lord is and committed himself as a witness, Abraham resolves to act on the commitment. The sequence shows testimony as something that issues in action rather than remaining in statement.

The connection is causal as well as sequential. The declaration establishes that the idols did not originate the heavens and the earth; the resolution undertakes to demonstrate that they cannot act at all. The demonstration will test the claim the declaration made.

The sūrah's method here is to let the narrative carry the argument. The declaration at verse 56 states the proposition; the actions at verses 58 and following exhibit it. The reader is shown the demonstration rather than told its conclusion.

**The Oath and Its Weight**

The oath by God is not decorative. It places the intended action under Divine witness, which means the action is undertaken as obedience rather than as initiative. The commentators' readings converge on this: the scheme is a plan concerning the idols, sworn before God, and therefore within the office Abraham holds.

This matters for how the action is to be judged. The breaking of the idols could be described as vandalism; the oath indicates that it is something else. The sūrah does not argue the point but records the oath, and the oath is what frames the action.

The framing is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the prophets throughout. Their actions are presented as undertaken within a conferred office, and the office is what verse 51 established when it said that God gave Abraham sound judgment aforetime and knew him.

**What the Verse Prepares**

The verse prepares the demonstration described at verse 58 and the exchange that follows at verses 59 to 65. Its function is to establish that what happens next is planned rather than spontaneous, and that the plan was formed after the argument was exhausted.

The preparation matters because the demonstration's force depends on its being legible as a demonstration. An act of spontaneous destruction would prove nothing; a planned setup, discovered after the fact, invites the inference it is designed to produce. The verse establishes the planning.

The sūrah's compression here is deliberate. It does not describe the planning in detail but records the resolution, and the resolution is sufficient to establish the intention. What follows supplies the execution and the response.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:58

> **So he broke them into pieces—save the largest of them—that haply they may have recourse to it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Broken Into Pieces**

*So he broke them into pieces—save the largest of them.* The verse records the execution of the plan announced at verse 57, and the exception is its central feature. Abraham does not destroy all the idols; he leaves the largest intact, and the leaving is what makes the act a demonstration rather than a destruction.

The commentators note that some add that Abraham took the ax with which he smashed the other idols and hung it around the neck of, or leaned it against, the largest one. The detail, whether historical or explanatory, indicates how the demonstration was read: as a setup designed to point at the largest idol.

The purpose of the exception is stated in the verse: *that haply they may have recourse to it.* The largest idol is left so that the people will turn to it, and the turning is what exposes its incapacity. The demonstration is not the breaking but the recourse.

**The Demonstration's Logic**

The setup is precise. If the people return to find the lesser idols broken and the largest intact, the obvious inference is that the largest broke them. The inference is what the demonstration invites, and the invitation is designed to be tested against the idol's actual capacity.

When the people do turn to the largest idol, as verse 63 will record, they will find that it cannot answer. The demonstration therefore does not assert the idols' incapacity; it arranges a situation in which the incapacity becomes visible. The arrangement is the argument.

This is a different mode from the verbal argument of verses 52 to 56, and al-Qurṭubī's note identifies it as such. The verbal argument asked for an account and received an inheritance; the demonstration asks a question that cannot be answered in the same way, because it is addressed to the idols rather than to the people.

**"That Haply They May Have Recourse"**

The clause *that haply they may have recourse to it* states the demonstration's aim, and the aim is diagnostic rather than destructive. Abraham wants his people to turn to their largest god, and the turning will show what the god can do.

The word *laʿalla* — haply, perhaps — indicates an aim that is offered rather than guaranteed. The demonstration makes the inference available; whether it is drawn is another matter. This is the same structure the sūrah noted at verse 31, where the passes were made *that haply they may be guided*.

The sūrah's consistent use of this form indicates a theology of evidence: what is provided is the means of seeing, not the seeing itself. The demonstration at verse 58 is provision, and verses 59 to 65 will record the response to it.

**The Festival Day**

The commentators record that Abraham lagged behind a group of his people on a festival day in order to accomplish his demonstration. The timing is significant: a festival is when the community is gathered elsewhere, and the absence ensures the demonstration will be discovered rather than witnessed.

The discovery matters for the demonstration's effect. A witnessed act would be attributed to the actor; a discovered state invites inference about the objects. The people will find the idols broken and the largest intact, and the finding will direct their attention to the idols rather than to Abraham.

The timing also indicates planning, which verse 57 established. Abraham did not act opportunistically but chose a moment when the demonstration would have its intended effect. The commentators' note supplies the detail that the verse's compression omits.

**The Verse and Verse 21**

The demonstration tests the claim the sūrah made at verse 21: *Or have they taken gods from the earth who resurrect?* The idols are shown to be unable to protect themselves, let alone to give life. The test is applied not by argument but by arrangement.

The connection is strengthened by verse 43, where the sūrah asked *Or do they have gods to defend them apart from Us? They cannot help themselves.* The demonstration at verse 58 exhibits precisely this: the idols cannot help themselves, and the exhibition is public.

The sūrah's method is thus consistent. It states the proposition at verse 21, argues it at verse 43, and demonstrates it at verse 58. The three are not redundant but cumulative, and each addresses a different faculty — attention, reasoning, and observation.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not describe Abraham's reasoning in performing the act, and the omission is deliberate. The reasoning was given at verse 56; what remains is the execution, and the verse records it in a single clause. The sūrah does not explain the act twice.

Nor does the verse comment on the propriety of the action. The oath at verse 57 frames it, and the framing is sufficient. The sūrah's method is to record rather than to justify, and the recording leaves the assessment to the reader who has followed the argument.

The compression is characteristic of this account. Unlike 37:84–96, which gives the destruction at greater length, this verse states the act and its exception in a single sentence. The exception is the point, and the sentence gives it.

**The Setup and the Response**

The verse sets up the exchange that follows. The people will return, discover the state of the idols, and ask who has done it. The question at verse 59 is the response the setup was designed to produce, and the response is exactly what Abraham intended.

The setup's success is measured by the question's form. *Who has done this to our gods?* treats the idols as agents who have had something done to them, which is the assumption the demonstration is designed to test. The question reveals the assumption, and the following verses will expose it.

The sūrah's narrative method is therefore economical. It arranges a situation, records the response, and lets the response do the argumentative work. The reader is not told what to conclude but shown a question that cannot be answered consistently with the assumption behind it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:59

> **They said, "Who has done this to our gods? Verily he is among the wrongdoers!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Discovery**

*They said, "Who has done this to our gods?"* The question is the response the demonstration was designed to produce, and its form is significant. The people ask who has acted *against* the gods, which presupposes that the gods are the kind of thing that can be acted against.

The presupposition is what the demonstration was arranged to expose. A god who can be broken is not a god, and the question's own framing concedes the point. The sūrah records the question without comment, and the absence of comment lets the concession stand.

The commentators' note that Abraham lagged behind on a festival day explains how the discovery occurred. The people returned to find a state they had not witnessed, and the state directed their attention to the idols rather than to any human agent.

**"Verily He Is Among the Wrongdoers"**

The second half of the verse adds a judgment: *Verily he is among the wrongdoers!* The exclamation indicates that the conclusion is reached before any investigation. The people do not ask who might have done it or why; they declare the actor a wrongdoer.

The declaration reveals the priority at work. The concern is not that the gods have been shown incapable but that someone has acted against them. The incapacity is not noticed, or is not permitted to count, and the judgment protects the assumption the demonstration exposed.

This is the same structure the sūrah recorded at verse 36, where the disbelievers reduced the Prophet's criticism to an offence. In both cases a challenge is converted into a grievance, and the conversion avoids the challenge.

**The Question and Its Irony**

There is an irony in the question that the verse leaves unstated. The people ask who has done this *to our gods*, and the answer will be that the largest of them did it — which is what verse 63 will record them saying. The question contains its own refutation.

The irony is not merely rhetorical. If the largest idol broke the others, then the others were unable to resist, and none of them is a god. The question assumes the gods' agency; the answer the people will give destroys the assumption. The sūrah arranges the exchange so that the refutation comes from the people themselves.

This is the demonstration's design. Abraham did not assert the idols' incapacity; he arranged a situation in which the people would assert their agency and thereby expose its absence. The argument is conducted by the respondents rather than by the prophet.

**The Verse and Verse 58**

Verses 58 and 59 form a unit: the setup and the discovery. The exception — leaving the largest idol — was designed to produce exactly the question that verse 59 records. The setup's success is measured by the question's form, and the form is as intended.

The commentators' note about the ax supports this reading. Whether Abraham hung it around the largest idol's neck or leaned it against him, the effect was to direct attention to that idol as the likely agent. The question at verse 59 shows the direction worked.

The unit demonstrates the mode of argument al-Qurṭubī identified at verse 57: after verbal argument, a different mode of demonstration. The demonstration does not persuade by reasoning but arranges a situation in which the respondents' own words do the work.

**The Judgment and the Investigation**
