The people's declaration that the actor is a wrongdoer precedes any identification of the actor. Verse 60 will record that they heard of a young man who mentioned the idols, and verse 61 that they resolved to bring him before the people. The sequence shows judgment before evidence.

The commentators note at verses 60 and 61 that the people called others to bear witness against Abraham either because they did not want to punish him without some proof or because they wanted the punishment to be public. The note indicates that the concern with proof came after the judgment, not before.

This is a precise observation about how the exchange operates. The judgment is reached immediately; the procedure that follows gives it the appearance of justification. The sūrah records the sequence without comment, and the sequence is the commentary.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The response parallels what the Prophet ﷺ faces. At verse 36 the disbelievers reduced his criticism to an offence and mocked him; here the people reduce Abraham's demonstration to wrongdoing and declare him a wrongdoer. Both responses convert a challenge into a grievance.

The parallel is what the account is included to establish. Verse 41 said that messengers were mocked before the Prophet ﷺ; the Abrahamic narrative supplies the instance. The reader is shown the pattern operating in a narrative he recognises, and the recognition transfers to his own situation.

The function is consolatory in the way verse 41 was. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the response he receives is the standard response, and that it does not indicate a defect in his message. Abraham's demonstration succeeded; the response to it was a judgment against him.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes the people's position after the demonstration, and the position is unchanged. The idols have been shown incapable; the response is to identify a wrongdoer. The demonstration succeeded in exposing the assumption but did not produce the inference.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of evidence throughout. At verse 30 the audience was asked *Will they not, then, believe?*; at verse 32 they turn away from the signs; at verse 45 the deaf do not hear however much they are warned. The provision of evidence does not guarantee its reception.

The verse therefore completes the demonstration's arc. The setup was arranged, the discovery occurred, the question was asked, and the judgment was declared. What remains is the identification of the actor and the public confrontation, which verses 60 to 65 will record.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:60

> **They said, "We heard a young man mention them; he is called Abraham."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Young Man Mentioned Them**

*They said, "We heard a young man mention them; he is called Abraham."* The verse records the identification, and the identification is based on speech rather than on evidence. The people do not report seeing Abraham at the idols; they report having heard him mention them.

The commentators note that *mention them* here means to mention them by way of censure and blame, and that the verb can connote disapproval depending on context, as noted at verse 36. The people are recalling Abraham's criticism, and the recollection is what connects him to the act.

The word *young man* — *fatan* — is significant. It identifies Abraham by his youth rather than by any status, and the identification is dismissive: the person who would do this is a junior member of the community, not one whose words carry weight.

**The Connection to Verse 36**

The commentators explicitly link this verse to verse 36, where the sūrah noted that *mention* can connote blame and that the disbelievers accused the Prophet ﷺ of making mention of their gods. The parallel is deliberate and connects the two confrontations.

In both cases a prophet's criticism is recalled as the ground of accusation. The criticism is not answered; it is cited as evidence of hostility. The sūrah's use of the same verb in both places ties the accounts together and identifies the response as a pattern.

The connection strengthens the consolatory function of the Abrahamic narrative. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the accusation he faces — that he speaks against the gods — is the accusation Abraham faced, and that it was cited as evidence against him rather than engaged with.

**Identification Without Evidence**

The identification rests on hearsay. *We heard a young man mention them* is a report of speech, not of action, and the verse records it as the ground on which Abraham is identified. No one claims to have seen him at the idols.

The commentators note at verse 61 that the people called others to bear witness against Abraham either because they did not want to punish him without some proof or because they wanted the punishment to be public. The note indicates that the concern with proof arose after the identification, and that the proof sought was testimony against him rather than evidence of the act.

This is the same structure recorded at verse 59, where the judgment preceded the investigation. The sequence is: discovery, judgment, identification by hearsay, and then a procedure to supply the appearance of justification. The sūrah records the sequence without comment.

**The Naming of Abraham**

The verse gives Abraham's name for the first time in this account. Verses 52 to 58 referred to him as *he*; here he is named, and the naming marks the transition from the private confrontation to the public one.

The naming is reported by his opponents, which is significant. Abraham does not identify himself; he is identified by those who heard him speak. The sūrah's method is to let the opponents supply the identification, and the supplying is part of the account.

The naming also prepares verse 61's resolution to bring him before the people. Once named, he can be summoned, and the summons is what the following verses record. The verse's function is transitional, and the naming is the transition.

**Hearsay as Ground**

The verse's reliance on hearsay is worth examining. The people are not uncertain about who acted; they are certain, and the certainty rests on the fact that Abraham had spoken against the idols. The inference from speech to action is made without hesitation.

The inference is not unreasonable on its own terms — a man who has declared the idols to be in error is a likely agent against them. But the inference is not evidence, and the verse records it as the ground of identification without noting the gap.

The gap matters for what follows. When Abraham is brought before the people, the question will be whether he did it, and his answer at verse 63 will redirect the question to the largest idol. The redirect works because the people's ground was never evidence of the act.

**The Verse and the Pattern**

The verse contributes to the pattern the sūrah has been establishing. At verse 24 the opponents could not bring proof; at verse 36 they reduced criticism to offence; at verse 38 they demanded a date; here they identify a suspect by hearsay. Each response substitutes something for evidence.

The pattern is what gives the Abrahamic narrative its function. The account is not included for its own sake but to exhibit the pattern in a narrative the audience recognises, and the recognition is what allows the application to the present case.

The commentators' cross-references support this reading. The note linking this verse to verse 36 indicates that they read the two accounts as parallel, and the parallel is the point of including the narrative at this length.

**What the Verse Leaves**

The verse leaves Abraham's response unrecorded. He is identified, and the following verse records the resolution to bring him before the people. What he will say is not anticipated, and the omission preserves the narrative's movement toward the public confrontation.

The confrontation is where the demonstration will be completed. The setup at verse 58 arranged a situation; the discovery at verse 59 produced a question; the identification at verse 60 names a suspect; the confrontation at verses 62 to 65 will supply the answer that redirects the question.

The sūrah's method is to move the account forward without anticipating its conclusion. Each verse records a step, and the steps accumulate toward the exchange in which the demonstration achieves its purpose. The verse's brevity is part of that movement.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:61

> **They said, "So bring him before the eyes of the people, that haply they may bear witness."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Before the Eyes of the People**

*They said, "So bring him before the eyes of the people, that haply they may bear witness."* The resolution is to make the matter public, and the phrase *before the eyes of the people* — *ʿalā aʿyuni'n-nās* — indicates that the proceeding is designed for visibility rather than for adjudication.

The commentators note two readings of the purpose. Al-Ṭabarī records that they did not want to punish him without some proof; al-Rāzī adds that they wanted the punishment to be public, to serve as an example to others. Both readings are preserved, and both concern the appearance of justification.

The two readings are compatible. A public proceeding supplies both the appearance of proof and the example. The verse's phrase — *that haply they may bear witness* — supports both, since witnessing serves the record and the audience simultaneously.

**Witnessing and Its Function**

The clause *that haply they may bear witness* specifies the proceeding's aim, and the aim is testimonial rather than investigative. The people are to witness, not to examine, and the distinction is what the verse's phrasing reveals.

The commentators' note that the concern with proof arose at this stage rather than earlier indicates the sequence: judgment at verse 59, identification by hearsay at verse 60, and now a proceeding to supply testimony. The testimony is sought after the conclusion, not before it.

This is the same structure recorded throughout the passage. At verse 24 the demand for proof was issued to those who had never supplied any; here testimony is sought for a judgment already reached. In both cases the procedure follows the conclusion rather than producing it.

**The Public Character**

Al-Rāzī's reading that the punishment was to be public, to serve as an example, identifies the proceeding's social function. The aim is not merely to deal with Abraham but to deter others, and the publicity is what makes the deterrence available.

The deterrence is directed at the criticism rather than the act. Abraham was identified because he had spoken against the idols; the public proceeding addresses the speaking. The example is therefore about speech, and the audience is meant to learn what speaking against the gods produces.

This connects the verse to verse 36, where the disbelievers took the Prophet ﷺ in mockery for making mention of their gods. In both cases the response is directed at the criticism, and in both the response is made visible. The parallel is what the account is included to establish.

**The Verse and the Demonstration**

The public proceeding is what allows the demonstration to be completed. Abraham's setup at verse 58 arranged a situation; the confrontation will supply the answer that redirects the question. Without the public proceeding, the redirect would have no audience.

The sūrah's narrative design is therefore precise. The demonstration was arranged to be discovered; the discovery produced a question; the identification named a suspect; the proceeding brings the suspect before the people. Each step makes the next possible, and the final step makes the demonstration's purpose achievable.

The purpose is stated at verse 58: *that haply they may have recourse to it.* The recourse will occur at verse 63, when the people are directed to the largest idol. The proceeding is the setting in which the recourse is invited, and the invitation is the demonstration's completion.

**"Haply They May Bear Witness"**

The word *laʿalla* — haply — appears here as it did at verse 58 and verse 31. The sūrah's repeated use of the term indicates a consistent view of what evidence and proceedings can achieve: they make a conclusion available without guaranteeing it.

The people intend the witnessing to confirm their judgment; the verse's phrasing leaves open whether it will. The demonstration has been arranged so that the witnessing may produce a different conclusion than the one intended, and the openness is what the word conveys.

This is the sūrah's theology of evidence in miniature. What is provided — the demonstration, the proceeding, the testimony — makes seeing possible. Whether seeing occurs is another matter, and the sūrah does not claim to control it.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The resolution to bring Abraham before the people parallels the public character of the Prophet's confrontation. At verse 36 the mockery was public; at verse 42 the Prophet ﷺ was told to *Say* — to speak publicly. The Abrahamic account supplies the precedent for a confrontation conducted before an audience.

The precedent is consolatory in the way the earlier verses were. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the public proceeding he faces has a precedent, that the precedent was conducted by one given sound judgment and known by God, and that the proceeding became the occasion for the demonstration's completion.

The sūrah does not promise that the Prophet's proceeding will have the same outcome. It shows that the pattern exists and that the pattern's outcome at verse 63 was not what the conveners intended. The consolation is the pattern, not a guarantee.

**What the Verse Sets Up**

The verse sets up the exchange at verses 62 to 65, in which Abraham will be questioned and will redirect the question to the largest idol. The public character of the proceeding is what gives the redirect its force: the demonstration is completed before witnesses.

The sūrah's compression is evident here. The verse records the resolution in a single sentence and moves on. What follows supplies the exchange, and the exchange is where the demonstration achieves what verse 58 intended.

The design is economical and complete. Each verse in the sequence performs one function — setup, discovery, identification, convening — and the functions accumulate toward the exchange. The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is single and clear.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:62

> **They said, "Was it you who did this to our gods, O Abraham?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Direct Question**

*They said, "Was it you who did this to our gods, O Abraham?"* The question is put directly and by name, and the naming marks the transition from the identification at verse 60 to the confrontation at verse 61. Abraham is now before the people, and the question is the proceeding's opening move.

The question's form is significant. It asks whether he did it, which presupposes that the doing is the matter at issue. The demonstration at verse 58 was arranged to shift the issue from the doing to the gods' capacity, and the question shows that the shift has not yet occurred.

The sūrah records the question without comment, as it recorded the question at verse 59. Both questions treat the idols as agents who have had something done to them, and both presuppositions are what the demonstration is designed to expose. The repetition of the form is the demonstration's opening.

**The Setting and Its Purpose**

The confrontation occurs before the people, as verse 61 resolved. The publicity is what makes the demonstration completable: the redirect Abraham is about to make requires an audience, and the audience is assembled.

The commentators note at verse 61 that the people wanted the punishment to be public, to serve as an example to others. The irony is that the proceeding convened for deterrence becomes the occasion for the demonstration's completion. The sūrah does not note the irony; the narrative supplies it.

The setting also explains the form of Abraham's response at verse 63. A private answer would have been a statement; a public answer is a demonstration, and the demonstration is what verse 58 arranged. The proceeding supplies the conditions the setup required.

**The Question and Verse 59**

Verse 62 repeats the question asked at verse 59 — *Who has done this to our gods?* — now addressed to a named individual. The repetition is not redundant: the first question was asked of the air, the second of Abraham, and the second requires an answer.

The sūrah's method is to let the repetition do the work. The same presupposition is voiced twice, and the second voicing is directed at the one who arranged the situation. Abraham's answer will address the presupposition rather than the question, and the repetition makes the redirect possible.

The connection between the two verses is what the demonstration depends on. If the question had been asked only once, the redirect would have appeared evasive. Asked twice, and addressed the second time to a named person, the redirect becomes a demonstration rather than a deflection.

**What the Question Presupposes**

The question presupposes that the gods are the kind of thing that can be wronged. *Did you do this to our gods?* treats them as objects of an action, and the treatment implies that they have a standing that can be violated. The presupposition is the assumption the demonstration targets.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that some see in Abraham's response the implication that the largest idol was angered by the fact that the other idols were worshipped. The reading identifies the presupposition precisely: the people's question assumes the gods have feelings and agency, and the assumption is what the following verse will test.

The sūrah does not state the presupposition explicitly. It records the question and lets the question reveal it, which is more effective than stating it because the revelation comes from the people's own words rather than from the prophet's assertion.

**The Verse and the Demonstration's Design**

Verse 62 is the point at which the demonstration begins to work. The setup at verse 58 arranged the state of the idols; the discovery at verse 59 produced a question; the identification at verse 60 named a suspect; the proceeding at verse 61 assembled the audience; and now the question is put.

Each step has been necessary, and the sūrah's compression of each into a single verse indicates that the narrative is designed for economy. Nothing is included that does not advance the demonstration, and the advance is toward the redirect at verse 63.

The design is what al-Qurṭubī identified at verse 57: after verbal argument, a different mode of demonstration. The verbal argument occupied verses 52 to 56; the demonstration occupies verses 58 to 65, and this verse is its culmination.

**Abraham's Position**

The verse places Abraham before his people, accused, and named. The position is the one the sūrah has been describing throughout: a prophet confronting a community attached to ancestral practice, publicly, at cost. Verse 54 noted the risk of the judgment; this verse exhibits it.

The position is also the Prophet's. At verse 36 the disbelievers mocked him publicly; at verse 42 he was told to speak publicly. The Abrahamic account supplies the precedent, and the precedent is what the sūrah offers as consolation.

The sūrah does not soften the position or promise a favourable outcome. It records the question and moves to the answer, and the answer is what the demonstration has been arranged to make possible. The verse's function is to create the condition the answer requires.

**What the Verse Leaves Open**

The verse does not anticipate Abraham's answer. The question is recorded and the narrative moves on, and the omission preserves the demonstration's force. An anticipated answer would be a statement; an unanticipated one is a demonstration.

The commentators' discussion of the following verse — whether Abraham's response constitutes a lie — indicates that the answer was understood to be unexpected. The discussion occupies considerable space in the classical tradition, and its existence testifies to the answer's surprising character.

The sūrah's method is to record without anticipating. Each verse performs one function, and the functions accumulate toward the exchange. The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function — putting the question — is single and complete.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:63

> **He said, "Nay, but it was the largest of them that did this. So question them, if they speak!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Redirect**

*He said, "Nay, but it was the largest of them that did this. So question them, if they speak!"* The answer does not address the question asked. Abraham neither confirms nor denies that he broke the idols; he redirects the inquiry to the largest idol, which verse 58 left intact.

The redirect is the demonstration's completion. The setup was arranged so that the largest idol would appear to be the agent; the answer supplies the appearance with a voice. What follows — the command to question the idols — is what converts the appearance into an argument.

The clause *if they speak* is the verse's hinge. It states the condition under which the redirect would be informative, and the condition is one the idols cannot meet. The command to question them is therefore not a suggestion but a test, and the test's outcome is known in advance.

**The Commentators' Problem**

The commentators record a ḥadīth attributed to the Prophet stating that Abraham lied only concerning three things: when he said *Truly I am sick* (37:89), that Sarah was his sister, and that the largest idol destroyed the others. The third is this verse, and the ḥadīth treats it as a lie.

The commentary notes that many commentators wrestle with the problematic notion of a prophet lying under any circumstances, and that they do so in a manner similar to their treatment of Abraham's apparent worship of celestial bodies at 6:76–78. The parallel indicates that both passages present the same difficulty.

Al-Qurṭubī mentions that perhaps Abraham told these lies while he was a child. Al-Rāzī reasons differently, and the commentary records the discussion without settling it. The plurality of solutions indicates that the tradition took the difficulty seriously rather than dismissing it.

**Why the Difficulty Matters**

The commentators' wrestling with the verse is instructive about how they read it. If the statement were simply false, the difficulty would be about Abraham's character; if it were a demonstration, the difficulty would be about its form. The tradition's uncertainty indicates that both readings are available.

The reading that treats the statement as a ruse is supported by verse 58's exception. Abraham left the largest idol precisely so that the people might have recourse to it, and the answer at verse 63 supplies the recourse. On this reading the statement is not a claim but a move in the demonstration.

Al-Qurṭubī's note that some see in the response the implication that the largest idol was angered by the worship of the others supports the ruse reading. The implication is not asserted but offered as what the situation suggests, and the offering is the demonstration's method.

**"So Question Them, If They Speak"**

The command is the verse's argumentative core. Abraham does not assert that the idols cannot speak; he invites the people to test whether they can. The invitation is addressed to the audience assembled at verse 61, and the test is one they can perform immediately.

The conditional *if they speak* is precisely framed. It does not say the idols do not speak but makes the question's answerability depend on their capacity. If they can speak, they can be questioned; if they cannot, the question about who broke the others has no answer from them.

The command therefore completes the demonstration. The setup arranged the appearance; the redirect supplied the claim; the command tests it. The test's outcome is what verse 65 will record, when the people are made to acknowledge that the idols do not speak.

**The Verse and Verse 21**

The verse applies the test the sūrah posed at verse 21: *Or have they taken gods from the earth who resurrect?* The idols cannot give life; here they cannot speak. The test is applied not by argument but by invitation, and the invitation is addressed to those who worship them.

The commentators note at verse 66 that similar questions about the idols' inability to help or harm appear at 5:76, 6:71, 10:18, 10:106, 13:16, 20:89, 22:12, and 25:55. The recurrence indicates that this is a settled argument in the Qur'an, and this verse is its most dramatic instance.

The drama is what distinguishes the instance. The argument is not stated but performed, and the performance requires the setup described at verse 58. The sūrah includes the narrative because the argument's force here exceeds what statement alone achieves.

**The Demonstration Completed**

The verse completes what verse 58 began. The exception — leaving the largest idol — was made *that haply they may have recourse to it*; the answer at verse 63 supplies the recourse; the command tests it. The three are one movement, and this verse is its culmination.

The completion is achieved through the people's own agency. Abraham does not demonstrate the idols' incapacity; he invites the people to question them, and the questioning will show the incapacity. The demonstration is conducted by the respondents, which is what makes it effective.

The sūrah's method here is characteristic. It does not assert conclusions but arranges situations in which the conclusions become visible. The arrangement requires the narrative — the setup, the discovery, the proceeding — and the narrative is what the sūrah supplies at this length.

**What the Verse Achieves**

The verse shifts the issue from the act to the gods. The question at verse 62 asked whether Abraham did it; the answer makes the question unanswerable unless the idols can speak. The shift is what the demonstration was designed to produce, and it is produced by the people's own question.

The shift is also what verse 65 will record. The people are made to revert, acknowledging that the idols do not speak, and the acknowledgement is the demonstration's outcome. The verse's command is what produces the acknowledgement, and the production is not coerced but invited.

The sūrah's economy is evident. A single sentence — *Nay, but it was the largest of them that did this. So question them, if they speak!* — completes a demonstration that occupied six verses of setup. The compression is possible because the setup did the work.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:64

> **So they consulted among themselves and said, "Verily it is you who are the wrongdoers!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Consulting Among Themselves**

*So they consulted among themselves and said, "Verily it is you who are the wrongdoers!"* The consultation is the verse's first and most telling feature, and it indicates that the people have been given pause. They do not respond immediately to Abraham's command; they confer.

The conferring is precisely what the demonstration was designed to produce. A question that cannot be answered without testing the idols' capacity forces deliberation, and the deliberation is the first sign that the presupposition behind the question at verse 62 has been disturbed.

The sūrah records the consultation without describing it. What is recorded is its outcome, and the outcome is a reversal: the wrongdoers are identified as the people themselves rather than as Abraham.

**The Reversal**

The declaration — *Verily it is you who are the wrongdoers!* — reverses the judgment made at verse 59, where the people declared that the actor against the gods was among the wrongdoers. The same word is used, and the reversal is exact.

Al-Ṭabarī records that according to some, this verse means that some of the people thought they were wronging Abraham in accusing him. The reading identifies the declaration as a recognition of injustice rather than merely a shift of accusation, and the recognition is the demonstration's effect.

The reversal is significant because it comes from the people themselves rather than from the prophet's assertion. Abraham did not claim that they were the wrongdoers; they declared it after consulting among themselves. The declaration is therefore not a concession extracted but a conclusion reached, and the reaching is what makes it meaningful.

**The Consultation and the Demonstration**

The consultation is the point at which the demonstration achieves its effect. Verse 63's command — *question them, if they speak* — cannot be executed without confronting the idols' incapacity, and the confrontation forces the people to consider what they have been worshipping.

The sūrah's method is to let the people's own deliberation produce the conclusion. Abraham does not argue that the idols cannot speak; he commands the people to question them, and the command's unexecutability is what the consultation reveals.

This is the mode al-Qurṭubī identified at verse 57: after verbal argument, a different mode of demonstration. The verbal argument occupied verses 52 to 56 and produced no movement; the demonstration produces a reversal in two verses.

**The Verse and Verse 59**

Verses 59 and 64 form a pair around the demonstration. At verse 59 the people declared the actor a wrongdoer before any investigation; at verse 64 they declare themselves wrongdoers after consulting. The two declarations use the same term and mark the demonstration's arc.

The arc is what the sūrah intends. The setup at verse 58, the discovery at verse 59, the identification at verse 60, the proceeding at verse 61, the question at verse 62, and the redirect at verse 63 all lead to this reversal, and the reversal is the demonstration's outcome.

The sūrah does not comment on the reversal. It records the consultation and the declaration, and the recording is sufficient. The reader sees the movement from premature judgment to considered recognition without being told to see it, which is how the sūrah handles every turn in this account.

**The Limits of the Reversal**

The reversal is not a conversion. The following verse will record that the people reverted, and the commentators gloss the term as being overcome briefly by Abraham's argument. The recognition is real but temporary, and the sūrah does not present it as more than it is.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of evidence throughout. At verse 30 the audience was asked *Will they not, then, believe?*; at verse 45 the deaf do not hear however much they are warned. The provision of evidence produces recognition without guaranteeing conversion.

The limit is what makes the account realistic. The demonstration succeeds in producing a reversal; it does not succeed in producing faith. The sūrah records both the success and the limit, and the recording is more credible for including the second — an account that claimed conversion here would be harder to trust in what it claims elsewhere.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The reversal is consolatory in a specific way. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the demonstration can produce recognition, and that the recognition can come from the opponents themselves rather than from his assertion. The consolation is not that conversion follows but that the argument can land.

The limit is also consolatory, in the way verse 41 was. The recognition was brief, and the people reverted. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that a successful argument does not guarantee a lasting effect, and that the absence of a lasting effect is not evidence against the argument.

The sūrah offers this form of comfort repeatedly. At verse 25 that he is not innovating; at verse 36 and 41 that he is not alone in being mocked; here that the argument can produce recognition even when recognition does not persist.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the demonstration worked. The people consulted and reached a conclusion contrary to their initial judgment, and the conclusion came from their own deliberation. The demonstration's design — setup, discovery, proceeding, redirect — achieved its purpose.

The verse also establishes the limit. The conclusion was reached under the pressure of an unanswerable question, and the following verse will show that it did not hold. The sūrah records both facts, and the recording is what makes the account credible rather than triumphalist.

The sūrah's method is consistent throughout: state what happened, including what did not follow from it. The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is to record a moment rather than to draw a conclusion from it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:65

> **Then they reverted, "Certainly you know that these speak not!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reversion**

*Then they reverted, "Certainly you know that these speak not!"* The verse records the reversal of the reversal, and the term rendered *reverted* is the verse's most discussed feature. Al-Ṭabarī explains that it translates *nukisū ʿalā ruʾūsihim*, which can mean were made to relapse, or literally were turned upside down or turned over onto their heads or beginnings.

The commentators record three readings. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the idol worshippers were overcome, albeit briefly, by Abraham's argument and demonstration and changed their minds. Some think the phrase means they were overcome by the madness of evil. Others read it as going back in their thinking because they were convinced, meaning that they began to think properly about the matter, which al-Rāzī records.

The plurality of readings is instructive. The term denotes a turning, and the commentators differ about its direction — relapse into error, or turning toward proper thought. The sūrah does not settle the question, and the ambiguity is part of the verse's force.

**"Certainly You Know That These Speak Not"**

The declaration is the demonstration's outcome stated by the people themselves. They acknowledge that the idols do not speak, which is the condition Abraham's command at verse 63 made decisive. The acknowledgement is exact and complete.

The phrase *certainly you know* is directed at the people collectively, and the knowing is what the consultation at verse 64 produced. The people are not learning something new; they are stating what they have come to know, and the stating is the demonstration's completion.

The acknowledgement is what makes the demonstration conclusive rather than merely persuasive. Abraham did not assert the idols' incapacity; the people declared it, in their own words and before their own assembly. The declaration comes from those who worship them, which is what gives it weight.

**The Ambiguity of the Turning**

The commentators' disagreement about the direction of the turning reflects the verse's ambiguity. If the people relapsed, the acknowledgement was momentary and the reversion was into error. If they turned toward proper thought, the acknowledgement was a beginning.

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss — that they were overcome briefly by Abraham's argument — supports the first reading. Al-Rāzī's — that they began to think properly — supports the second. The commentary preserves both, and the preservation indicates that the tradition read the moment as genuinely ambiguous.

The ambiguity is what makes the verse realistic. A demonstration that produces an acknowledgement does not thereby produce conversion, and the sūrah does not claim otherwise. The people say what the demonstration compels them to say, and what follows from the saying is left open rather than resolved in the prophet's favour.

**The Verse and the Demonstration**

The verse completes the demonstration's arc. The setup at verse 58 arranged the exception; the command at verse 63 tested it; the consultation at verse 64 produced a reversal; and this verse records the people stating the condition that makes the demonstration conclusive.

The completion is achieved entirely through the people's own words. Abraham's contribution was the setup and the command; the acknowledgement is theirs. This is what al-Qurṭubī identified at verse 57 as a different mode of demonstration, and the mode's advantage is that the conclusion comes from the respondents.

The sūrah's design is therefore complete. Six verses of setup and two of exchange produce an acknowledgement that no amount of verbal argument achieved. The economy is the design's success, and the verse is its final move.

**What the Acknowledgement Does Not Achieve**

The acknowledgement does not produce worship of God. The people state that the idols do not speak, and the following verses record Abraham's continued challenge. The acknowledgement is a recognition of incapacity, not a transfer of allegiance.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of evidence. At verse 44 the audience was asked to consider the reduction of the land; at verse 45 the deaf do not hear however much they are warned. Recognition and conversion are different, and the sūrah distinguishes them throughout.

The distinction is what makes the account useful. A reader who expects demonstration to produce faith will be disappointed; a reader who understands that demonstration produces recognition will understand what the account shows. The sūrah supplies the second understanding.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The verse is consolatory in a qualified way. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that demonstration can produce acknowledgement from opponents, and that the acknowledgement may not persist. Both facts matter: the first establishes that the argument can land, the second that its landing does not guarantee an outcome.

The qualification is what makes the consolation credible. An account in which the demonstration produced immediate conversion would be less useful to a man whose preaching was not producing conversion. The sūrah supplies the account that matches his situation.

This is the form of comfort the sūrah has offered throughout. At verse 41 that messengers were mocked and the mockers were beset; here that the argument can produce acknowledgement even when acknowledgement does not persist. Neither promises a particular outcome.

**What the Verse Leaves**

The verse leaves the people's position after the acknowledgement unstated. They have said what the demonstration compelled them to say, and what they will do is not recorded. The following verses supply Abraham's continued challenge, which indicates that the matter is not settled.

The omission is deliberate. The sūrah does not claim that the demonstration resolved anything beyond the acknowledgement. What it shows is that the idols' incapacity was stated by their own worshippers, in their own words, before the assembled people, and the stating is the demonstration's achievement.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is single: to record the acknowledgement. What follows from it is left to the narrative, and the narrative continues with Abraham's challenge at verses 66 and 67.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:66

> **He said, "Do you worship, apart from God, that which benefits you not in the least, nor harms you?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Renewed**

*He said, "Do you worship, apart from God, that which benefits you not in the least, nor harms you?"* Abraham's challenge returns to the argument after the demonstration, and the return is significant. The acknowledgement at verse 65 established the idols' incapacity to speak; this verse extends it to benefit and harm.

The extension is the argument's completion. An idol that cannot speak might still be credited with influence; an idol that can neither benefit nor harm cannot be credited with anything. The question therefore closes the last remaining ground.

The phrase *in the least* — *shayʾan* — is emphatic. The denial is not that the idols benefit little but that they benefit not at all, and the totality of the denial is what makes the question decisive.

**The Recurring Argument**

The commentators note that similar statements and questions regarding the idols' inability to help or harm appear at 5:76, 6:71, 10:18, 10:106, 13:16, 20:89, 22:12, and 25:55. The recurrence across eight other passages indicates that this is a central Qur'anic argument rather than a local one.

The recurrence also indicates that the argument requires repetition. A single statement does not settle the matter, and the Qur'an returns to it because the matter is not settled by statement alone. The Abrahamic narrative is the most developed instance, and its length reflects the argument's importance.

The present instance is distinctive in being embedded in a demonstration. The other passages state the argument; this one performs it. The performance is what the sūrah supplies at this length, and the length is justified by the argument's centrality.

**Benefit and Harm as the Test**

The test Abraham applies is practical rather than metaphysical. He does not ask whether the idols exist or whether they are divine in some abstract sense; he asks whether they benefit or harm. The test is one his people can apply immediately and repeatedly.

The practicality is what makes the test effective. A metaphysical argument requires premises that may be disputed; a practical test requires only observation. The people who worship the idols can observe whether they have received benefit or harm from them, and the observation is available to them at any time.

This is the same method the sūrah has used throughout. At verse 30 the appeal was to water; at verse 32 to the sky; at verse 44 to the reduction of the frontiers. Each appeal is to something observable, and the appeal at verse 66 is the most directly practical.

**The Verse and Verse 65**

Verse 66 follows the acknowledgement at verse 65 and extends it. The people have said that the idols do not speak; Abraham asks whether they benefit or harm. The extension is not redundant: speech is one capacity, and its absence does not by itself establish the absence of all capacity.

The sequence is therefore argumentative. The demonstration established one incapacity through the people's own acknowledgement; the question establishes a further incapacity through their own observation. The two together close the case, and the closing is achieved through what the people themselves can verify.

The sūrah's method is cumulative. Each verse adds a step, and the steps accumulate toward a conclusion that no single step establishes. The verse's function is to add the step that the acknowledgement did not supply.

**The Question's Addressee**

The question is addressed to the people, and the addressing is significant. Abraham does not assert that the idols do not benefit or harm; he asks whether his people worship what does not. The question places the burden on them, and the burden is one they cannot discharge.

This is the same method the sūrah used at verse 24, where the Prophet ﷺ was told to say *Bring me your proof!* In both cases the challenge takes the form of a question that requires the respondents to supply something they do not have.

The method is more effective than assertion because it does not give the respondents anything to rebut. An assertion can be denied; a question about one's own experience cannot. The question at verse 66 is of the second kind.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse applies to Abraham's case the test the sūrah posed at verse 21: *Or have they taken gods from the earth who resurrect?* The idols cannot give life; here they cannot benefit or harm. The two tests are complementary, and together they exclude every function a god might perform.

The connection is what gives the Abrahamic narrative its place in this sūrah. The account is not included as a story but as an instance of the argument, and the instance is the most developed in the Qur'an. The sūrah supplies it because the argument requires development.

The commentators' list of parallel passages indicates that the argument is made elsewhere more briefly. The present instance is longer because it is embedded in a demonstration, and the demonstration requires the narrative the sūrah supplies.

**What the Verse Requires**

The verse's practical implication is that worship should be assessed by its effects. What a person worships is shown by what he receives from it, and the receiving is observable. The test does not require theological premises; it requires attention to one's own experience.

The test is available to any reader. The question is not addressed only to idol worshippers but to anyone who devotes himself to something that neither benefits nor harms him. The sūrah does not make the application explicit, but the question's form makes it available.

This is the sūrah's consistent method: state the case in a form that allows the reader to apply it. The verse does not say *so it is with you*; it asks a question that any reader can put to himself, and the putting is the application.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:67

> **Fie upon you, and upon that which you worship apart from God. Do you not understand?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fie Upon You**

*Fie upon you, and upon that which you worship apart from God. Do you not understand?* The exclamation is the verse's opening, and the commentators note that it renders the Arabic expression *uff*, which is also mentioned at 17:23 as what one should not say to one's parents. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that it is an utterance meant to express annoyance, and even disgust.

The word choice is significant. *Uff* is not an argument but an expression of feeling, and its use by a prophet to his people indicates the extremity of the situation. The demonstration has been completed, the acknowledgement has been given, and the response has been reversion.

The commentators' note that the same expression is forbidden toward one's parents sharpens the point. Abraham uses toward his people a word that would be improper toward a parent, and the impropriety is a measure of what the demonstration has established.

**The Object of the Exclamation**

The exclamation is directed at both the people and what they worship: *Fie upon you, and upon that which you worship apart from God.* The double object is significant — the idols are included in the expression of disgust, which is itself a statement about their status.

The inclusion of the idols is the argument's final move. Having established that they do not speak, benefit, or harm, Abraham treats them as objects of disgust rather than of reverence. The treatment is not an argument but a conclusion expressed in the register of feeling.

The sūrah's method here departs from its usual economy. The preceding verses argued and demonstrated; this verse expresses. The departure indicates that the argument has been completed and that what remains is not persuasion but recognition.

**"Do You Not Understand?"**

The closing question is directed at the faculty rather than at the facts. Abraham does not ask whether the people accept his argument; he asks whether they understand. The question implies that the material is available and that what is lacking is the act of comprehension.

This is the same move the sūrah made at verse 10, where the commentators note that the challenge is to a faculty rather than to a fact. The recurrence indicates a consistent view: the obstacle to belief is not a shortage of evidence but a failure to apply the faculty that would process it.

The question is therefore not rhetorical in the sense of expecting no answer. It expects an answer — that they do not understand — and the expectation is what makes it an indictment rather than a query.

**The Verse and Verse 65**

Verse 67 follows the reversion recorded at verse 65, and the sequence explains the exclamation. The people acknowledged that the idols do not speak; then they reverted. The acknowledgement did not hold, and the exclamation is the response to its not holding.

The commentators' gloss at verse 65 — that they were overcome briefly by Abraham's argument — indicates that the reversion was quick. The exclamation at verse 67 therefore addresses not a considered rejection but a failure to sustain a recognition that had been achieved.

This is what makes the exclamation appropriate. A considered rejection would invite further argument; a failure to sustain a recognition invites the expression of disgust. The verse's register matches the situation the preceding verse described.

**Understanding as a Category**

The question about understanding connects to the sūrah's treatment of the faculty throughout. At verse 10 the challenge was to understanding; at verse 30 the audience was asked *Will they not, then, believe?*; at verse 45 the deaf do not hear however much they are warned.

The recurrence indicates that the sūrah locates the obstacle in the use of a faculty rather than in its possession. The people have the capacity to understand; what they lack is the act. The question at verse 67 asks about the act, and the asking is the indictment.

This is consistent with the sūrah's theology of evidence. What is provided — the demonstration, the argument, the observation — makes understanding possible. Whether understanding occurs is another matter, and the sūrah does not claim to control it.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The exclamation is consolatory in a specific way. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that a prophet may reach the point of expressing disgust at his people's persistence, and that the expression does not indicate a failure of the message. Abraham's demonstration succeeded; the persistence followed anyway.

The sūrah does not present the exclamation as a model of prophetic conduct but as a record of a moment. The prophets are described in their full humanity, including the extremity of feeling that follows a completed argument and a rejected conclusion.

The record is what makes the account useful. A prophet who never reached such a point would be less relevant to a man whose preaching was not producing results. The sūrah supplies the account that matches the situation.

**What the Verse Closes**

The verse closes the argumentative phase of the Abrahamic account. The question was asked at verse 52, the appeal to the fathers was given at verse 53, the judgment was declared at verse 54, the positive claim was stated at verse 56, the demonstration was arranged at verse 58, and the acknowledgement was given at verse 65.

What remains is the response — the fire, which the following verses will record. The argument is complete, and the exclamation marks its completion. The sūrah does not continue arguing because there is nothing further to argue.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is to close. Two exclamations and one question complete an account that occupied sixteen verses, and the compression is possible only because the preceding verses did the work that makes the closing register intelligible.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:68

> **They said, "Burn him, and help your gods, if you would take action!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Burn**

*They said, "Burn him, and help your gods, if you would take action!"* The response to the demonstration is not an answer but an execution. The people do not address the acknowledgement they made at verse 65; they turn to violence, and the turn is the verse's whole content.

The commentators note that this incident is mentioned in more detail at 37:97, where Abraham's people say *Erect a building for him and cast him into the fire*. The present account compresses the command into a single exclamation, and the compression conveys the haste that the longer account describes.

The phrase *help your gods* is theologically pointed. The people describe the burning as assistance to the idols, which implies that the idols are in a position requiring assistance. The implication contradicts the acknowledgement at verse 65 — that the idols do not speak — and the contradiction is not noticed by those who voice it.

**The Contradiction the Verse Records**

The verse's central interest is the contradiction it records without comment. At verse 65 the people declared that the idols do not speak; at verse 68 they propose to help them. The interval between the two verses is short, and the sūrah does not bridge it with any explanation.

The absence of explanation is the commentary. A reader who follows the sequence sees the acknowledgement fail to hold, and sees what replaces it. The sūrah does not say *they reverted*; it shows the reversion in the form of a command, which is more effective than any statement.

This is what verse 65's ambiguity was preparing. The commentators disagreed about whether the people's turning was a relapse or a beginning; verse 68 resolves the ambiguity in the direction of relapse, and resolves it by showing rather than telling.

**"If You Would Take Action"**

The closing exhortation — *if you would take action!* — addresses the people's own hesitation. The command is issued to a group, and the exhortation indicates that the group requires urging. The violence is not spontaneous but must be动员ed, and the mobilising is recorded.

The exhortation also reveals what the people take to be at stake. The burning is presented as assistance to the gods, which means the gods' position is thought to require defence. The defence is what the exhortation calls for, and the calling indicates that it has not yet been undertaken.

The verse therefore records a collective decision rather than an individual act. The people confer, exhort one another, and command the burning. The collective character is what makes the subsequent deliverance at verse 69 significant: it is not a rescue from one enemy but from a community.

**The Popular Stories and Their Status**

Al-Rāzī notes that some of the popular stories attached to this incident have no direct basis in the Qur'an itself — in particular the assertion that it was Nimrod who commanded the burning. The note is a caution about the narrative's elaboration in later tradition, and it is recorded here for that reason.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī also report traditions that the structure built was so large and became so hot that a bird flying too close would be incinerated in midair. The detail is vivid and the commentators record it, but its status is that of tradition rather than of Qur'anic text.

The distinction matters for how the account is to be read. The Qur'an gives the command, the Divine address to the fire, and the deliverance; the elaborations supply scale and drama. The commentators preserve both while marking the difference, and the marking is part of their method.

**Abraham's Prayer**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī report that Abraham recited a prayer as he was being cast into the fire: *O God, Thou art alone in Heaven, and I am alone on earth in worshipping Thee.* The prayer is not in the Qur'anic text but is recorded in the tradition the commentators preserve.

The prayer's content is theologically apt. It states the situation the demonstration established — that Abraham alone worships the One God — and addresses it to the One who is alone in Heaven. The parallelism between the two solitudes is what gives the prayer its force.

The prayer also connects to verse 56, where Abraham said *I am among those who bear witness unto this*. There he located himself within a class of witnesses; here the prayer states his isolation. The two statements are not contradictory but mark the distance between the claim and the cost of making it.

**The Verse and the Demonstration**

Verse 68 is the response to a demonstration that succeeded. The people acknowledged the idols' incapacity and then proposed to burn the one who arranged the acknowledgement. The sequence is the account's most instructive feature, and the sūrah records it without softening.

The sequence also completes the pattern verse 41 described: messengers were mocked before the Prophet ﷺ, and those who scoffed were beset by what they mocked. Here the mockery takes the form of a command to burn, and the besetting will follow at verse 70.

The consolatory function is specific. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that a successful demonstration does not guarantee a favourable response, and that the response may be violence. The account does not promise that the violence will fail, though verse 69 will record that it did.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes the limit of demonstration. The idols' incapacity was acknowledged by their own worshippers; the acknowledgement did not prevent the command to burn. The sūrah records both facts, and the recording is what makes the account useful rather than triumphalist.

The limit is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of evidence throughout. At verse 45 the deaf do not hear however much they are warned; at verse 65 the people reverted after acknowledging. The provision of evidence makes recognition possible without guaranteeing conversion, and verse 68 shows what may follow instead.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is single: to record the response. What follows — the Divine address to the fire — is the answer to the command, and the answer is given in the next verse rather than here.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:69

> **We said, "O Fire! Be coolness and peace for Abraham."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address to the Fire**

*We said, "O Fire! Be coolness and peace for Abraham."* The verse records the Divine response to the command at verse 68, and the response is addressed to the fire rather than to Abraham. The addressing is the verse's most striking feature: the fire is spoken to as an agent capable of receiving a command.

The command has two terms — *coolness* and *peace* — and the commentators note that both were necessary. Al-Ṭabarī records that some say that were it not for the command to the fire to be peace, the coolness would have overcome Abraham. The reading indicates that coolness alone would have been lethal in its own way.

The pairing is therefore precise. Coolness removes the burning; peace removes the harm that extreme cold would cause. The verse's two words together specify a condition in which Abraham is preserved, and the specification indicates that the miracle was not a simple suspension of heat.

**Al-Rāzī's Three Explanations**

Al-Rāzī records three possible ways the fire became coolness and peace for Abraham. The first: God removed the heat and the ability to burn from the fire while leaving its illumination. The second: God caused a change in the nature of Abraham's body so that he was not affected by the fire. The third: God placed a barrier between Abraham and the fire that kept it from burning him.

The three explanations differ in what is altered — the fire, the body, or the relation between them. Al-Rāzī records all three without preferring any, and the recording indicates that the tradition did not consider the mechanism settled.

The plurality is instructive about how the commentators handled miracle accounts. The fact of the deliverance is not in question; the manner of it is. The tradition preserves multiple accounts of the manner while treating the fact as established, and the distinction is methodologically significant.

**Coolness Retained, Burning Removed**

Al-Rāzī's first explanation is worth attending to because it preserves the fire's appearance. If God removed the heat and the ability to burn while leaving the illumination, then the fire remained visibly a fire. The onlookers would have seen a fire that did not burn, and the seeing is what makes the event a sign.

The preservation of appearance matters for the account's function. A fire that ceased to be a fire would prove nothing about Divine power over fire; a fire that remained a fire and did not burn demonstrates the power precisely. The explanation is therefore the most theologically useful of the three.

The sūrah does not settle the question, and the commentators' preservation of all three indicates that they did not consider it necessary to settle. What the verse asserts is the address and the deliverance; the mechanism is left to explanation, and multiple explanations are available.

**The Address as a Category**

The Divine address to the fire belongs to a Qur'anic category in which natural elements are spoken to as agents. The commentators do not elaborate the category here, but its existence is what makes the verse's form intelligible rather than merely poetic.

The form is significant for what it implies about the fire's status. A thing addressed as capable of obedience is not an independent force but a creature under command. The verse therefore makes a theological point about the natural order in the act of describing the miracle.

This connects to the sūrah's treatment of creation at verses 30 to 33, where the heavens and the earth were described as separated and the celestial bodies as gliding in assigned orbits. The natural order is presented throughout as responsive to command, and verse 69 exhibits the responsiveness.

**The Verse and Verse 68**

Verses 68 and 69 form a pair: the command to burn and the command to be cool. The two commands are addressed in opposite directions — the people command regarding Abraham, and God commands the fire — and the pairing exhibits the difference in authority.

The sūrah's method is to place the two commands adjacent without comment. The reader sees a community mobilising to burn a man and a single Divine address that renders the mobilisation ineffective. The juxtaposition is the argument, and it requires no elaboration.

The economy is characteristic of the account. The command to burn occupies one verse; the Divine response occupies one verse; the outcome is recorded at verse 70. Three verses complete the incident, and the compression is possible because the juxtaposition does the work.

**The Deliverance and Its Meaning**

The deliverance is not merely a rescue but a sign. The people proposed to help their gods by burning the one who had exposed their incapacity; the fire did not burn him. The failure of the burning is a demonstration directed at the same audience as the breaking of the idols.

Al-Rāzī notes at verse 70 that Abraham's opponents were overcome by both the verbal argument and the miracle of his withstanding the fire unharmed. The two modes — argument and miracle — are identified as complementary, and the verse supplies the second.

The complementarity is what al-Qurṭubī identified at verse 57: after verbal argument, a different mode of demonstration. The breaking of the idols was the first demonstration; the fire is the second. The two together constitute the account's argumentative arc.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not describe Abraham's experience in the fire, nor does it record his response to the deliverance. The prayer reported by the commentators supplies something of the former, but the Qur'anic text gives only the address and its effect.

The omission is consistent with the account's method. The sūrah records the commands and the outcomes, not the interior states. What Abraham felt is not the matter; what God commanded and what followed is. The verse's brevity reflects the priority.

The omission also leaves the event as a sign rather than as an experience. A reader is given something to consider rather than something to feel with, and the considering is what the sūrah's method throughout invites.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:70

> **They desired to scheme against him, but We made them the greatest losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scheme Reversed**

*They desired to scheme against him, but We made them the greatest losers.* The verse records the outcome of the command at verse 68, and the reversal is exact. The people schemed; the scheming produced loss for them rather than for Abraham.

The word rendered *scheme* is the same used at verse 57, where Abraham said *I shall scheme against your idols*. The recurrence is deliberate: the people's scheme is set against Abraham's, and the outcomes are opposite. The verse does not note the parallel, but the shared vocabulary makes it available.

The phrase *the greatest losers* — *al-akhsarīn* — is superlative. The loss is not partial but maximal, and the superlative indicates that the outcome was not merely a failure but a reversal in which the schemers incurred what they intended for another.

**Overcome by Argument and Miracle**

Al-Rāzī notes that Abraham's opponents were overcome by both the verbal argument and the miracle of his withstanding the fire unharmed. The note identifies the two modes of the account and confirms that both contributed to the outcome.

The verbal argument occupied verses 52 to 56 and the demonstration verses 58 to 67; the miracle is recorded at verse 69. Al-Rāzī's note gathers the three into a single account of the opponents' being overcome, and the gathering indicates that the tradition read them as one movement.

The two modes are complementary in the way al-Qurṭubī identified at verse 57. Argument addresses the understanding; miracle addresses what argument cannot reach. The account uses both, and the verse records the combined effect — which is why the opponents are described as overcome rather than merely persuaded, since persuasion would have required only the first.

**The Verse as Summary**

The verse functions as a summary of the whole Abrahamic confrontation, gathering nineteen verses of exchange into a single statement of outcome. The question was asked, the appeal to the fathers was given, the judgment was declared, the demonstration was arranged, the acknowledgement was extracted, the reversion occurred, the burning was commanded, and the fire did not burn.

The summary is compressed into a single sentence, and the compression is possible because the preceding eighteen verses supplied the detail. The verse does not need to recount; it states the outcome, and the outcome is legible to a reader who has followed the account.

The sūrah's method is thus cumulative. Each verse performs one function, and the functions accumulate toward a summary that requires no elaboration. The verse's brevity is the design's success rather than a limitation.

**The Verse and Verse 41**

The verse completes the pattern verse 41 described: *Messengers were certainly mocked before thee. Then those who scoffed at them were beset by that which they used to mock.* The Abrahamic account supplies the instance, and the instance is exact.

The people mocked the claim that the idols could not act; they were beset by a fire that did not burn. What they mocked — the incapacity of their gods and the power of Abraham's God — became the thing that overcame them. The reversal is the pattern's operation.

The instance is what gives verse 41's consolation its content. The Prophet ﷺ was told that the mockers were beset; the account shows how, and the showing is more useful than the telling because it exhibits the mechanism rather than asserting the outcome.

**Loss as a Category**

The term *loss* is used elsewhere in the Qur'an of those whose effort produces nothing, and the superlative here indicates the extremity of the case. The people expended effort — consulting, convening, building, burning — and the effort produced the opposite of its intention.

The category is significant because it distinguishes loss from harm. The people were not merely defeated; they lost what they invested. The distinction matters for the verse's theological point, which concerns the futility of effort directed against a prophet rather than the punishment of the directors.

The sūrah does not elaborate the loss beyond the superlative. What follows is the deliverance at verse 71, which turns from the opponents' outcome to Abraham's. The verse's function is to close the confrontation, and the closing is achieved by the superlative alone.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Situation**

The verse is consolatory in the way verse 41 was. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the opposition to a prophet can be overcome, and that the overcoming comes through both argument and sign. The consolation is not a promise but a precedent, and the precedent is specific.

The specificity matters. The account does not say that opposition is always overcome; it shows one case in which it was, and shows the mechanisms. A reader can assess whether the mechanisms are available in his own case, and the assessment is left to him.

This is the form of comfort the sūrah offers throughout. At verse 25 that the message is not new; at verse 36 and 41 that the mockery is not unprecedented; here that the opposition can be overcome. None is a guarantee, and the absence of guarantee is what makes each credible.

**What the Verse Closes**

The verse closes the confrontation that began at verse 52. Nineteen verses of question, answer, judgment, demonstration, acknowledgement, reversion, and command are gathered into a single statement of outcome, and the gathering is the account's conclusion.

What follows turns from the confrontation to its consequences: the deliverance to a blessed land, the gift of Isaac and Jacob, and the making of them imams. The turn is from conflict to establishment, and the verse marks the transition.

The sūrah's design is evident in the transition. The confrontation was necessary but is not the account's purpose; the purpose is to establish the line of guidance that verses 72 and 73 will describe. The verse closes the means and opens the end.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:71

> **And We delivered him and Lot to the land that We blessed for all peoples.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Delivered to a Blessed Land**

*And We delivered him and Lot to the land that We blessed for all peoples.* The verse turns from the confrontation to its consequence, and the consequence is deliverance rather than victory. Abraham is not installed over his people; he is removed from them, and the removal is the deliverance.

The mention of Lot alongside Abraham is significant. Lot is not part of the confrontation recorded in the preceding verses; his inclusion indicates that the deliverance extends beyond the prophet to those with him. The sūrah will return to Lot at verse 74.

The phrase *for all peoples* — *li'l-ʿālamīn* — indicates that the blessing is not local or exclusive. The land is blessed for all, and the universality is stated without elaboration, which leaves its sense to be gathered from the sūrah's other uses of the term.

**Which Land**

The commentators record two identifications. Al-Ṭabarī says the land refers to the Levant (*Shām*); al-Qurṭubī says it refers to Makkah, where Abraham went to build the Kaʿbah with Ishmael, with a cross-reference to 2:127. Both readings are preserved without adjudication.

Al-Qurṭubī also records that the land from which they were delivered is reputed to be Mesopotamia (*ʿIrāq*). The identification of the origin complements the identifications of the destination, and the pairing gives the deliverance a geography.

The plurality of identifications is characteristic of the commentators' method. Where the Qur'an does not specify, the tradition preserves multiple reports without settling the question. The verse's argument does not depend on which land is meant, only on its being blessed.

**The Blessed Land as a Theme**

The commentary notes that the idea of a blessed land appears again at verse 81 in connection with Solomon, at 7:137 with regard to the Children of Israel, and at 17:1 in the context of the Prophet's Night Journey. The recurrence indicates that the theme is established rather than local.

The recurrence also connects the Abrahamic account to the sūrah's other narratives. Solomon at verse 81 and the Children of Israel at 7:137 are part of the same pattern of deliverance to a blessed land, and the pattern is what gives the individual instances their significance.

The cross-reference to 17:1 is particularly significant, since it connects Abraham's deliverance to the Prophet's Night Journey. The connection is not drawn by the commentators here, but the shared vocabulary makes it available to a reader who has the references in mind.

**Deliverance Rather Than Victory**

The verse's choice of *delivered* rather than a term denoting victory is significant. Abraham's opponents were made the greatest losers at verse 70, but Abraham is not described as prevailing over them. He is delivered from them, and the deliverance is removal rather than conquest.

The distinction matters for the account's theology. The prophet's vindication does not take the form of rule over his opponents; it takes the form of separation from them and establishment elsewhere. The sūrah does not present prophetic success as political triumph.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment throughout. At verse 34 the Prophet ﷺ was told that he would die as every human being dies; at verse 41 that the mockers would be beset. Neither promises political victory, and the pattern is what verse 71 continues.

**Lot's Inclusion**

The inclusion of Lot in the deliverance is the verse's most consequential detail for what follows. Lot is named here for the first time in this sūrah, and verse 74 will give his own account. The inclusion prepares the reader for the narrative that follows.

The pairing also indicates that deliverance is not individual. Abraham is delivered and Lot with him, and the with-him is what makes the deliverance the beginning of a community rather than the rescue of a man. The blessed land is blessed *for all peoples*, and the pairing enacts the universality.

The sūrah's method is to prepare narratives without announcing them. Lot is named in a verse about Abraham, and the naming is what makes his own account at verse 74 expected rather than abrupt. The economy is characteristic of the sūrah's design.

**The Verse in the Account's Structure**

The verse marks the transition from conflict to establishment. Verses 52 to 70 recorded the confrontation and its outcome; verse 71 records the consequence; verses 72 and 73 will record the gifts and the office. The structure moves from opposition to inheritance.

The transition is what gives the Abrahamic account its purpose in this sūrah. The confrontation is not included for its drama but to establish the line of guidance that follows, and the line is what the sūrah needs for its argument about the continuity of revelation.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is transitional. The confrontation occupied nineteen verses; the transition occupies one. The proportion indicates where the sūrah's interest lies, and the interest is in what follows rather than in what preceded.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the prophet's vindication takes the form of establishment rather than triumph. Abraham is delivered to a land blessed for all peoples, and the blessing is the ground on which what follows will be built. The gifts at verse 72 and the office at verse 73 presuppose the land.

The establishment is also what makes the account useful to the Prophet ﷺ. He is shown that a prophet may be removed from his people and established elsewhere, and that the establishment is the beginning rather than the end. The consolation is the pattern, and the pattern includes the removal.

The sūrah does not promise the Prophet ﷺ a similar deliverance. It shows that the pattern exists and that it produced a line of guidance. Whether the pattern will repeat is left open, and the openness is consistent with the sūrah's method throughout.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:72

> **And We bestowed upon him Isaac, and Jacob as an added gift. And each of them We made righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Isaac and Jacob**

*And We bestowed upon him Isaac, and Jacob as an added gift. And each of them We made righteous.* The verse records the gifts that followed the deliverance, and the gifts are persons rather than possessions. What is bestowed is a lineage, and the lineage is what verse 73 will describe as imams.

The order is significant. Isaac is named first and Jacob as an addition, and the commentators note the reasoning: the initial gift was Isaac, and Abraham had not prayed for a grandson. Al-Ṭabarī records this reading, and al-Qurṭubī supplies the reasoning.

Al-Ṭabarī also records an alternative reading in which the first part of the verse means *We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob as a gift*, treating both as a single gift. The two readings differ in whether Jacob is an addition or part of the original bestowal, and both are preserved.

**"As an Added Gift"**

The term rendered *as an added gift* is *nāfilan*, and al-Ṭabarī's note that it is thought to refer to Jacob only rests on the reasoning that Isaac was the gift prayed for and Jacob was not. The reading distinguishes between what was asked and what was given beyond the asking.

The distinction is theologically useful. It indicates that Divine bestowal exceeds request, and that the excess is itself a gift. The verse does not elaborate the point, but the term *nāfilan* carries it, and the commentators' gloss makes it explicit.

The alternative reading — that both were a single gift — does not contradict the point but locates it differently. On that reading the bestowal is undivided, and the addition is not marked grammatically. The commentators preserve both because the verse supports both.

**"Each of Them We Made Righteous"**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note that *each of them* means all three — Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob — and that each was made righteous. The inclusion of Abraham is significant: he is not the source of righteousness transmitting it to his descendants but a recipient of it alongside them.

The making is Divine. The verse does not say that they were righteous but that God made them righteous, and the making is what connects the verse to verse 51, where God gave Abraham sound judgment aforetime. The gifts are conferred throughout rather than achieved.

The conferral is what makes verse 73 possible. If righteousness were achieved, the office of imam would be a reward; because it is conferred, the office is a commission. The sūrah's consistent presentation of the gifts as given rather than earned is what gives the following verse its force.

**The Verse and Verse 51**

Verse 72 completes what verse 51 began. There God gave Abraham sound judgment aforetime and knew him; here He bestows Isaac and Jacob and makes all three righteous. The two verses frame the Abrahamic account, and the framing establishes that everything in between was conducted with conferred capacity.

The confrontation at verses 52 to 70 was therefore not a personal achievement but the exercise of a gift. The verse's statement that each was made righteous confirms this: the righteousness that sustained the confrontation was conferred, and the conferral is recorded at both ends of the account.

The sūrah's design is evident in the framing. Verse 51 establishes the gift before the confrontation; verse 72 confirms it after. The reader is not left to infer that Abraham's conduct was enabled; the enabling is stated twice.

**Lineage as Gift**
