The bestowal of Isaac and Jacob is a bestowal of lineage, and the lineage is what makes the account significant beyond Abraham's own life. Verse 73 will describe the three as imams guiding by God's command, and the imamate depends on the lineage the verse establishes.

The sūrah's interest in lineage is consistent with its argument about continuity. At verse 25 the claim was that every messenger before the Prophet ﷺ was given the same message; the Abrahamic lineage supplies a concrete instance of the continuity, and the instance is named rather than described in general terms.

The naming is what makes the instance useful. Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob are specific persons in a specific line, and the line is what connects the account to the Prophet ﷺ, who is understood to descend from it. The verse supplies the connection without drawing it.

**Righteousness as a Category**

The term rendered *righteous* — *ṣāliḥīn* — denotes those who are sound, upright, fit for their purpose. The verse's use of it for all three indicates a shared quality rather than a hierarchy, and the sharing is what makes the collective imamate at verse 73 intelligible.

The quality is conferred rather than achieved, and the conferral is what distinguishes it from moral accomplishment. The verse does not describe their conduct; it states that God made them righteous, and the stating is sufficient for the argument that follows.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the prophets throughout. Their capacities are given, their offices are conferred, and their conduct is the exercise of what was given. The presentation preserves the priority of the gift over the achievement.

**What the Verse Prepares**

The verse prepares the statement at verse 73 that the three were made imams guiding by God's command. The righteousness conferred at verse 72 is the qualification for the office, and the office is what the sūrah needs for its argument about the continuity of guidance.

The preparation is economical. The verse states the gifts and the righteousness in a single sentence, and the sentence supplies everything verse 73 requires. The sūrah does not elaborate the righteousness because the office, not the quality, is its interest.

The economy is characteristic of the account's closing verses. The confrontation occupied nineteen verses; the gifts and the office occupy two. The proportion indicates that the confrontation was the means and the establishment of guidance is the end.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:73

> **And We made them leaders guiding people by Our command, and We inspired them to do good deeds, establish prayer, and give almsgiving, and they used to worship Us only.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Made Imams**

*And We made them imams, guiding according to Our Command.* The verse states the office conferred on Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and the commentators note that on the concept of *imams* the reader should see also 2:124 and 17:71. Al-Ṭabarī explains that their being made imams means they were the ones who were followed and imitated.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī add that they taught and guided people in matters of religion and good deeds. The two glosses are complementary: the first identifies the imams' position, the second their function. Together they define the office as one of being followed in matters of religion.

The making is Divine, as at verse 72. The imamate is not achieved by the three but conferred on them, and the conferral is what connects the office to the righteousness conferred in the preceding verse. The sequence is: made righteous, then made imams.

**Guiding According to Our Command**

The phrase *guiding according to Our Command* — *yahdūna bi-amrinā* — limits the guidance. The imams guide, but the guidance is according to God's command rather than their own judgment. The limitation is the verse's most important qualification.

The limitation connects to verse 27, where the angels were described as not preceding God in speech and acting according to His Command. The same phrase is used of the imams, and the parallel indicates that the prophets' guidance is of the same order as the angels' obedience — responsive rather than originating.

The parallel is theologically significant. It places the prophets within the created order rather than above it, and it defines their authority as derived. The imams are followed because they convey a command that is not their own, and the conveying is what qualifies them.

**The Three Things Revealed**

*And We revealed unto them the doing of good deeds, the performance of prayer, and the giving of alms.* The verse specifies the content of the guidance, and the three items are practical rather than doctrinal. Good deeds, prayer, and alms are acts rather than propositions.

The specification is significant for what it indicates about the guidance's character. The imams guide in matters of practice, and the practice is named. Al-Qurṭubī's and al-Rāzī's gloss that they taught and guided people in matters of religion and good deeds is confirmed by the specification.

The three items are also the substance of the religious life the sūrah describes elsewhere. Prayer and alms appear throughout the Qur'an as the paired acts of worship, and good deeds is the general category under which both fall. The verse gathers them without elaboration.

**"They Were Worshippers of Us"**

The verse closes: *And they were worshippers of Us.* The clause returns to the theme with which the sūrah opened its treatment of the angels — worship as the defining activity of the highest created beings. The imams are described in the same terms.

The description is significant because it places the prophets in the posture of worship rather than of authority. They guide, but they are also worshippers, and the worship is what qualifies the guidance. The verse's closing clause is therefore not a summary but a qualification.

The connection to verses 19 and 20 is deliberate. There the angels glorify night and day without tiring; here the imams are worshippers of God. The parallel establishes that worship is not beneath the prophets but is their defining relation, and the relation is what makes their guidance trustworthy.

**The Verse and Verse 25**

Verse 73 supplies the concrete instance of the claim made at verse 25: *We sent no messenger before thee, save that We revealed unto him, "Verily, there is no god but I; so worship Me!"* The three imams were revealed to, and what was revealed included worship.

The instance confirms the claim's specificity. The verse does not say that the imams were given a general revelation but names its content — good deeds, prayer, alms — and the naming shows that the revelation at verse 25 was not abstract but practical.

The confirmation is what the sūrah needs. The claim at verse 25 was general and could be dismissed as vague; the instance at verse 73 is specific and cannot. The sūrah supplies the instance because the argument requires it.

**The Imams and the Prophet**

The verse's description of the imams is consolatory in a specific way. The Prophet ﷺ is shown that the office he holds has precedents, that the precedents were conferred rather than achieved, and that the office includes being a worshipper rather than only a guide.

The last point is the most significant. A prophet who is also a worshipper is not above the requirements he conveys, and the sūrah's insistence on the point is consistent with its treatment of the angels at verses 19 to 28. Worship is the defining relation for all created beings, including the prophets.

The consolation is not that the office is easy but that it is shared. The imams guided according to God's command and worshipped Him, and the Prophet ﷺ does the same. The pattern is what the sūrah offers, and the pattern is specific enough to be useful.

**What the Verse Completes**

The verse completes the Abrahamic account. The confrontation began at verse 52 and ended at verse 70; the deliverance came at verse 71; the gifts at verse 72; and the office at verse 73. Twenty-two verses move from a question about idols to the establishment of a line of guidance.

The movement is what gives the account its purpose in this sūrah. The confrontation is not included for its drama but to establish the line, and the line is what connects Abraham to the Prophet ﷺ. The verse's closing clause — *they were worshippers of Us* — is the account's final word.

The sūrah's design is evident in the proportion. The confrontation occupies nineteen verses; the establishment occupies three. The proportion indicates that the conflict was the means and the guidance is the end, and the verse marks the end.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:74

> **And Lot—We granted him sound judgment and knowledge, and We delivered him from the town that was doing shameful deeds. Verily, they were a wicked and vile nation.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Judgment and Knowledge Given to Lot**

*And as for Lot, We gave unto him judgment and knowledge, and We saved him from the town that was committing vile deeds.* Al-Rāzī notes that the verse continues *And We revealed unto them* from verse 73, so that the sense is *And We revealed unto them and unto Lot*. Al-Qurṭubī records an alternative reading: *And remember Lot. We gave unto him judgment and knowledge.*

The two readings differ in whether Lot is included in the preceding statement or introduced separately, and both are preserved. The first makes him part of the Abrahamic group; the second gives him his own introduction. The sūrah's placement between the two accounts supports either.

Al-Rāzī explains that *judgment* — *ḥukm* — refers to the ability to decide between disputants, and notes that other prophets are also described as being given *ḥukm*, at 3:79, 6:89, 12:22, 19:12, and 45:16. The recurrence indicates that the term denotes a conferred capacity rather than an acquired skill.

**Saved From the Town**

*And We saved him from the town that was committing vile deeds.* The deliverance is stated without narration, and the compression is deliberate. The commentators direct the reader to the fuller accounts at 11:77–83, 7:80–84, 15:57–77, 26:160–173, 27:54–58, 29:28–35, and 54:33–38.

The list is long, and its length indicates that Lot's story is among the Qur'an's most frequently recounted. The present account gives the gifts and the deliverance in a single verse, and relies on the reader's familiarity with the fuller versions elsewhere.

The choice of *the town that was committing vile deeds* rather than a name is significant. The town is identified by its practice rather than by its location, and the identification is what the verse's judgment rests on.

**"Truly They Were an Evil People, Iniquitous"**

The closing clause states the judgment, and the doubling of terms — *evil people, iniquitous* — is emphatic. The people are not merely wrong but wrong in a way that warrants two descriptions, and the doubling indicates that a single term would not carry the judgment.

The judgment is stated by the sūrah rather than by Lot. The verse does not record Lot's accusation; it records the Divine assessment, and the assessment is what authorises the deliverance. The order is significant: the deliverance is stated first, the judgment second.

The sūrah's method here is consistent with its treatment of the other narratives. The prophet is delivered, the people are judged, and the narration is compressed to the point where the fuller account must be supplied from elsewhere. The compression serves the sūrah's purpose, which is the pattern rather than the story.

**The Verse and Verse 73**

Al-Rāzī's reading that the verse continues from verse 73 connects Lot to the imams. On this reading Lot received the same revelation as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and his inclusion extends the group rather than beginning a new account.

Al-Qurṭubī's alternative reading gives Lot his own introduction, which separates him from the group and treats his account as parallel rather than continuous. The two readings produce different structures, and the commentators preserve both.

The ambiguity does not affect the verse's content. On either reading Lot was given judgment and knowledge and was delivered from a town committing vile deeds. The structural question concerns his relation to the preceding group rather than his own status.

**Ḥukm as a Conferred Capacity**

Al-Rāzī's gloss that *ḥukm* is the ability to decide between disputants identifies it as a practical capacity rather than a theoretical one. The prophet is given the ability to adjudicate, and the ability is conferred rather than developed.

The cross-references confirm the pattern: 3:79, 6:89, 12:22, 19:12, and 45:16 all describe prophets as given *ḥukm*. The recurrence indicates that the capacity is a standard feature of the prophetic office rather than a personal endowment.

This connects to verse 72, where the three were made righteous, and verse 73, where they were made imams. The conferral is the sūrah's consistent presentation: the prophets' capacities are given, and their conduct is the exercise of what was given.

**The Verse and the Pattern**

The verse supplies another instance of the pattern the sūrah has been establishing. A prophet is given capacity, confronts a people, and is delivered while the people are judged. Abraham's account occupied twenty-two verses; Lot's occupies one.

The compression is possible because the pattern is established. A reader who has followed the Abrahamic account does not need the narration; the gifts and the deliverance are sufficient, and the fuller accounts elsewhere supply the detail.

The sūrah's method is thus economical. It develops one account at length to establish the pattern, then compresses the subsequent instances to their essential elements. The proportion indicates that the pattern is the interest rather than the individual stories.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the pattern extends beyond Abraham. Lot received the same gifts and the same deliverance, and the extension is what makes the pattern general rather than particular. The sūrah will continue with Noah, David, Solomon, Job, and others.

The extension is what the sūrah needs for its argument about continuity. At verse 25 the claim was that every messenger before the Prophet ﷺ was given the same message; the sequence of accounts from verse 74 onward supplies the instances.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is to add an instance. The instance is added in a single verse, and the adding is sufficient. What follows continues the sequence.

---

**The Compression and Its Reason**

The verse gives in one sentence what occupies eighteen verses in Sūrah 26 and fourteen in Sūrah 27. The compression is not a lack of interest but a difference of purpose: those sūrahs narrate the confrontation, while this one supplies an instance of a pattern already established at length through Abraham.

The commentators' list of seven parallel passages indicates that a reader would have the narrative available elsewhere. The sūrah relies on that availability and states only what its own argument requires — the gifts conferred, the deliverance granted, and the judgment on the town.

This is why the verse can open with *And as for Lot* without introduction. The reader knows who Lot is and what befell his town; what the sūrah adds is his place in the sequence of prophets given judgment and knowledge, and the adding requires only a clause.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:75

> **And We caused him to enter Our mercy, for among the later generations he is of the righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Entering the Mercy**

*And We caused him to enter Our Mercy; verily, he was among the righteous.* The commentators note that entering into God's Mercy is an idea mentioned at 4:175, 7:151, 9:99, 42:8, 45:30, 48:25, and 76:31. The recurrence indicates that the phrase is an established idiom rather than a local image.

The idiom denotes inclusion rather than acquisition. One enters mercy as one enters a place, and the entering is caused by God rather than achieved by the one who enters. The verse's verb — *We caused him to enter* — makes the causation explicit.

The connection to the preceding verse is immediate. Lot was saved from the town; he was then caused to enter the Mercy. The deliverance and the inclusion are two aspects of a single act, and the verse states the second.

**The Commentators' Two Readings**

Al-Rāzī records that in this instance some interpret the Mercy to mean the status of prophethood, while others say it refers to the rewards given to him by God in the Hereafter. Both readings are preserved, and they locate the Mercy in different realms.

The prophethood reading connects the Mercy to the gifts stated at verse 74 — judgment and knowledge. On this reading the entering is the conferral of the office, and the verse restates what the preceding verse described.

The Hereafter reading connects the Mercy to what follows death. On this reading the deliverance from the town was temporal and the entering is eschatological, and the verse extends Lot's account beyond his earthly deliverance.

**Why Both Readings Are Preserved**

The commentators do not adjudicate between the two readings, and the non-adjudication is instructive. The phrase *Our Mercy* is general enough to bear both, and the verse's argument does not depend on which is meant.

What both readings share is that the Mercy is conferred rather than earned. Whether it is prophethood or the rewards of the Hereafter, it is given by God, and the giving is what the verse asserts. The ambiguity concerns the referent rather than the relation.

This is characteristic of the commentators' method. Where the Qur'an does not specify, the tradition preserves multiple reports without settling the question. The verse's force is not diminished by the ambiguity because the force lies in the conferral.

**"Verily He Was Among the Righteous"**

The closing clause states the ground of the inclusion, and the ground is righteousness. The term *ṣāliḥīn* is the same used at verse 72 of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and the shared term places Lot in the same category.

The righteousness is stated as a fact rather than argued. The verse does not describe Lot's conduct; it states that he was among the righteous, and the stating is sufficient for the inclusion that precedes it.

The order is worth noting. The entering is stated first and the righteousness second, which might suggest that the righteousness is the consequence rather than the ground. The commentators do not resolve the question, and the verse's syntax leaves it open.

**The Verse and Verse 74**

Verses 74 and 75 form a unit: the gifts and the deliverance, then the inclusion in the Mercy. The two verses complete Lot's account, and the completion is achieved in two verses against the twenty-two occupied by Abraham's.

The compression is possible because the pattern is established and the fuller accounts exist elsewhere. The commentators' list of seven parallel passages indicates that a reader would have the detail available, and the sūrah relies on that availability.

The unit's brevity is the sūrah's method. One account is developed at length to establish the pattern; subsequent instances are compressed to their essentials. The proportion indicates that the pattern is the interest.

**The Mercy as a Category**

The commentators' list of parallel passages indicates that entering God's Mercy is a general category rather than a particular reward. The phrase appears in contexts of belief, repentance, and reward, and its range is broad.

The breadth is what makes the phrase useful here. The verse does not need to specify whether the Mercy is prophethood or eschatological reward because the category covers both, and the covering is what allows the commentators to preserve both readings.

The category also connects to the sūrah's treatment of God's attributes. At verse 42 the name *al-Raḥmān* was used, and al-Rāzī noted that it causes one to reflect that God encompasses all things with His Mercy. The Mercy Lot enters is the same encompassing Mercy.

**What the Verse Completes**

The verse completes Lot's account and prepares the transition to Noah at verse 76. The sequence of prophets continues, and each instance is compressed to the gifts, the deliverance, and the inclusion.

The pattern is now established across two accounts: Abraham at length, Lot in two verses. What follows will continue the compression, and the compression is possible because the reader has the pattern.

The sūrah's design is evident in the proportion. The pattern required development; the instances require only statement. The verse's brevity is the design's success rather than a limitation.

---

**The Idiom of Entering**

The phrase *We caused him to enter Our Mercy* uses a spatial idiom for a relational reality, and the commentators' list of seven parallel passages shows the idiom is established rather than invented here. One enters mercy as one enters a place, and the entering is caused rather than achieved.

The causation is the verse's emphasis. Lot did not attain the Mercy; he was caused to enter it. The formulation is consistent with the sūrah's presentation throughout — the prophets' capacities and stations are conferred, and their conduct is the exercise of what was conferred.

The closing clause *verily he was among the righteous* states the character of the one who entered, and the same term was used at verse 72 of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. The shared vocabulary places Lot in the same company, and the placing is what the two verses together achieve.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:76

> **And Noah, when he cried out to Us before, We answered him and We delivered him from the grieved people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Noah Cried Out Aforetime**

*And [remember] Noah, when he cried out aforetime, We answered him and saved him and his family from great distress.* The verse introduces Noah, and the commentators note that before Abraham and his progeny he was saved from the punishment meted out to his people in the form of the flood.

The word *aforetime* — *nādhā qablu* — places Noah before the sequence the sūrah has been recounting. The order of the accounts is not chronological in the strict sense, since Abraham preceded Noah in the narration, but the verse marks Noah's priority in time.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī identify the distress as the flood; al-Rāzī adds that it can also be interpreted to include the mockery and insults Noah and his followers suffered. The two readings are preserved, and they locate the distress differently.

**The Distress and Its Two Readings**

Al-Rāzī's reading that the distress includes the mockery and insults is significant because it extends the deliverance beyond the physical. On this reading Noah was saved not only from the flood but from the suffering of being mocked, and the suffering is treated as a distress requiring deliverance.

The reading is theologically useful. It indicates that the hardship of prophetic mission is not merely a precondition of deliverance but a form of distress from which deliverance is needed. The verse's phrase *great distress* is broad enough to cover both.

The commentators preserve both readings without adjudicating, and the preservation indicates that the tradition did not consider the question settled. The verse's force does not depend on which is meant, since both are forms of distress requiring Divine response.

**"We Answered Him"**

The verse's central claim is that the cry was answered. Noah cried out and God answered, and the answering is what the deliverance consists in. The verse does not describe the cry's content; it states the fact of the answer.

The omission is consistent with the sūrah's method. The fuller accounts at 7:59–64, 10:71–73, 11:25–48, 23:23–30, 26:105–121, 37:75–82, 54:9–15, and Sūrah 71 supply the content. The present verse states the answer and relies on the reader's familiarity.

The answer is what connects the verse to the sūrah's treatment of prayer elsewhere. At verse 68 the commentators reported Abraham's prayer as he was cast into the fire; here Noah's cry is answered. The two are instances of the same relation.

**Family or People**

Al-Rāzī notes that *family* renders *ahl*, which can also mean people, and that *his family* can thus be understood to mean his people — those who followed the religion he taught. The reading extends the deliverance beyond blood relations.

The reading is significant for the sūrah's theology. If the family is defined by faith rather than by descent, then the deliverance is available to anyone who follows the prophet's teaching. The commentators' note makes this explicit.

The alternative reading — that the family means blood relations — is not excluded, and the commentators preserve both. The ambiguity is characteristic of the term *ahl*, and the verse does not resolve it.

**The Verse and the Sequence**

Verse 76 continues the sequence of prophetic accounts that began at verse 74. Abraham was developed at length; Lot was compressed to two verses; Noah is introduced here and completed at verse 77. The compression continues.

The sequence is what the sūrah needs for its argument about continuity. At verse 25 the claim was that every messenger before the Prophet ﷺ was given the same message; the accounts supply the instances, and each instance confirms the claim.

The sūrah's method is economical. One account establishes the pattern; the subsequent instances state the essentials. The proportion indicates that the pattern is the interest rather than the individual narratives.

**The Verse and Verse 41**

The verse connects to verse 41's statement that messengers were mocked before the Prophet ﷺ and that those who scoffed were beset by what they mocked. Noah is the paradigmatic instance, and al-Rāzī's reading that the distress included mockery makes the connection explicit.

The instance is what gives verse 41's consolation its content. The Prophet ﷺ was told that the mockers were beset; Noah's account shows how, and the showing is more useful than the telling because it exhibits the mechanism.

The connection is not drawn by the commentators here, but the shared vocabulary makes it available. The mockery, the cry, the answer, and the drowning are the elements verse 41 described, and the verse supplies them in sequence.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the pattern extends to the earliest prophet. Noah was given the answer, the deliverance, and the saving of his family, and the extension confirms that the pattern is general rather than particular.

The extension is what the sūrah's argument requires. The claim at verse 25 was universal; the instances from verse 74 onward supply the confirmation, and Noah's priority in time makes the confirmation as broad as the claim.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is to add an instance. The instance is added in a single verse, and the adding is sufficient. Verse 77 completes it.

---

**The Cry and the Answer**

The verse's structure is cause and effect in a single sentence: Noah cried out, and God answered. The sūrah does not record the cry's content, relying on the eight fuller accounts the commentators list, and the omission focuses attention on the answering rather than the asking.

Al-Rāzī's reading that the distress included the mockery and insults Noah and his followers suffered extends the answer beyond physical rescue. On this reading the deliverance addresses the hardship of the mission itself, and the hardship is treated as a distress requiring Divine response rather than as its ordinary cost.

The reading is what connects the verse to the sūrah's consolation of the Prophet ﷺ. At verse 41 he was told that messengers were mocked before him; here the mockery is named as part of the distress from which Noah was saved. The two verses together indicate that the deliverance includes relief from the mockery and not only from the flood.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:77

> **And We helped him against those people who denied Our signs. Truly they were an evil people; so We drowned them all together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Helped Against the Deniers**

*And We helped him against those people who denied Our signs.* The verse states the form of the answer to Noah's cry, and the form is assistance against his opponents rather than merely rescue from them. The help is directed at the deniers, and the direction is the verse's emphasis.

The phrase *those people who denied Our signs* identifies the opponents by their denial, and the denial is of the signs rather than of Noah personally. The distinction matters: the conflict is about the signs, and Noah is their bearer rather than their source.

The verse completes the answer stated at verse 76. There the cry was answered; here the answer's form is given. The two verses together describe a single Divine response with two aspects — deliverance for Noah and assistance against his opponents.

**"Truly They Were an Evil People"**

The judgment is stated in the same terms used of Lot's people at verse 74: *Truly they were an evil people.* The repetition is deliberate and connects the two accounts. The people are judged in the same words, and the repetition indicates that the judgment is of a kind rather than particular.

The judgment authorises what follows. The drowning is not presented as a response to injury done to Noah but as a consequence of the denial and the evil. The verse states the ground before stating the outcome, and the order is significant.

The sūrah's method is consistent. At verse 74 Lot's people were judged evil and iniquitous; here Noah's are judged evil. The judgments are stated by the sūrah rather than by the prophets, and the stating is what authorises the outcomes.

**Drowned All Together**

*So We drowned them all together.* The phrase *all together* — *ajmaʿīn* — indicates that the drowning was complete and simultaneous. The commentators identify the flood as the punishment meted out to Noah's people, and the completeness is the verse's emphasis.

The completeness connects to verse 41's statement that those who scoffed were beset by what they mocked. The mocking was collective and the drowning was collective, and the correspondence is exact. The verse supplies the instance verse 41 described.

The verse does not elaborate the drowning. The fuller accounts at 7:59–64, 11:25–48, 26:105–121, 54:9–15, and Sūrah 71 supply the detail. The present verse states the outcome and relies on the reader's familiarity with the narrative.

**The Two Aspects of the Answer**

Verses 76 and 77 together describe the answer to Noah's cry, and the answer has two aspects: deliverance for Noah and his family, and destruction for the deniers. The two are not alternatives but complements, and the verses state them in sequence.

The sequence is significant. The deliverance is stated first and the destruction second, which places the prophet's safety before the opponents' punishment. The order indicates that the deliverance is the primary purpose and the punishment its consequence.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment throughout. At verse 70 Abraham's opponents were made the greatest losers, but the verse follows the deliverance at verse 69. The pattern is: the prophet is saved, and the opponents' fate follows.

**The Verse and Verse 41**

The verse is the clearest instance of the pattern verse 41 described. *Messengers were certainly mocked before thee. Then those who scoffed at them were beset by that which they used to mock.* Noah's people mocked the warning; they were drowned by the water the warning concerned.

The correspondence is exact, and the sūrah does not draw it. The verse states the drowning without noting that it is the fulfilment of the pattern, and the omission leaves the recognition to the reader.

The instance is what makes verse 41's consolation concrete. The Prophet ﷺ was told that the mockers were beset; Noah's account shows the besetting in its most complete form, and the showing is what gives the consolation its weight.

**The Verse and the Sequence**

Verse 77 completes Noah's account, which occupied two verses against the twenty-two of Abraham's. The compression continues, and the compression is possible because the pattern is established and the fuller accounts exist elsewhere.

The sequence from verse 74 onward is now: Lot, Noah. What follows will continue with David and Solomon at verse 78, and the compression will continue. The sūrah's method is to state the essentials of each instance and rely on the reader's familiarity.

The proportion indicates the sūrah's interest. The pattern required development in one account; the instances require only statement. The verse's brevity is the design's success rather than a limitation.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the pattern's outcome is consistent. The prophet is delivered, the opponents are judged, and the judgment takes the form appropriate to the denial. Noah's drowning corresponds to Lot's deliverance from the town, and both correspond to Abraham's fire.

The consistency is what the sūrah needs for its argument. The claim at verse 41 was that those who scoffed were beset; the instances from verse 74 onward confirm the claim, and the confirmation is what makes the consolation credible.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is to complete an instance. The instance is completed in a single verse, and the completing is sufficient. What follows continues the sequence.

---

**The Pattern Fulfilled**

The verse is the sūrah's clearest fulfilment of the pattern stated at verse 41: those who scoffed at the messengers were beset by that which they used to mock. Noah's people mocked the warning of a punishment by water, and water was what came. The correspondence is exact and the sūrah leaves it uncommented.

The completeness of the drowning — *all together* — is what makes the instance paradigmatic. There is no partial fulfilment and no exception; the mocking community is destroyed as a whole, and the wholeness is what verse 41's claim requires if it is to serve as consolation.

The verse's compression is therefore functional. It states the help against the deniers, the judgment on them, and the drowning in a single sentence, and the sentence supplies everything the pattern requires. The fuller accounts narrate the event; this verse exhibits its structure.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:78

> **And David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which the flock of a certain person had strayed at night; and We were witnesses to their judgment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Judgment Regarding the Tillage**

*And [remember] David and Solomon, when they rendered judgment regarding the tillage, when the people's sheep strayed therein by night.* The verse introduces a new type of account: not a confrontation with a people but a judicial decision. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *tillage* refers to crops or an orchard, and that the animals wandered in unguarded and destroyed the vegetation.

The setting is specific and mundane. A flock strays at night and destroys a field, and two prophets adjudicate the resulting dispute. The sūrah's inclusion of this account among the prophetic narratives indicates that adjudication is part of the prophetic office.

The phrase *by night* is significant. The straying occurred when the field was unguarded, which means neither party was at fault in the ordinary sense. The dispute concerns compensation rather than blame, and the distinction is what makes the judgment interesting.

**The Two Accounts of the Judgment**

Al-Ṭabarī records one account: David rendered judgment and awarded the sheep to the owner of the field as compensation, but Solomon intervened and recommended that the sheep be given to the farmer until the land returned to its previous state, to which David gladly agreed.

Al-Qurṭubī records another version: Solomon recommended that the two owners exchange places, so that the shepherd would care for the field until it was restored, and in the meantime the farmer would benefit from the milk and wool of the flock.

The two versions differ in the mechanism but agree in the substance: Solomon's judgment preserved both parties' interests rather than transferring the loss entirely to one. The commentators preserve both without adjudicating between them.

**What the Qur'an Does Not Transmit**

The commentary notes that the Qur'an itself transmits none of these details, not even that there was a disagreement between the two prophets. The caution is important: the versions are tradition rather than text, and the verse states only that both rendered judgment and that God was Witness.

The distinction matters for how the account is to be read. The Qur'an asserts the fact of the judgment and God's witnessing; the elaborations supply the content. The commentators preserve the elaborations while marking their status, and the marking is part of their method.

This is the same caution al-Rāzī offered at verse 68 regarding the Nimrod attribution. The tradition records elaborations while noting that they lack direct Qur'anic basis, and the noting is what distinguishes scholarly commentary from narrative expansion.

**"We Were Witness to Their Judgment"**

The closing clause states that God witnessed the judgment, and the witnessing is the verse's theological point. The judgment was rendered before God, and the witnessing indicates that it was subject to assessment.

The clause also prepares verse 79, which states that God made Solomon to understand it. The witnessing and the understanding are connected: God witnessed the judgment and conferred the understanding that produced the better ruling.

The verse does not state which judgment was better, and the omission is significant. The Qur'an asserts the fact of the judgment and God's witnessing; the assessment is left to verse 79, which states the conferral without condemning the alternative.

**The Verse and the Prophetic Office**

The account extends the conception of the prophetic office beyond proclamation to adjudication. The prophets are not only messengers but judges, and the judging is part of what God confers. Al-Rāzī's gloss at verse 74 that *ḥukm* is the ability to decide between disputants is confirmed here.

The extension is significant for the sūrah's argument. At verse 73 the imams were described as guiding according to God's command; here two prophets adjudicate a concrete dispute. The guidance is not abstract but includes the resolution of particular cases.

The account also connects to verse 79, where both are described as given judgment and knowledge. The conferral is stated after the narrative, and the stating confirms that the adjudication was an exercise of conferred capacity rather than personal opinion.

**The Verse and the Sequence**

Verse 78 introduces a different type of account into the sequence. The preceding narratives concerned confrontation and deliverance; this one concerns adjudication. The variety indicates that the prophetic office has multiple aspects, and the sūrah supplies instances of each.

The sequence from verse 74 onward is now: Lot, Noah, David and Solomon. What follows will continue with Job and others, and the variety will continue. The sūrah's method is to supply instances rather than to develop a single type.

The variety is what the sūrah's argument requires. The claim at verse 25 was that every messenger was given the same message; the instances show that the message was delivered in multiple registers — proclamation, confrontation, adjudication — and the variety confirms the claim's breadth.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the prophetic office includes adjudication, and that the adjudication is witnessed by God. The two claims are stated in a single verse, and the stating is sufficient for the account that follows at verse 79.

The verse's brevity reflects the fact that its function is to introduce the instance. The judgment is described in a single clause, the elaborations are left to tradition, and the theological point — God's witnessing — is stated without elaboration.

The sūrah's method is consistent. It states the essentials and relies on the reader's familiarity for the detail. The verse's compression is possible because the account is well known, and the compression serves the sūrah's purpose, which is the pattern rather than the story.

---

**Adjudication as Prophetic Work**

The account's inclusion among the prophetic narratives extends the office beyond proclamation. The prophets confront, warn, and deliver; here they also adjudicate a dispute between two parties with no villain in it. The straying occurred at night, the field was unguarded, and the loss was nobody's fault.

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss that *tillage* refers to crops or an orchard, and that the animals wandered in unguarded, establishes the case's ordinariness. This is not a matter of principle but of compensation, and its inclusion indicates that the prophetic office extends to the resolution of ordinary disputes.

The commentators' caution that the Qur'an transmits none of the details — not even that the two prophets disagreed — is methodologically important. The verse asserts the judgment and God's witnessing; the mechanism belongs to tradition, and the tradition is preserved with its status marked rather than absorbed into the text.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:79

> **We made Solomon to understand it, and unto both We gave judgment and knowledge. We compelled the mountains and the birds to glorify along with David; We did this.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Solomon Made to Understand**

*We made Solomon to understand it.* Al-Ṭabarī explains that this refers to the judgment he gave that was adopted by David. The conferral is specific: Solomon was made to understand the particular case, and the understanding produced the ruling that prevailed.

The making is Divine, and the verse does not say that Solomon reasoned better but that God made him understand. The conferral is consistent with the sūrah's presentation throughout: the prophets' capacities are given rather than achieved.

The verse does not disparage David's judgment. It states that Solomon was made to understand it, and the stating is sufficient. The commentators' accounts record that David adopted Solomon's ruling gladly, and the adoption indicates that the better ruling was recognised rather than imposed.

**"Unto Both We Gave Judgment and Knowledge"**

The clause is significant because it gives both prophets the same gifts. Solomon was made to understand the particular case; both were given judgment and knowledge. The distinction is between a specific conferral and a general one, and the verse makes both.

The equality is theologically important. The sūrah does not present Solomon as superior to David but as given a specific understanding in a specific case. The general gifts are shared, and the sharing is what the clause asserts.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the prophets. At verse 72 Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob were each made righteous; here David and Solomon are each given judgment and knowledge. The gifts are conferred on each, and the conferral does not create a hierarchy.

**The Mountains and the Birds**

*We compelled the mountains and the birds to glorify along with David.* The commentators note that the birds' glorification with David is also mentioned at 27:16, 34:10, and 38:19. The recurrence indicates that this is an established feature of the Davidic account rather than a local detail.

Al-Qurṭubī records two readings of the mountains' glorification: that when David glorified God the mountains would echo in response, or that David could command the mountains to do so. The two readings differ in whether the mountains respond or obey, and both are preserved.

Al-Rāzī offers a third reading: the mountains glorified God in accord with the universal sense of 17:44 — *And there is no thing, save that it hymns His praise, though you do not understand their praise* — but David possessed an awareness of this glorification that intensified his own certainty and glorification.

**Al-Rāzī's Reading and 17:44**

Al-Rāzī's reading is the most theologically developed. On his account the mountains were already glorifying, as 17:44 asserts of all things; what was conferred on David was awareness of the glorification, and the awareness intensified his own.

The reading preserves the universality of 17:44 while accounting for the specificity of David's case. All things glorify; David was given to perceive it. The miracle is not the mountains' glorification but David's awareness of it.

The reading also connects to the sūrah's treatment of creation at verses 30 to 33, where the natural order was presented as responsive to command. The glorification of all things is the general case; David's awareness is the particular instance.

**"We Did This"**

The verse closes with *We did this* — *wa-kunnā fāʿilīn* — and the clause asserts Divine agency over everything just described. The understanding given to Solomon, the judgment and knowledge given to both, and the compelling of the mountains and birds are all Divine acts.

The assertion is the verse's theological summary. The sūrah does not present the prophets' capacities as achievements or the natural order's response as coincidence; it states that God did these things, and the stating gathers the verse's content into a single claim.

The clause is characteristic of the sūrah's method. It describes and then asserts the agency behind the description, and the assertion is what gives the description its significance. Without the clause, the account would be a narrative; with it, it is a statement about Divine action.

**The Verse and Verse 78**

Verse 79 completes the account begun at verse 78. There the judgment was rendered and God witnessed; here the understanding is conferred and the agency asserted. The two verses together describe a single event and its theological significance.

The completion is economical. The judgment occupied one verse; the conferral and the assertion occupy one. The proportion indicates that the sūrah's interest is in the conferral rather than in the narrative detail.

The connection between the two verses is the witnessing. God witnessed the judgment at verse 78 and conferred the understanding at verse 79, and the connection indicates that the conferral was responsive to the case rather than general.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse establishes that the prophets' capacities are conferred and that the natural order responds to Divine command. The two claims are stated in a single verse, and the stating is what the sūrah's argument requires.

The claims connect to the sūrah's earlier treatment. At verse 27 the angels act according to God's Command; at verse 33 the celestial bodies glide in orbits; here the mountains and birds glorify along with David. The natural order and the prophets are alike in being responsive.

The connection is what gives the account its significance. David is not above the natural order but within it, and both are under command. The verse's closing assertion — *We did this* — gathers both into a single statement of Divine agency.

---

**Awareness Rather Than Alteration**

Al-Rāzī's third reading is the most economical of the three because it requires no change in the mountains. On the account of 17:44 all things already hymn God's praise; what David received was awareness of a glorification already occurring, and the awareness intensified his own certainty.

The reading preserves both the universality of 17:44 and the specificity of David's case. Nothing is added to the natural order; something is added to the prophet's perception. The miracle is located in awareness rather than in alteration, which is a more restrained account than the echoing or the commanding.

The restraint is characteristic of al-Rāzī's method and is why the commentators preserve his reading alongside the others. The verse does not specify the mechanism, and a reading that requires the least departure from established doctrine is available without excluding the more dramatic accounts.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:80

> **And We taught him how to make garments for you to protect you against your own might, but are you thankful?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**We Taught Him How to Make Garments**

The verse reports a Divine instruction in a practical craft, and the verb carries the same weight it carried at verse 74, where God gave Lot judgment and knowledge and taught him something. The teaching here concerns armour, and the sūrah presents it as a conferred skill rather than an acquired one.

Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī note that *garments* renders *labūs*, a term that can denote any weapon, though its root *l-b-s* pertains more properly to something worn. The commentators preserve both possibilities rather than choosing, since the word's range covers defensive equipment generally.

The dual range matters for interpretation. If *labūs* is a weapon, the teaching is martial; if it is something worn, the teaching is a craft of manufacture. The verse's reference to 34:11 — *Make coats of mail, and measure well the links thereof* — indicates the latter, a work of measured construction.

**Measure Well the Links Thereof**

The cross-reference to 34:11 supplies what this verse omits: the instruction includes a standard of workmanship. The links are to be measured, and the measuring is part of the teaching rather than an addition to it.

This is why the verse is significant for the sūrah's account of David. His distinction is not only prophetic but artisanal, and the artistry is governed by a rule. The craftsman who measures is distinguished from the one who merely assembles.

The sūrah thus places technical precision inside the prophetic narrative. A prophet is taught to make armour well, and the quality of the making is specified by God. The specification elevates the craft rather than treating it as incidental to the office.

**To Protect You Against Your Own Might**

Al-Qushayrī glosses *against your own might* as protection from battles and wars and from weapons. The phrase is thus read as describing the danger from which the armour protects, and the danger arises from human violence rather than from nature.

The wording is striking because the might in question is human might. The armour protects against the violence that human strength produces, which places the invention within a moral economy rather than a purely technical one.

The commentators connect this to 16:81, where God makes coats that protect from heat and coats that protect from human might. The parallel treats armour as one of the provisions by which human life is sustained against its own aggression.

**The Garments and the Might**

The verse's logic is that might requires a counter-provision. Strength is not sufficient for safety, and the teaching of armour-making is the Divine answer to the danger that strength itself creates.

This reading gives the verse its place in the sūrah. David is a warrior-king, and the armour he is taught to make is directed against the violence his own office involves. The teaching is thus addressed to the condition of the one who receives it.

The provision is therefore not merely defensive equipment but a recognition that human might is a hazard requiring management. The sūrah records the management among the gifts conferred on David.

**But Are You Thankful?**

The verse closes with a direct question: *but are you thankful?* The interrogative turns the report of a gift into an address, and the address is directed to the recipient rather than to the reader.

Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī record the phrase without commentary, allowing the question to stand. Its placement immediately after the teaching indicates that the gift and the demand for gratitude are a single matter.

The question is open. The sūrah does not report the answer, and the reader is left with the demand rather than the response. The openness is what makes the verse an address rather than a narrative statement.

**Gratitude as the Completion of the Gift**

The structure of the verse — teaching, protection, question — indicates that a conferred gift is incomplete without acknowledgment. The armour protects the body, but the question concerns the disposition of the one who receives it.

This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of other prophets, where gifts are recorded alongside the conduct they call for. The gift of judgment at verse 79 is matched by glorification; the gift of armour is matched by the question of gratitude.

The pairing indicates that Divine provision carries an expectation, and the expectation is stated rather than assumed. The sūrah asks the question it expects to be answered.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

The verse follows the accounts of David and Solomon's judgment and precedes Solomon's dominion over wind and satans. Its position places practical craftsmanship between judicial authority and cosmic command.

The sequence suggests a range of prophetic capacity: adjudication, manufacture, and sovereignty. Each is conferred, and each is directed toward the governance of human affairs.

The verse's technical content is thus integral to the sūrah's purpose. It records what a prophet was taught to make, and the recording indicates that the making belongs to the account of the prophetic office.

---

**The Craft and the Office**

The verse's technical content is what distinguishes it within the catalogue. Other verses record judgment, dominion, or patience; this one records a taught skill of manufacture, and the recording places the skill inside the prophetic account.

The distinction between making armour and wielding it is worth noting. David is taught to construct, not merely to use, and the construction is governed by a standard of measurement given in the parallel passage.

The verse therefore presents craftsmanship as a form of knowledge conferred by God. The knowledge has a practical object and a normative standard, and both belong to the teaching rather than to the craftsman's invention.

The closing question about gratitude completes the structure. A skill conferred is a gift, and a gift calls for acknowledgment; the verse asks the question rather than answering it, leaving the demand standing before the recipient.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:81

> **And unto Solomon, the wind blowing violently: it ran by his command to the land that We had blessed. And We know all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wind Blowing Violently**

The verse grants Solomon command over the wind, and the wind is described as blowing violently. The violence is not restrained but directed, and the direction is what constitutes the gift.

Al-Ṭabarī records that Solomon could command the wind to run to the land God had blessed, where he resided. The commentators cite 34:12c and 38:36 as parallel accounts, and 27:17–44 for the wider narrative of his court.

The violence of the wind is therefore an instrument rather than a hazard. What would be destructive without command becomes a means of transport under it, and the transformation is the substance of the gift.

**It Ran by His Command**

The phrase places the agency explicitly with Solomon: the wind ran by his command. The command is his, though the capacity is conferred, and the verse distinguishes the exercise of authority from its source.

This formulation parallels verse 79, where the mountains and birds glorified with David. In both cases a created order responds to a prophetic voice, and the response is described as obedience to command.

The distinction matters theologically. The wind does not obey Solomon by nature but by Divine appointment, and the appointment is what the verse records. The command is effective because it was authorized.

**The Land That We Had Blessed**

The wind carries toward the blessed land, and the blessing is stated as an established fact rather than explained here. Al-Ṭabarī identifies it as the land where Solomon resided.

The sūrah elsewhere associates blessing with specific places, and the reference relies on the reader's familiarity with the designation. The blessed land is a known term rather than a new description.

The connection between the wind and the blessed land gives the gift a destination. The command is not over motion generally but over travel toward a specific place, and the place is characterized by its blessing.

**Solomon's Court**

The commentators direct the reader to 27:17–44 for the account of Solomon's miraculous court, indicating that this verse is a summary rather than a narrative. The fuller account describes the assembly of jinn, humans, and birds.

The summarizing treatment is consistent with the sūrah's method. It states the fact of dominion without narrating its occasions, relying on the reader's knowledge of the fuller accounts elsewhere.

This economy allows the sūrah to move quickly through a series of prophetic endowments. Each receives a verse or two, and the cumulative effect is a catalogue of conferred capacities rather than a biography.

**And We Know All Things**

The verse closes with a statement of Divine knowledge, and the closing is abrupt relative to the narrative it concludes. The knowledge asserted is comprehensive rather than specific.

The statement functions as a check on the account. Solomon's command over the wind is real, but it occurs within a knowledge that encompasses all things, and the encompassing knowledge is God's alone.

The placement indicates that the sūrah does not permit the endowment to be read as independent power. The command is exercised, the wind obeys, and the knowledge behind both is Divine.

**Command Within Knowledge**

The juxtaposition of Solomon's command and God's knowledge establishes a hierarchy without diminishing either. The command is genuine and the knowledge is comprehensive, and both are stated in one sentence.

This is characteristic of the sūrah's treatment of prophetic power. Endowments are recorded in full, and each record is closed by an assertion of Divine transcendence that prevents the endowment from being read as autonomy.

The closing clause is therefore structural rather than ornamental. It supplies the theological frame within which the miraculous command is to be understood.

**The Sequence of Endowments**

The verse follows the teaching of armour-making and precedes the account of the satans. Its position places dominion over natural forces between craftsmanship and the command of jinn.

The progression moves from what David made to what Solomon commanded, and from command over wind to command over satans. Each endowment extends the scope of the prophetic office.

The sūrah's catalogue is thus cumulative. The reader is given a series of conferred capacities, each distinct, and the series indicates the range of what the prophetic office can include.

---

**The Instrument and Its Direction**

The verse's emphasis falls on direction rather than on power. The wind already blows violently; what Solomon receives is the capacity to determine where it goes, and the determination is toward the blessed land.

This distinction between force and direction is what makes the gift administrative rather than merely spectacular. A king needs movement and transport, and the verse supplies both through a single instrument.

Al-Ṭabarī's specification that the wind ran to the land where Solomon resided grounds the gift in a concrete arrangement. The command has a destination, and the destination is identified rather than left abstract.

The closing assertion of Divine knowledge frames the whole arrangement. Solomon commands, the wind obeys, and the knowledge encompassing both is God's, which prevents the command from being read as an independent sovereignty.

---

**The Command and Its Authorization**

The verse's phrase that the wind ran by his command is the crux, and it needs to be read alongside the closing statement that God knows all things. The command is Solomon's in exercise, and the authorization is Divine.

The distinction prevents the account from describing an independent power. Nothing in the wind's obedience derives from Solomon's own capacity; it derives from the appointment recorded in the verse, and the appointment is what makes the command effective.

The cross-references the commentators supply to 34:12c and 38:36 show the arrangement described elsewhere in similar terms. The parallel accounts confirm that the command over wind is a settled feature of Solomon's portrait rather than an isolated claim.

The blessed land as destination gives the gift a shape. It is not dominion over motion in general but over travel toward a specific place, and the place is designated by its blessing rather than by its name.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:82

> **Among the satans are those who dove deep for him, and performed other deeds besides this. And We guarded them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those Who Dove Deep for Him**

The verse describes satans who dived deep for Solomon, and al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī gloss the diving as descent into the sea. Al-Thaʿlabī adds that the purpose was the extraction of precious gems.

The activity is thus specified: not diving generally but diving for valuables from the sea bed. The labour is extractive, and its product is precious rather than useful.

The commentators' specification indicates that the account had a developed traditional background. The sūrah states the diving; the tradition supplies its object, and the tradition is preserved with its sources named.

**Other Deeds Besides This**

Al-Ṭabarī records that among the other deeds the satans performed was building. The verse's reference to further works is thus filled out by a specific example rather than left wholly open.

The combination of diving and building indicates a range of labour, from extraction to construction. Both are physical works performed under command, and both serve Solomon's purposes.

The verse's economy — *other deeds besides this* — allows the tradition to supply detail without the text committing to it. The text asserts the range; the tradition illustrates it.

**And We Guarded Them**

Al-Thaʿlabī explains *guarded them* as God ensuring that the effects of the satans' works would not become corrupted. The guarding is thus directed at the durability of the products rather than at the workers.

Al-Rāzī supplies the reason: the wont of satans — usually understood to refer to jinn — is to do something seemingly good and then cause it to become bad. The guarding prevents this reversal.

The two readings together describe a real hazard. Labour performed by beings of unreliable disposition requires protection against sabotage, and the protection is Divine rather than contractual.

**The Disposition of the Workers**

Al-Rāzī's account of the satans' wont is theologically significant. It characterizes them as capable of apparent good followed by actual harm, and the characterization explains why guarding was necessary.

The characterization is not a claim that every work was corrupted but that corruption was their tendency. The guarding addresses the tendency, and the works are preserved despite it.

This reading preserves both the value of the labour and the unreliability of the labourers. Solomon benefits from the works, and God's guarding makes the benefit secure.

**Punishment for Deviation**

The commentators direct the reader to 34:13–14 for the tasks assigned to the satans and the punishment they receive for deviating from Solomon's commands. The cross-reference supplies the disciplinary dimension.

The reference indicates that the command was enforceable. The satans worked under authority that included sanction, and the sanction is recorded in the parallel passage.

The combination of command, labour, guarding, and sanction describes a complete arrangement. Each element is recorded, and the arrangement is presented as Divinely supervised throughout.

**Labour Under Constraint**

The verse depicts beings of hostile disposition performing productive labour under constraint. The productivity is genuine and the constraint is necessary, and both are stated without resolution of the tension.

The tension is characteristic of the sūrah's treatment of Solomon's dominion. His authority extends over beings who are not his allies, and the extension requires Divine support to be effective.

The account thus distinguishes authority from alliance. Solomon commands the satans without their goodwill, and the command holds because God guards its effects.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse completes the account of Solomon's endowments begun at verse 81. Wind and satans are both placed under his command, and both require Divine support to serve him reliably.

The completion is economical. Two verses record a dominion described at length elsewhere, and the economy serves the sūrah's catalogue of prophetic capacities.

The guarding clause is what distinguishes the account from a simple assertion of power. The power is real, and it is sustained by a protection that the verse names explicitly.

---

**Work, Sabotage, and Guarding**

The verse's most distinctive feature is its account of unreliable labour. The satans dive and build, and their works are preserved by Divine guarding precisely because their disposition inclines toward reversal.

Al-Rāzī's characterization — that they do something seemingly good and then cause it to become bad — supplies the reason the guarding was needed. Without it the works would be temporary and their benefit uncertain.

The arrangement therefore depends on a protection that is external to the workers. Neither Solomon's command nor the satans' skill secures the products; the guarding does, and the guarding is God's.

The cross-reference to 34:13–14 completes the picture by supplying the sanction for deviation. Command, labour, guarding, and punishment together describe an arrangement that is supervised at every point.

The verse's function in the catalogue is to show the outer limit of the conferred dominion. Authority extends even over beings of hostile disposition, and the extension holds only because it is sustained.

---

**Why the Guarding Was Necessary**

The guarding clause is the verse's theological core, and it is easy to pass over. The satans' labour is real and productive, but its products are not secure unless they are guarded, and the reason is the workers' disposition.

Al-Rāzī's account explains the hazard concretely: the wont of these beings is to produce something apparently good and then cause it to become bad. The sabotage is not accidental but characteristic, which is why the guarding is not optional.

Al-Thaʿlabī's gloss specifies what is guarded: the effects of the works, so that they do not become corrupted. The protection is directed at the durability of the product rather than at the safety of the workers.

Together the two readings describe an arrangement in which value is produced by unreliable agents and preserved by a third party. Neither the command nor the skill secures the outcome; the guarding does.

The reference to 34:13–14 adds the sanction for deviation, completing an arrangement supervised at every stage: assignment, labour, preservation, and punishment for failure to comply.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:83

> **And [remember] Job, when he cried unto his Lord, "Truly affliction has befallen me! And Thou art the most Merciful of the merciful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**And [Remember] Job**

The verse introduces Job with the same formula used for earlier prophets, and the introduction is followed immediately by his cry. The sūrah gives no narrative frame, moving directly to the supplication.

The commentators direct the reader to 38:41–44 for the fuller account, indicating that this verse presumes familiarity with the story. The reference is economical and relies on the parallel passage.

The omission of narrative detail focuses the verse on the supplication itself. What the sūrah records is what Job said, not what happened to him, and the saying is the verse's content.

**Truly Affliction Has Befallen Me**

The cry opens with a statement of fact rather than a request. Job declares his condition, and the declaration is unelaborated — affliction has befallen him, and that is the whole of the statement.

The plainness of the declaration is significant. There is no complaint against God and no demand, only an acknowledgment of what has occurred. The acknowledgment is the beginning of the supplication.

The commentators transmit the background of the story from Biblical sources, including Satan's seeking and receiving permission to test Job by destroying his property, family, and health. The background is preserved as tradition rather than as Qur'anic narrative.

**The Biblical Background**

The commentators note that the story of Job is not told in the Qur'an with the same level of narrative detail as in the Biblical description. The transmission is therefore partial and its source is identified.

The story is sometimes combined with dimensions not found in the Biblical account, such as explicit mention of Satan's jealousy as a reason for seeking permission, and the notion that Satan overheard God's praise of Job.

Al-Ṭabarī records these additional dimensions and connects the overhearing to 37:6–10c. The combination of sources is marked as such, and the reader is given the composite with its components distinguished.

**And Thou Art the Most Merciful of the Merciful**

The cry closes with an assertion about God rather than a petition. Job states the superlative of mercy, and the statement functions as the appeal without being phrased as a request.

The structure is thus declaration followed by doxology. What has befallen him is stated, and who God is follows, and the two together constitute the supplication.

The absence of an explicit request is characteristic of the prophetic supplications in this sūrah. The prophets describe their condition and affirm God's attributes, and the answer follows without a demand having been made.

**The Form of the Supplication**

The verse's two clauses — affliction stated, mercy affirmed — form a pattern seen in the supplications of Noah, Lot, and Jonah. The pattern is consistent across the accounts.

The consistency indicates a deliberate literary structure. Each prophet's cry is recorded in the same form, and the form emphasizes condition and Divine attribute over petition.

The structure also reflects the sūrah's theology of prayer. The one who prays is not required to specify the remedy; the description of need and the affirmation of mercy are sufficient.

**Affliction as the Occasion**

The verse presents affliction as the occasion of the cry rather than as a punishment or a test explained within the text. The sūrah does not adjudicate why Job suffers.

The commentators supply explanations from tradition, but the verse itself records only the suffering and the response to it. The explanation remains external to the Qur'anic account.

This restraint allows the verse to function as a model of supplication without committing to a theodicy. The reader is given the cry, not the justification of the suffering that occasioned it.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

The verse follows the accounts of David and Solomon and precedes the answer at verse 84. Its position places patient suffering after conferred dominion.

The juxtaposition is significant. The sūrah moves from power to affliction, and from the command of wind and satans to the cry of a man who has lost everything.

The movement indicates that the prophetic office includes both conditions, and the sequence presents them without ranking. Dominion and affliction are both part of the account.

---

**The Cry Without a Petition**

The verse's form is what makes it exemplary. Job states his affliction and affirms God's mercy, and the two clauses together constitute a complete supplication without containing a request.

The absence of petition is not a defect in the prayer but a feature of its structure. The description of need and the affirmation of the Divine attribute are sufficient, and the answer at verse 84 confirms it.

The commentators' transmission of the Biblical background, including Satan's request for permission and his jealousy, is preserved as tradition with its sources marked. The Qur'anic account itself remains confined to the cry.

This restraint is what allows the verse to serve as a model. It presents the form of supplication without committing to an explanation of the suffering that occasioned it, leaving the theodicy to tradition.

The verse's placement after Solomon's dominion is also deliberate. The sūrah moves from command over wind and satans to the cry of one who has lost property, family, and health, and the movement is unexplained.

---

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse's silences are as instructive as its content. It does not explain why Job suffers, does not mention Satan, and does not narrate the loss of property, family, and health that the tradition describes.

Those elements are supplied by the commentators from Biblical sources, and al-Ṭabarī adds the further dimensions of Satan's jealousy and his overhearing of God's praise of Job, connecting the latter to 37:6–10c.

The marking of these materials as transmitted tradition rather than as Qur'anic narrative is methodologically important. It preserves the distinction between what the text says and what the tradition reports about it.

Because the verse is confined to the cry, it can function as a model of supplication without requiring the reader to accept any particular account of the suffering. The form of the prayer is separable from the theodicy.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:84

> **So We answered him and removed the affliction that was upon him, and We gave him his family, and the like thereof along with them, as a mercy from Us and a reminder to the worshippers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**So We Answered Him**

The verse opens with the answer, and the answer is stated without description of its manner. The sūrah records that God answered and then what the answer accomplished.

The immediacy of the response is what the verse emphasizes. The cry at verse 83 is answered at verse 84, and the sequence is direct.

The lack of narrative elaboration is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the earlier supplications. Noah's cry is answered, Job's is answered, and the answering is recorded rather than dramatized.

**Removed the Affliction That Was Upon Him**

The first element of the answer is removal. The affliction that was upon him is taken away, and the phrasing indicates that the affliction had been a condition he carried.

The removal is complete in the sense that the verse specifies no residue. What was upon him is removed, and the removal is the direct reversal of what the cry reported.

The correspondence between the cry and the answer is exact. Job declared that affliction had befallen him, and the answer is that the affliction is removed. The answer addresses precisely what was stated.

**And We Gave Him His Family**

The second element is restoration of family, and the verse adds *and the like thereof along with them*. The restoration is thus not merely a return but an increase.

The doubling is significant. What was lost is returned and its like is added, so the final state exceeds the original. The increase is part of the answer rather than a separate gift.

The commentators connect this to the fuller account at 38:41–44, where the restoration is described in similar terms. The parallel indicates a consistent tradition about the outcome.

**As a Mercy from Us**

The verse characterizes the answer as mercy, and the characterization is attributed to God: *a mercy from Us*. The answer is thus presented as an act of Divine mercy rather than as a reward.

The attribution matters because it distinguishes the answer from a transaction. Job's cry was answered by mercy, and the mercy is God's own rather than something earned.

This is consistent with the cry itself, which affirmed that God is the most Merciful of the merciful. The answer corresponds to the attribute Job named.

**And a Reminder to the Worshippers**

The verse gives the account a second function: it is a reminder to the worshippers. The story is thus addressed to those who worship, and its purpose is instructive.

The designation of the audience as worshippers rather than as believers generally indicates that the reminder concerns the conduct of worship. The account shows how one who worships responds to affliction.

The reminder is what makes the verse more than narrative. It is presented as a model, and the model is explicitly offered to those who worship.

**The Double Purpose**

The verse's two closing phrases — mercy and reminder — indicate that the account operates on two levels. It is an act of Divine mercy toward Job and an instruction for others.

The combination is characteristic of the sūrah's prophetic narratives. Each account records what God did and what the recording is for, and the second element addresses the reader.

The reminder thus extends the account's relevance beyond its subject. Job's affliction and answer are given so that worshippers may take from them a pattern for their own supplication.

**The Answer's Completeness**

The answer includes removal, restoration, increase, mercy, and instruction. Each element is stated, and together they describe a response that exceeds the cry's content.

Job asked for nothing specific, and the answer supplies everything: relief, family, more than was lost, and a place in the sūrah's instruction to worshippers.

The completeness is the verse's point. The supplication that specified no remedy received a comprehensive answer, and the comprehensiveness is recorded for those who worship.

---

**Restoration Exceeding Loss**

The answer's structure is worth setting out. Removal of affliction comes first, then restoration of family, then the like thereof added to what was restored, and finally the characterization of the whole as mercy.

The addition of the like is what distinguishes this restoration from a simple return. The final state is greater than the original, and the increase is part of the answer rather than a subsequent gift.

The characterization as mercy corresponds to the attribute Job named in his cry. He affirmed that God is the most Merciful of the merciful, and the answer is described in the same term.

The reminder to the worshippers extends the account beyond its subject. The story is given so that those who worship may take from it a pattern, and the pattern is the sufficiency of describing need and affirming mercy.

The verse thus completes the movement begun at verse 83. The cry specifies no remedy, the answer supplies everything, and the supplying is recorded as an instruction for others.

---

**The Sequence Within the Answer**

The order of the answer's elements is not arbitrary. Removal comes before restoration, and restoration before increase, and the whole is then characterized as mercy and offered as a reminder.

This sequence means the answer first undoes the harm and then exceeds the original state. The increase is not compensation for the loss but something added after the loss has been removed.
