The final characterization as mercy ties the answer to the attribute Job named. He affirmed God as the most Merciful of the merciful, and the response is described in the same vocabulary.

The reminder to the worshippers then extends the account outward. What was done for Job is recorded so that those who worship may learn from it, and the instruction concerns the sufficiency of describing need and affirming mercy.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:85

> **And [remember] Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhu'l-Kifl—each was among the patient.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhu'l-Kifl**

The verse names three figures together, and the naming is followed by a single characterization applying to all: each was among the patient. The grouping is by quality rather than by narrative.

The sūrah gives no account of what any of the three endured. The patience is asserted without the occasion being specified, which distinguishes this verse from the preceding accounts.

The economy of the treatment indicates that the verse's purpose is to name the quality and its bearers. The narrative is omitted, and the characterization stands alone.

**Idrīs and His Identifications**

Al-Ṭabarī records that Idrīs is usually associated with the Biblical Enoch, and also with Elias, with reference to 6:85. Later sources associate him with Hirmis or Hermes.

The commentators preserve all these identifications rather than settling on one, and direct the reader to 19:56–57c for further discussion. The plurality is presented as the state of the tradition.

The multiplicity of identifications is itself informative. It indicates that the figure's identity was not fixed in the tradition, and the commentators record the range rather than resolving it.

**The Preservation of Uncertainty**

The commentators' treatment of Idrīs is methodologically notable. They list Enoch, Elias, and Hermes without adjudicating, and the listing preserves the uncertainty as part of the record.

This approach is consistent with their handling of other contested identifications in the sūrah. Where the tradition is divided, the division is reported rather than resolved.

The reader is thus given the state of knowledge rather than a settled conclusion. The identification of Idrīs remains open, and the openness is documented.

**Dhu'l-Kifl**

The verse names Dhu'l-Kifl without further description, and the commentators do not supply a narrative here. The name is given and the characterization of patience applies to him.

The absence of elaboration distinguishes him from the other prophets in the sequence, whose accounts are narrated. He is named among the patient and the naming is the extent of the account.

The treatment indicates that the sūrah's catalogue includes figures whose stories are not told. The quality is what matters for the verse's purpose, not the narrative.

**Each Was Among the Patient**

The characterization applies to all three equally, and the equality is stated explicitly: *each was among the patient*. The phrase is distributive rather than collective.

The distributive form indicates that each individually possessed the quality. The grouping does not dilute the attribution; it extends it to each member.

Patience is thus the unifying characteristic of the three, and it is the quality the sūrah wishes to record. The verse is a statement about patience rather than about the three figures.

**Patience as the Quality**

The verse's placement after Job's account is significant. Job's affliction and answer have just been narrated, and patience is the quality that account exemplifies.

The naming of three further patient figures extends the exemplification. The quality is not confined to Job but is shared, and the sharing is what the verse records.

The sequence thus moves from a narrated instance to a general attribution. The reader has seen patience in Job and is then told that others possessed it.

**The Verse's Function in the Catalogue**

The verse continues the sūrah's catalogue of prophets while changing its mode. The preceding verses narrate; this one names and characterizes.

The change of mode allows the catalogue to include figures without narratives. The sūrah can record their place in the prophetic succession without telling their stories.

The verse thus serves a structural purpose. It maintains the catalogue's continuity while varying its treatment, and the variation keeps the sequence from becoming uniform.

---

**Naming Without Narrating**

The verse's brevity is deliberate. Three figures are named and one quality is attributed to each, and no account is given of what any of them endured. The attribution stands without narrative support.

This treatment allows the catalogue to include figures whose stories are not told. The sūrah records their place in the prophetic succession through the quality they share rather than through their histories.

Al-Ṭabarī's handling of Idrīs illustrates the commentators' method. Enoch, Elias, and Hermes are all recorded, none is preferred, and the reader is directed to 19:56–57c for further discussion of the range.

The preservation of the uncertainty is itself a result. The identification of Idrīs is not settled in the tradition, and the commentators report the state of the tradition rather than resolving it.

The verse's placement after Job's account is significant. Patience has just been narrated in detail, and the naming of three further patient figures extends the exemplification from one instance to a general attribution.

The distributive form — each was among the patient — ensures that the attribution applies individually rather than collectively, so the grouping extends the quality without diluting it.

---

**The Quality and Its Bearers**

The verse attributes patience to three figures and gives no account of what any of them endured. The attribution is therefore independent of narrative, and its force derives from the naming rather than from any story.

This independence is what allows the sūrah to include figures whose histories are not told. Dhu'l-Kifl receives no elaboration at all, and the absence of elaboration does not weaken the attribution.

Al-Ṭabarī's treatment of Idrīs shows how the commentators handle a figure whose identity is contested. Enoch, Elias, and Hermes are all recorded, none is preferred, and the reader is sent to 19:56–57c for the range of views.

The preservation of that range is a substantive result. The identification remains open, and the commentators report the tradition's state rather than settling a question the sources do not settle.

The verse's position after Job's account gives patience a narrated instance before the general attribution. The reader has seen what patience looks like, and is then told that these three shared it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:86

> **We caused them to enter Our Mercy; truly they are among the righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**We Caused Them to Enter Our Mercy**

The verse closes the account of Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhu'l-Kifl with the same formulation used of Lot at verse 75, and al-Qushayrī notes that many understand the Mercy here to mean the Garden in the Hereafter.

The identification is significant because it fixes the referent. The Mercy is not an abstract favour but a place, and the entering is caused rather than achieved, which is what the causative verb expresses.

The cross-reference to verse 75 confirms the parallel treatment. Lot was caused to enter the Mercy, and the three patient prophets are caused to enter it, and the shared formulation places them in the same company.

**The Causative and Its Implication**

The verb's causative form is what the verse turns on. The prophets do not enter the Mercy by their own attainment; they are caused to enter, and the causing is Divine.

This formulation is consistent with the sūrah's presentation throughout. Judgment, knowledge, dominion, and now entry into the Mercy are all conferred, and the prophets' conduct is the exercise of what was conferred.

The implication is that the Mercy is not a reward proportionate to effort but a destination assigned. The righteousness stated in the verse characterizes those who enter rather than constituting the means of entry.

**Truly They Are Among the Righteous**

The closing clause characterizes the three as righteous, and the characterization is stated as a fact rather than as an assessment. The sūrah asserts their standing without qualification.

The same term was used at verse 72 of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and at verse 75 of Lot. The repeated vocabulary establishes a company, and the company is defined by the shared attribution.

The attribution is what unifies the catalogue. The prophets differ in their endowments and their histories, but the righteousness is common to them, and the common attribution is what the sūrah records.

**The Verse as Closure**

The verse functions as the conclusion of a sequence that began with Abraham. Judgment, knowledge, deliverance, dominion, patience, and now entry into the Mercy form a progression.

The progression is not chronological but thematic. Each prophet illustrates a different aspect of the office, and the sequence concludes with what all of them receive.

The concluding position gives the verse a summarizing force. It states the common destination of the prophets already named, and the statement closes the first movement of the catalogue.

**The Garden as the Mercy**

Al-Qushayrī's note that many understand the Mercy as the Garden is preserved as a majority reading rather than as the only one. The commentators record the prevalence without excluding alternatives.

The identification matters for the verse's eschatological reference. If the Mercy is the Garden, the entering is future rather than present, and the verse concerns the prophets' ultimate destination.

The reading is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the Hereafter elsewhere. The prophets' rewards are described in terms of what they will receive, and the Garden is the standard designation.

**Entry Rather Than Attainment**

The verse's emphasis on entry distinguishes it from language of attainment or earning. The prophets are brought in, and the bringing is the Divine act the verse records.

This distinction is theologically important. It prevents the righteousness stated in the verse from being read as the cause of the entry, and keeps the entry within the order of Divine giving.

The formulation thus preserves both the prophets' standing and the priority of the Mercy. They are righteous, and they are caused to enter, and the two statements are not in competition.

**The Company of the Righteous**

The verse establishes a company defined by a shared attribute rather than by a shared history. The three named prophets have no narrative in common within the sūrah, but they share the attribution of righteousness.

This mode of grouping allows the catalogue to function as a list of exemplars. The reader is given names and a quality, and the quality is what connects them.

The grouping also prepares for the next sequence, which turns to Jonah, Zachariah, and Mary. The catalogue continues, and the pattern of naming and characterizing continues with it.

---

**The Entering and the Righteousness**

The verse's two clauses are connected but not causally in the obvious direction. The prophets are caused to enter the Mercy, and they are among the righteous, and the second clause characterizes rather than earns the first.

Al-Qushayrī's note that many understand the Mercy as the Garden fixes the referent as a place, which makes the causative verb's force plain: entry into the Garden is caused, not achieved.

The cross-reference to verse 75 is what establishes the parallel with Lot. The same verb and the same object are used, and the repetition places the three patient prophets in Lot's company.

The righteousness attributed to them is the same term used of Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and Lot in the preceding verses, so the shared vocabulary builds a company defined by a common attribute.

The company is not defined by shared history. The three have no narrative connecting them in this sūrah, and the grouping rests entirely on the attribution of patience and righteousness.

The verse's closing position gives it summarizing force. It states the destination common to the prophets already named, and the statement closes the movement that began with Abraham.

The emphasis on entry rather than attainment is theologically deliberate. It keeps the Mercy in the order of Divine giving, and prevents the righteousness from being read as the purchase price of the Garden.

The formulation is therefore consistent with the sūrah's treatment throughout: judgment, knowledge, dominion, patience, and now entry are all conferred, and the prophets exercise what they have been given. The consistency is what allows the catalogue to accumulate without repeating itself, since each gift differs in kind while the mode of giving remains the same.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:87

> **And [remember] Dhu'l-Nūn, when he went away in anger, and thought We had no power over him. Then he cried out in the darkness, "There is no god but Thee! Glory be to Thee! Truly I have been among the wrongdoers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Dhu'l-Nūn**

Al-Ṭabarī records that Dhu'l-Nūn, mentioned only in this verse of the Qur'an, is another name for Jonah, and explains that *nūn* means a large fish or whale, making the name something like the man of the fish.

The designation is thus descriptive rather than personal. It identifies Jonah by his defining experience, and the identification is supplied by the commentators because the name occurs nowhere else.

The uniqueness of the designation is worth noting. The sūrah uses a name found in no other verse, and the commentators must explain it, which indicates that the reference presumed familiarity.

**The Assumed Narrative**

The commentators note that the Qur'an does not tell the story of Jonah in full but assumes its audience had prior knowledge of his preaching to Nineveh, his flight on a ship, and his being cast overboard and swallowed by a fish.

The cross-references supplied are 37:139–45 for the fuller account and 68:48–50, which is also understood to be about Jonah. The sūrah relies on this background rather than supplying it.

The reliance is characteristic of the sūrah's method. It states the prophetic episode it needs and presumes the narrative is available elsewhere, which allows the catalogue to move quickly.

**When He Went Away in Anger**

The verse records Jonah's departure in anger, and al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī preserve two readings of the anger's direction. It may have been directed against God because He spared Nineveh after having promised to destroy it.

The alternative reading is that he was expressing a just anger on behalf of God in the face of his people's disobedience. The two readings differ in whether the anger is problematic or zealous.

The commentators preserve both without preferring either, and refer to 37:142c. The preservation is methodologically consistent with their treatment of other contested matters in the sūrah.

**And Thought We Had No Power Over Him**

The verse's most difficult clause states that Jonah thought God had no power over him. The thought is recorded as his, and the recording is unqualified by any correction within the verse.

The clause has been read variously in the tradition, and the sūrah does not explain it. What is recorded is the thought and the departure, and the consequence follows at verse 88.

The difficulty is part of the account's force. A prophet is described as entertaining a thought about Divine power, and the description is preserved rather than softened.

**Then He Cried Out in the Darkness**

The verse turns on the cry, and the darkness is its setting. The cry is recorded in full, and its three clauses form a complete supplication: tawḥīd, glorification, and confession.

The confession is the clause that turns the account. Jonah acknowledges that he has been among the wrongdoers, and the acknowledgment is the movement from the thought recorded earlier to the cry.

The structure of the cry parallels the supplications of Noah, Job, and others in this sūrah. Condition is described, God's attributes are affirmed, and the answer follows.

**There Is No God but Thee**

The cry opens with the declaration of Divine unity, and the declaration is addressed directly to God in the second person. The address is intimate rather than propositional.

The glorification follows immediately: *Glory be to Thee!* The two clauses together affirm God's unity and transcendence, and the affirmation precedes the confession.

The order is significant. The cry establishes who God is before stating what Jonah has been, and the establishment is what makes the confession intelligible as a prayer rather than as despair.

**The Cry and the Answer**

The verse's structure moves from departure and thought to cry, and verse 88 records the answer. The sequence is compact, and each element is stated without elaboration.

The compactness is what makes the account exemplary. A prophet departs in anger, entertains a thought, cries out, and is answered, and the four movements occupy two verses.

The sūrah thus presents the account as a pattern rather than as a biography. The reader is given the form of the failure and the form of the recovery, and the form is what instructs.

---

**The Departure, the Thought, and the Cry**

The verse's four movements occupy a single sentence, and the compression is what gives the account its exemplary force. Departure, thought, cry, and confession are stated without narrative padding.

Al-Ṭabarī's identification of Dhu'l-Nūn as Jonah, with the explanation that nūn means a large fish or whale, is necessary because the name occurs in no other verse of the Qur'an.

The commentators' note that the story is not told in full but assumes prior knowledge of the preaching to Nineveh, the flight, the casting overboard, and the swallowing explains the verse's economy.

The cross-references to 37:139–45 and 68:48–50 supply the fuller narrative, and the sūrah's reliance on them allows it to record only the supplication and its setting.

The two readings of the anger's direction are preserved without preference: it may have been against God for sparing Nineveh, or a just anger on behalf of God at his people's disobedience.

The preservation of both readings is methodologically consistent with the commentators' treatment of contested matters elsewhere in the sūrah, and neither is excluded.

The difficult clause about thinking God had no power over him is recorded without correction or softening, and the recording is part of the account's honesty about the prophetic condition.

The cry's three clauses — unity, glorification, confession — form a complete supplication, and the confession is what turns the account from the recorded thought toward the answer at verse 88. The order is deliberate: who God is comes before what Jonah has been, and the precedence is what makes the confession a prayer rather than a statement of despair.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:88

> **So We answered him, and saved him from grief. Thus do We save the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**So We Answered Him**

The verse opens with the answer, and al-Ṭabarī specifies that God saved Jonah from the belly of the fish. The specification supplies what the verse itself leaves implicit.

The cross-reference to 37:143–44c indicates that the fuller account of the deliverance is available elsewhere. This verse records the fact of the answer rather than narrating the rescue.

The economy is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the earlier supplications. The cry is recorded at length, the answer briefly, and the brevity focuses attention on the cry.

**And Saved Him from Grief**

The verse describes the saving as deliverance from grief, and the grief is not further specified. It may refer to the distress of the fish's belly or to the anguish that occasioned the cry.

The generality of the term allows both readings. The grief is what Jonah experienced, and the saving is its removal, and the verse does not distinguish between physical and spiritual distress.

The undifferentiated description is characteristic of the sūrah's accounts. The prophets' distress is named without analysis, and the deliverance is stated without mechanism.

**Thus Do We Save the Believers**

The verse's closing clause generalizes the account. What was done for Jonah is what God does for the believers, and the generalization extends the account beyond its subject.

The formulation is identical in function to the reminder at verse 84. The prophetic account is given so that others may take from it, and the extension is explicit.

The designation of the recipients as believers rather than as prophets broadens the application. The pattern of cry and answer is available to believers generally, not to the prophets alone.

**Junayd's Reading**

Al-Sulamī records that for the Sufi Junayd, this verse means God saves the believers from their troubles through their own sincerity, an awareness of their needfulness of God, the virtues, and manifesting an attitude of surrender.

The reading locates the means of salvation in dispositions rather than in external rescue. Sincerity, needfulness, virtue, and surrender are what effect the saving, and they are the believer's own.

The reading is preserved alongside al-Ṭabarī's literal account rather than replacing it. The commentators record both the historical deliverance and the spiritual interpretation without preferring either.

**The Two Readings Together**

The literal and spiritual readings are not in competition. Jonah was saved from the fish, and the saving is also a pattern of how believers are saved from their troubles.

The preservation of both indicates that the verse operates on two levels simultaneously. It records an event and it states a principle, and the principle is derived from the event.

This double operation is characteristic of the sūrah's prophetic narratives. Each account is historical and exemplary, and the commentators preserve both dimensions.

**The Pattern of Cry and Answer**

The verse completes the pattern begun at verse 87. The cry is recorded, the answer follows, and the generalization extends the pattern to believers.

The pattern is the sūrah's central consolation. The Prophet ﷺ is addressed by a sequence of accounts showing that those who cried out were answered, and the sequence culminates in the generalization.

The generalization is what makes the consolation effective. It is not that Jonah was saved but that believers are saved, and the believer reading includes the one addressed.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse serves two purposes. It concludes Jonah's account, and it states the principle that the account illustrates. Both purposes are served by a single short verse.

The economy is deliberate. The sūrah does not need to elaborate the rescue because the fuller account exists elsewhere; what it needs is the generalization, and the generalization is supplied.

The verse thus completes the movement from particular to general that characterizes the sūrah's method. Each prophetic account ends with an extension to others, and this one is explicit.

---

**The Answer and Its Extension**

The verse is short, and its shortness is functional. It records the answer, specifies the deliverance, and extends the pattern to believers, and the three movements need no elaboration.

Al-Ṭabarī's specification that God saved Jonah from the belly of the fish supplies what the verse leaves implicit, and the cross-reference to 37:143–44c points to the fuller account.

The description of the saving as deliverance from grief is deliberately general, allowing the grief to be read as the distress of the fish's belly, the anguish that occasioned the cry, or both.

The closing generalization is the verse's point. What was done for Jonah is what God does for the believers, and the extension is explicit rather than left for the reader to infer.

The designation of the recipients as believers rather than prophets broadens the application, so the pattern of cry and answer is available to the one reading and not only to the prophets named.

Al-Sulamī's record of Junayd's reading locates the means of salvation in sincerity, awareness of needfulness, the virtues, and an attitude of surrender, so the saving works through dispositions.

The spiritual reading is preserved alongside the literal account rather than replacing it, and the coexistence of the two indicates that the verse operates historically and exemplarily at once.

The verse thus completes the movement from particular to general that characterizes the sūrah's method, and the generalization is what makes the consolation available to the one addressed. It is not that Jonah was saved that matters for the reader, but that the saving is described as God's manner of dealing with the believers. The shift from a single rescue to a stated pattern is the whole achievement of the verse, and it is accomplished in a clause of no more than four words. Everything preceding it belongs to Jonah; the clause belongs to everyone who reads, and the belonging is what the sūrah's consolation rests on.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:89

> **And Zachariah, when he cried out to his Lord, "My Lord! Leave me not childless, though Thou art the best of inheritors."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**And [Remember] Zachariah**

The verse introduces Zachariah with the same formula used for the preceding prophets, and al-Qushayrī notes that his story is told in 3:37–41 and 19:2–11.

The cross-references indicate that the sūrah presumes familiarity with the account. What is recorded here is the supplication, not the narrative that occasioned it.

The economy is consistent with the catalogue's method. Each prophet receives the verse or verses needed to record his supplication and its answer, and the narrative is available elsewhere.

**Leave Me Not Childless**

The cry is a request not to be left without an heir, and the request is stated without elaboration of the circumstances. The sūrah does not explain Zachariah's age or his wife's barrenness here.

The circumstances are supplied by the parallel accounts, where his wife is described as barren. The sūrah relies on the reader's knowledge of those accounts rather than repeating them.

The plainness of the request is what gives it force. It is a direct petition for an heir, and the petition is followed immediately by the affirmation of God as the best of inheritors.

**Though Thou Art the Best of Inheritors**

The clause is concessive and theologically significant. Al-Qushayrī explains that it means that in the absence of an heir, God would be the only one to inherit from Zachariah.

The explanation is supported by the allusion to 28:58, where God speaks of Himself as the only one left to inherit from a destroyed people. The parallel establishes the idiom.

The concession is what makes the prayer coherent. Zachariah asks for an heir while acknowledging that God would suffice without one, and the acknowledgment prevents the request from being a complaint.

**The Concession and the Request**

The structure of the cry is request followed by concession, and the order matters. The request comes first, and the concession qualifies it without withdrawing it.

The qualification is what distinguishes this supplication from a demand. Zachariah states his need and simultaneously affirms that God's sufficiency is not diminished by the absence of an heir.

The combination is characteristic of the prophetic supplications in this sūrah. Need is stated, and Divine sufficiency is affirmed, and the two are held together without tension.

**The Higher Reading**

Al-Sulamī records a Sufi interpretation at a higher level: Zachariah's prayer is that he not be left bereft of intimacy with God and without a way that would lead to Him.

The reading transforms the request from one for a biological heir to one for continued relationship with God. The childlessness is read as spiritual isolation rather than as the absence of a son.

The reading is preserved alongside the literal one rather than replacing it. The commentators record both, and the higher reading is marked as such by its designation.

**The Two Levels of the Prayer**

The literal and spiritual readings are both preserved, and their coexistence indicates that the verse admits both. The request for an heir is real, and the request for intimacy is its deeper form.

The preservation is methodologically consistent. Where the tradition offers a higher reading, it is recorded with its source and its level marked, and the literal reading is not displaced.

The double reading enriches the account without resolving it. The reader is given the prayer as a request for a son and as a request for God, and both are presented as valid.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

The verse follows Jonah's account and precedes the answer at verse 90. Its position places a request for an heir after an account of deliverance from distress.

The sequence varies the catalogue's content. Jonah's cry concerns his own situation, and Zachariah's concerns his succession, and the variation keeps the sequence from becoming repetitive.

The verse thus serves the catalogue's cumulative purpose. Each supplication illustrates a different form of need, and the accumulation of forms is what the sūrah presents.

---

**The Request and the Concession**

The verse records a request for an heir and immediately qualifies it with an affirmation of God's sufficiency, and the two clauses together form a petition that is not a complaint.

Al-Qushayrī's cross-references to 3:37–41 and 19:2–11 indicate that the narrative is presumed rather than supplied, so the sūrah records the supplication and relies on the parallel accounts.

The circumstances of Zachariah's age and his wife's barrenness are not stated here; they are available in the parallels, where she is described as barren, and the sūrah does not repeat them.

The concession that God is the best of inheritors means that in the absence of an heir God alone would inherit from Zachariah, and the idiom is supported by the allusion to 28:58.

The concessive structure is what makes the prayer coherent: the request is stated and then qualified by an acknowledgment that God's sufficiency is undiminished if it is not granted.

Al-Sulamī records a higher reading in which the prayer is that Zachariah not be left bereft of intimacy with God and without a way leading to Him, so childlessness is read as spiritual isolation.

The higher reading is marked as such by its designation and preserved alongside the literal one, so the request for a son and the request for continued intimacy with God are both presented as valid readings of the same words, without either displacing the other or being subordinated to it.

The verse's placement after Jonah's account varies the catalogue's content, since this cry concerns succession rather than deliverance, and the variation keeps the sequence from becoming uniform. Jonah asked to be rescued from a present distress; Zachariah asks that he not be left without a future to pass on. The two forms of need differ in kind, and the sūrah's accumulation of differing needs is what gives this catalogue its range rather than merely its repetition.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:90

> **So We answered him, and bestowed John upon him, and We set his wife aright for him. Truly they vied in good deeds. They called upon Us with desire and with fear, and they were humble before Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**So We Answered Him**

The verse opens with the answer, as the preceding accounts do, and the answer is stated before its content. The sūrah records that God answered and then what the answer included.

The immediacy of the response is consistent with the treatment of Noah, Job, and Jonah. The cry is answered, and the answering is recorded without narrative elaboration.

The pattern is by now established across the catalogue, and the repetition of the formula emphasizes the reliability of the answer rather than the distinctiveness of each case.

**And Bestowed John Upon Him**

The answer is the bestowal of John, and the bestowal is stated as a gift. The naming of the son is the fulfilment of the request not to be left childless.

The verse does not narrate the circumstances of the birth, which are available in the parallel accounts. What is recorded is the fact of the bestowal and the name of the one bestowed.

The economy is consistent with the catalogue's method. The fulfilment is stated, and the narrative is presumed, which allows the sequence to move to the characterization that follows.

**And We Set His Wife Aright for Him**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī interpret *set his wife aright* as God making her able to bear children, since she is described as barren in 3:40. The reading connects the phrase to the obstacle the parallel account records.

The commentators add that it can also mean something like making her physically sound and healthy in this regard. The second reading is broader and less specific about the mechanism.

Both readings are preserved, and the preservation indicates that the phrase was understood in more than one way. The commentators record the range rather than settling on a single gloss.

**Truly They Vied in Good Deeds**

Al-Qushayrī records that some commentators consider *they vied in good deeds* to include not only Zachariah, his wife, and John, but also the other prophets already mentioned in this sūrah.

The broader reading extends the characterization across the catalogue. The vying is not confined to the family but encompasses the prophets named in the preceding verses.

The reading is preserved as the view of some commentators rather than as the settled meaning, and the narrower reading remains available. The plurality is recorded without resolution.

**They Called Upon Us with Desire and with Fear**

Al-Qushayrī explains that to call upon God with both desire and fear means they prayed in times of both ease and hardship, out of both hope and distress.

The explanation establishes the pair as complementary rather than opposed. Desire and fear correspond to ease and hardship, and the prayer encompasses both conditions.

The characterization is what the verse offers as exemplary. The prayer is not confined to moments of need but extends across the whole range of circumstances, and the range is what the pair expresses.

**And They Were Humble Before Us**

The verse closes with humility, and the humility is directed toward God. The characterization completes the description of their prayer: desire, fear, and humility.

The three elements together describe a disposition rather than a technique. The prayer is characterized by what the one praying feels and how he stands before God, not by its form.

The closing emphasis on humility is significant. It is the disposition that unifies desire and fear, and it is stated last as the summarizing characterization.

**The Answer's Completeness**

The verse records a request for an heir and an answer that includes a son, the removal of the wife's barrenness, and a characterization of the family's devotion.

The answer thus exceeds the request. Zachariah asked not to be left childless, and the answer supplies a son, a wife made able to bear, and a place for the family among those who vied in good deeds.

The excess is consistent with the treatment of Job's restoration, where the like of what was lost was added. The answers in this sūrah consistently exceed the requests that occasioned them.

---

**The Bestowal and the Characterization**

The verse records an answer that exceeds the request in three ways: a son is bestowed, the wife's barrenness is addressed, and the family's devotion is characterized as exemplary.

The bestowal of John is stated as a gift and named, and the naming is the fulfilment of the request not to be left childless, while the circumstances of the birth are left to the parallels.

Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī read set his wife aright as God making her able to bear children, connecting the phrase to her description as barren in 3:40, and add a broader reading of physical soundness.

The preservation of both readings indicates the phrase was understood in more than one way, and the commentators record the range rather than settling on a single gloss.

Al-Qushayrī records that some take they vied in good deeds to include the other prophets already mentioned in the sūrah, extending the characterization beyond Zachariah's family.

That broader reading is preserved as the view of some rather than as the settled meaning, so the narrower reading remains available and the plurality is recorded without resolution.

The explanation of calling upon God with desire and fear as praying in both ease and hardship, out of both hope and distress, establishes the pair as complementary rather than opposed.

The closing emphasis on humility unifies desire and fear into a single disposition, and the three together characterize prayer by what the one praying feels rather than by its external form.

The excess of the answer over the request parallels Job's restoration, where the like of what was lost was added, so the answers in this sūrah consistently exceed what occasioned them.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:91

> **And as for she who preserved her chastity, We breathed into her of Our Spirit, and made her and her son a sign for the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**She Who Preserved Her Chastity**

Al-Ṭabarī records that this verse refers to Mary, who is also described in 66:12 as one who preserved her chastity. The identification is supplied because the verse names her only by description.

The designation is thus an attribute rather than a name, and the attribute is the one the parallel passage also uses. The two verses together establish the characterization.

The use of description rather than name is consistent with the Qur'an's treatment of Mary elsewhere. She is identified by her quality, and the quality is what the verse emphasizes.

**We Breathed into Her of Our Spirit**

The verse states the Divine act without elaboration of its mechanism. The breathing is recorded, and the Spirit is designated as God's, and the two together describe the conception.

The sūrah does not explain how the breathing occurred, and the commentators do not supply a mechanism here. What is recorded is the act and its attribution to God.

The restraint is characteristic of the Qur'anic account. The miracle is stated, and its mode is left undescribed, which preserves the account from speculation about its operation.

**And Made Her and Her Son a Sign**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Rāzī explain that Mary and Jesus together were a sign in the sense that the miracle of the virgin birth involved both of them. The sign is joint rather than individual.

The joint character of the sign is what the verse emphasizes by naming both. The miracle required both mother and son, and the sign is constituted by their combination.

The explanation is preserved as the commentators' reading, and it accounts for why the verse pairs them rather than treating each separately.

**Other Signs Related Independently**

Al-Rāzī adds that other signs are related to Mary or Jesus independently of one another, including the mysterious nourishment Mary received in the Temple at 3:37 and Jesus' speaking in the cradle at 19:29–30.

The addition distinguishes the joint sign from the individual ones. Not every miracle associated with them involves both, and the commentators mark the difference.

The distinction is methodologically important. It prevents the joint sign from being read as the only one, and preserves the separate accounts of each figure's miracles.

**A Sign for the Worlds**

The verse designates the sign as being for the worlds, and the designation extends its address beyond a single community. The sign is universal in its scope.

The plurality of worlds is the standard Qur'anic formulation for the entirety of creation, and its use here indicates the sign's comprehensive address.

The universality is what distinguishes this sign from those given to particular peoples. It is not a proof for one community but a sign for all, and the designation states that scope.

**The Verse's Place in the Catalogue**

The verse concludes the sequence of prophetic accounts that began with Abraham. Its position places Mary and Jesus at the end of the catalogue, and the placement is significant.

The inclusion of Mary among the prophets' accounts is notable. She is not a prophet in the usual sense, but her account is given alongside theirs, and the inclusion extends the catalogue.

The extension indicates that the sūrah's purpose is not a list of prophets but a sequence of Divine acts and their recipients. Mary's account belongs to that sequence.

**The Sign and the Preceding Accounts**

The verse's emphasis on the sign connects it to the preceding accounts, each of which records a Divine act. The pattern is consistent: God acts, and the act is recorded as evidence.

The sign is thus the culmination of the sequence. The earlier accounts record gifts and deliverances, and this one records a sign for the worlds, which is the most comprehensive designation.

The culmination is what closes the catalogue. The sūrah moves from particular gifts to a universal sign, and the movement completes the sequence's argument.

---

**The Description, the Act, and the Sign**

The verse identifies Mary by an attribute rather than by name, and al-Ṭabarī supplies the identification while noting that 66:12 describes her in the same terms, so the two verses establish the characterization.

The Divine act is stated without elaboration of its mechanism: God breathed into her of His Spirit, and the sūrah does not explain how the breathing occurred, nor do the commentators supply a mechanism here.

The restraint preserves the account from speculation about the miracle's operation, which is characteristic of the Qur'anic treatment of the conception throughout.

The commentators' account of why the two are paired is that the virgin birth required both of them, so the sign they constitute is a joint one and not the property of either alone.

The joint character is what the verse emphasizes by naming both, and the explanation accounts for why they are paired rather than treated separately.

Al-Rāzī adds that other signs are related to each independently, including the mysterious nourishment Mary received in the Temple at 3:37 and Jesus' speaking in the cradle at 19:29–30.

The addition is methodologically important because it prevents the joint sign from being read as the only one, and preserves the separate accounts of each figure's miracles.

The designation of the sign as being for the worlds extends its address beyond a single community, and the universality distinguishes it from signs given to particular peoples.

The verse concludes the sequence that began with Abraham, and the inclusion of Mary among the prophetic accounts indicates that the sūrah's subject is Divine acts and their recipients rather than a list of prophets.

The movement from particular gifts to a universal sign is what closes the catalogue, and the closing designation is the most comprehensive in the sequence. Armour, wind, dominion over jinn, an heir, and a deliverance from grief were given to particular prophets for particular purposes. The sign given through Mary and her son is addressed to the worlds, and the widening of address is the note on which the sequence ends.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:92

> **Truly this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord. So worship Me!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Truly This Community of Yours Is One Community**

The verse turns from the catalogue of prophets to a direct address, and the turn is marked by the shift to the second person. What preceded was narrative; what follows is instruction given to those addressed.

Al-Qushayrī notes the parallel at 23:52, indicating that the formulation is not unique to this sūrah. The two verses state the same claim in similar terms, and the parallel establishes the idiom.

The designation of the community as one is the verse's central assertion, and it is stated before the command to worship that follows. The unity is the premise, and the command is its consequence.

The address is to a single community rather than to several, and the singularity is what the rest of the verse develops. The prophets named were many; the community addressed is one.

**The Accusative and Its Readings**

Al-Qushayrī records that *one community* is in the accusative, which causes some to read the verse as meaning that this community will continue to be one as long as it holds to the truth.

The conditional reading makes the unity contingent rather than guaranteed. The community remains one on a condition, and the condition is its adherence to the truth.

The grammatical observation is what generates the reading. The accusative case permits a construction that the nominative would not, and the commentators note the consequence for meaning.

The reading is preserved as the view of some rather than as the settled sense, and the alternatives that follow are recorded alongside it without any being excluded.

**This Religion of Yours Is One**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record an alternative reading in which the verse means that this religion of yours is one. The substitution of religion for community shifts the referent from a group to a teaching.

The shift is significant because it locates the unity in doctrine rather than in membership. The community is one because its religion is one, and the religion's unity is what constitutes the community's.

Al-Qushayrī adds a third reading: that despite the multiplicity of the prophets mentioned in this sūrah, the doctrine and reality of God's Oneness is always the same.

The third reading is the one that connects most directly to the preceding catalogue. The prophets were many and their circumstances differed, but the doctrine they carried was identical.

**The Three Readings Together**

The commentators preserve all three readings without preferring one, and the preservation indicates that the verse was understood in more than one way from an early period.

The readings are not mutually exclusive. The community may be one, the religion may be one, and the doctrine of Divine Oneness may be unchanged, and the three claims are compatible.

The preservation is methodologically consistent with the commentators' treatment of other contested verses in this sūrah. Where the text admits several senses, the senses are recorded rather than reduced.

The reader is thus given the range of the tradition rather than a single interpretation, and the range is itself informative about how the verse was received.

**And I Am Your Lord**

The verse's second clause states the Divine Lordship, and the statement is in the first person. The shift from description of the community to assertion of Lordship is abrupt and deliberate.

The Lordship is stated as the ground of the command that follows. God is your Lord, and therefore worship Me, and the therefore is supplied by the sequence rather than stated.

The first-person formulation makes the address direct. The verse is not about God but spoken by God, and the speaking is what gives the command its authority.

The combination of the community's unity and God's Lordship establishes the two poles of the verse. One community, one Lord, and the correspondence is what the command expresses.

**So Worship Me**

The command is the verse's conclusion, and it is stated in the imperative with no qualification. The worship is directed to God alone, and the direction is exclusive.

The exclusivity is what connects the command to the doctrine of Oneness recorded in the third reading. If the doctrine is always the same, the command is always the same, and the command is to worship God.

The brevity of the command is striking after the grammatical discussion the verse has generated. The commentators debate the case of a noun, and the verse ends with two words of instruction.

The command is thus the verse's point. The unity of the community and the Lordship of God are stated so that the command may be given, and the command is what remains.

**The Verse After the Catalogue**

The verse's position after the accounts of the prophets is significant. The catalogue has shown many prophets, and the verse draws from it a single conclusion about the community and its worship.

The conclusion is that the multiplicity of prophets does not imply a multiplicity of religions. The doctrine was the same throughout, and the community addressed is one.

The verse thus interprets the catalogue for the reader. What preceded was a sequence of narratives, and the verse states what the sequence amounts to.

The interpretation is not left to inference. The sūrah draws its own conclusion, and the conclusion is that the community is one and its worship is directed to one Lord.

**The Cross-References on Unity**

The commentators direct the reader to 2:213, 3:19, 5:48, and 42:14 on the notion of one community and the disagreements and fragmentation among earlier religious communities.

The cross-references indicate that the theme is developed across the Qur'an rather than stated only here. The unity and the fragmentation are both treated elsewhere.

The references also prepare for the next verse, which records the fragmentation. The reader is directed to the fuller treatment before the fragmentation is stated.

The accumulation of references shows that the verse belongs to a sustained Qur'anic reflection on religious community, and the reflection is not confined to this sūrah.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:93

> **But they have fragmented their affair among themselves. Each is returning unto Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**But They Have Fragmented Their Affair**

The verse turns from the unity asserted at verse 92 to the fragmentation that followed, and the turn is marked by the adversative. The community was one, and it fragmented.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that *they have fragmented their affair* means the communities to which the prophets were sent came to disagree over matters of religion.

Al-Rāzī adds that they broke up into sects and denominations. The two glosses together describe both the disagreement and its institutional result.

The fragmentation is thus not merely a difference of opinion but a division into groups, and the division is what the verse records as the outcome of the disagreement.

**The Disagreement and Its Object**

Al-Ṭabarī specifies that the disagreement concerned matters of religion. The fragmentation was not over politics or interest but over the content of the faith itself.

The specification is important because it distinguishes this fragmentation from ordinary division. The communities divided over what they believed, and the division was therefore doctrinal.

The verse does not name the matters in dispute, and the commentators do not supply them here. What is recorded is the fact of disagreement over religion rather than its particular occasions.

The generality allows the verse to apply across the communities named in the catalogue. Each was sent a prophet, and each came to disagree over matters of religion.

**Sects and Denominations**

Al-Rāzī's gloss that they broke up into sects and denominations describes the institutional form the disagreement took. The division was not temporary but structured.

The formation of sects indicates that the disagreement persisted and organized itself. What began as disagreement became affiliation, and the affiliation outlasted the original dispute.

The verse's economy — a single clause — compresses this development. The commentators supply the detail, and the detail shows that the fragmentation was a process rather than an event.

The process is what the cross-references to 2:213, 3:19, 5:48, and 42:14 develop more fully, and the sūrah relies on those treatments rather than repeating them.

**Each Is Returning unto Us**

The verse's closing clause is the answer to the fragmentation, and the answer is not a resolution of the disagreement but a statement of return. Each is returning unto God.

The distributive *each* is significant. The return is individual rather than collective, and the fragmentation into groups does not produce a collective return.

The clause thus relativizes the divisions. The sects and denominations are real, and each member of them returns individually, and the individual return is what the verse emphasizes.

The return is future, and its futurity is what gives the clause its force. The disagreement is present, the return is coming, and the coming return is the verse's consolation and its warning.

**The Return and the Divisions**

The juxtaposition of fragmentation and return is the verse's structure. The divisions are recorded, and the return is stated, and the two are held together without resolution.

The absence of resolution is deliberate. The verse does not adjudicate the disagreements or reconcile the sects; it states that each is returning, and the return is what matters.

This treatment preserves the seriousness of the fragmentation without making it ultimate. The divisions are real and they are not final, and both claims are made in one verse.

The clause thus functions as both warning and consolation. Those who fragmented will return, and the return is to the One they fragmented from.

**The Verse's Relation to Verse 92**

The two verses form a pair: unity asserted, fragmentation recorded. The pairing is what gives the sequence its argument, and the argument is stated rather than implied.

The unity is not described as lost. Verse 92 asserts it in the present, and verse 93 records the fragmentation, and the two statements are not reconciled within the text.

The commentators' readings of verse 92 allow the tension to stand. If the unity is conditional on holding to the truth, the fragmentation is the failure of the condition.

The conditional reading thus connects the two verses. The community remains one as long as it holds to the truth, and the fragmentation is what happens when it does not.

**The Fragmentation in the Qur'an**

The cross-references supplied by the commentators show that the theme of fragmentation is treated repeatedly. The Qur'an returns to the division of earlier communities in several places.

The recurrence indicates that the theme is central rather than incidental. The unity of the community and its fragmentation are both sustained concerns.

The treatment elsewhere provides the fuller account that this verse summarizes. The sūrah states the fragmentation in a clause and relies on the reader's familiarity with the theme.

The reliance is consistent with the sūrah's method throughout. It states what its own argument needs and presumes that the wider treatment of the theme is already available to the reader elsewhere in the Qur'an.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse completes the movement begun at verse 92 and prepares for the eschatological sequence that follows. Unity, fragmentation, and return form a progression.

The progression is the sūrah's account of religious community. It begins with a claim about unity, records the division that followed, and states the return that will conclude it.

The account is not optimistic about the divisions but it is not despairing either. The fragmentation is real, and the return is certain, and the two are stated together.

The verse thus serves as a hinge between the two halves of the sūrah's argument. It closes the reflection on the prophets and the communities sent to them, and opens the sequence on judgment that occupies the following verses. The hinge is the individual return: the communities divided into sects and denominations, but the return is not collective, and it is precisely this irreducibly individual character of the return that carries the whole argument into what follows.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:94

> **And whosoever performs righteous deeds and is a believer, there shall be no ingratitude for his endeavor, and surely We shall write it down for him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whosoever Performs Righteous Deeds and Is a Believer**

The verse states a condition in two parts, and the order is notable: righteous deeds are named first, and belief second. The combination is what the verse requires.

The commentators direct the reader to 2:62c and 5:69c for the theological issues raised, namely what it means to be a believer and perform righteous deeds so that one is saved.

The cross-references indicate that the condition is treated elsewhere in the Qur'an, and the treatment is substantive rather than incidental. The question of who is saved is a sustained concern.

The reference to the essay on the Qur'anic view of sacred history and other religions shows that the verse bears on relations between religious communities, not only on individual salvation.

**There Shall Be No Ingratitude for His Endeavor**

The verse's promise is stated negatively: there shall be no ingratitude. The formulation denies the withholding of recognition rather than asserting its bestowal.

Al-Qushayrī notes that the idea of gratitude on God's part is mentioned in 2:158, 4:147, 35:30, 42:23, and 64:17, so the idiom is established across the Qur'an.

The attribution of gratitude to God is theologically significant. It describes the Divine response to human endeavor in terms normally reserved for human relations.

The accumulation of references shows that the idiom is not isolated here. The Qur'an repeatedly describes God as grateful, and the description is a settled feature of its vocabulary.

**The Negative Formulation**

The denial of ingratitude is stronger than an assertion of gratitude would be, because it excludes the possibility of the endeavor being disregarded rather than merely promising a reward.

The formulation addresses a specific anxiety: that effort might go unrecognized. The verse denies that this will occur, and the denial is unqualified.

The unqualified denial is what gives the promise its force. It is not that the endeavor will be partly recognized but that no ingratitude will be shown toward it at all.

The verse thus answers a concern about the relation between effort and recognition, and the answer is stated as an exclusion rather than as a comparison.

**And Surely We Shall Write It Down**

Al-Qushayrī notes that the phrase can also be rendered in the present tense, *We write it down*, referring to the recording of human deeds often mentioned in the Qur'an.

The references supplied are 10:21, 36:12c, 45:28–29c, and 78:29, indicating that the theme of recording is widespread. The deeds are written, and the writing is a recurring assertion.

The commentators add that the phrase can refer to God's writing these deeds in the Mother of the Book at 13:39, or to the book of deeds presented on the Day of Judgment.

The three readings — future recording, present recording, and recording in a specific book — are preserved together, and the preservation indicates the phrase's range.

**The Recording and Its Forms**

The distinction between the tenses matters. A future recording promises that the deeds will be written; a present recording asserts that they are being written now.

The reference to the Mother of the Book locates the recording in a specific Divine register, and the reference to the book of deeds locates it in an eschatological presentation.

The three forms are not mutually exclusive. The deeds may be recorded now, written in the Mother of the Book, and presented on the Day of Judgment, and the readings describe different aspects.

The commentators' preservation of all three allows the verse to carry the whole range, and the range is what makes the promise comprehensive.

**The Condition and the Promise**

The verse's structure is condition followed by promise, and the promise has two parts: no ingratitude for the endeavor, and its recording. The two together constitute the assurance.

The recording is what secures the non-ingratitude. The endeavor is not overlooked because it is written, and the writing is the guarantee that it will be acknowledged.

The connection between the two parts is implicit but clear. The verse does not explain why there will be no ingratitude, but the recording supplies the reason.

The assurance is thus grounded in a Divine act rather than in a disposition or an intention. God will not be ungrateful because God records, and the recording itself is stated as an accomplished fact rather than as a promise yet to be fulfilled.

**The Verse's Audience**

The verse is addressed to whoever meets the condition, and the formulation is general rather than restricted. The condition is stated, and whoever meets it is included.

The generality is what connects the verse to the cross-references on other religions. If the condition is belief and righteous deeds, its application is not confined to a single community.

The commentators' direction to the theological discussions indicates that the generality was contested and debated, and the debates are recorded rather than settled in the commentary.

The verse thus raises a question it does not itself answer, and the commentators preserve the question intact along with the substantive discussions it generated across the tradition.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

The verse follows the account of unity and fragmentation and precedes the eschatological sequence. Its position places individual responsibility between communal division and coming judgment.

The placement is significant. The communities fragmented, and the verse states that each individual's endeavor is recorded, and the recording is what survives the fragmentation.

The connection to verse 93's *each is returning unto Us* is close. The return is individual, and the recording is individual, and the two verses together emphasize personal accountability.

The emphasis is what prepares for the judgment scene at verse 97, where those who disbelieved confront the very thing they were heedless of, and the confrontation is recorded in their own words.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:95

> **And it is forbidden unto any town We have destroyed that they should ever return,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Apparent Double Negative**

The verse's Arabic contains an apparent double negative, and the commentators discuss and interpret it in order to avoid a meaning that would reverse the intended sense.

The reading to be avoided is that it is forbidden unto any destroyed town that they shall not return, which would mean that they would necessarily return to the world.

The commentators' concern is thus with a literal parsing that produces an unintended sense, and their interpretations are directed at avoiding that result.

The discussion is preserved in the commentary, and the preservation indicates that the difficulty was recognized and addressed rather than passed over.

**The Literal Reading**

Al-Rāzī records that if read as a literal double negative, the verse says the destroyed will inevitably and necessarily return in the Hereafter to God.

The reading is supported by the allusion to verses stating that unto God are all matters are returned, with 22:76 given as an example.

The reading thus transforms the difficulty into an eschatological assertion. The destroyed do not return to the world, but they do return to God, and the return is inevitable.

The transformation is what makes the literal reading viable. The double negative is not an error but a statement about the inescapability of the return to God.

**It Is Decided or Resolved**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that some read *it is forbidden* as *it is decided or resolved that*, meaning God has determined that the destroyed will not return to the world.

Al-Qushayrī records a further reading in which it means they will not repent, and notes that *tawbah* also carries the sense of return.

The semantic range of *tawbah* is what generates the second reading. If repentance is a return, then the denial of return can be read as the denial of repentance.

The two readings differ in what is denied: return to the world, or repentance. Both are preserved, and the difference is recorded without resolution.

**The Range of Readings**

The commentators preserve at least three interpretations: inevitable return to God, no return to the world, and no repentance. Each is recorded with its source.

The readings are not equivalent. The first asserts a return, the second denies a return to the world, and the third denies repentance, and the three make different claims.

The preservation of divergent readings is methodologically significant. The commentators do not harmonize them but record them, and the record shows the verse's difficulty.

The difficulty is acknowledged rather than concealed. The verse's Arabic is problematic, and the commentary presents the problem along with the proposed solutions.

**The Towns Destroyed**

The verse concerns towns God has destroyed, and the reference connects it to the prophetic accounts preceding. The towns destroyed are those that rejected their prophets.

The verse thus extends the catalogue's application. What was narrated about individual prophets concerns communities, and the communities' destruction is what the verse addresses.

The reference to destruction is unelaborated. The verse does not describe the destruction or name the towns, relying on the reader's familiarity with the accounts.

The reliance is consistent with the sūrah's method. It states what it needs and presumes the narrative is available elsewhere.

**The Impossibility of Return**

The verse's central claim, on the majority reading, is that the destroyed cannot return. The impossibility is stated as a Divine determination rather than as a natural fact.

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī's gloss that it is decided or resolved makes the impossibility a matter of Divine decree. The towns do not return because God has determined it.

The decretal framing is significant. The impossibility is not contingent on circumstances but fixed by determination, and the determination is what the verse records.

The framing connects the verse to the eschatological sequence that follows. What cannot be undone in the world will be confronted in the Hereafter.

**Repentance and Return**

Al-Qushayrī's reading that the verse means they will not repent relies on the semantic range of *tawbah*, which carries the sense of return as well as repentance.

The reading connects the verse to a theme central to the sūrah. The prophets called their communities to repentance, and the communities that were destroyed did not repent.

The reading thus gives the verse a moral dimension. The destruction is final not only physically but spiritually, and the finality is what the verse asserts.

The connection between return and repentance embedded in the Arabic root is what allows this reading, and the commentators note the semantic link explicitly rather than leaving the reader to infer it from the gloss alone.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse closes the account of the destroyed communities and prepares for the eschatological sequence beginning at verse 96. Its position is transitional.

The transition is from what cannot be undone to what is coming. The destroyed cannot return, and Gog and Magog will be unleashed, and the two statements frame the sequence.

The verse thus serves the sūrah's movement from prophetic history to eschatology. The history is concluded, and the coming events are introduced.

The conclusion is stated in terms of impossibility, which gives the transition its weight. What is past is fixed beyond recall, and what is coming is certain beyond avoidance, and the two certainties face each other across the verse. The destroyed cannot return to the world they were removed from, and the world they were removed from is itself approaching the event that will conclude it. The commentators' difficulty with the Arabic double negative is a difficulty about how to state an impossibility, and the several readings they preserve are so many attempts to say that what has been decreed cannot be undone. That the attempts differ is itself instructive, since the impossibility itself is agreed on by every one of the readers even where its precise formulation is not.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:96

> **till the time when Gog and Magog are unleashed, and they rush down from every hill,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Till the Time When Gog and Magog Are Unleashed**

The verse continues the sentence begun at verse 95, and the continuation specifies the limit of the impossibility. The destroyed cannot return until a specified time.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that Gog and Magog are described as vicious and destructive beings locked away by Dhu'l-Qarnayn in 18:94–99, and directs the reader to the commentary on those verses.

The cross-reference is essential because the sūrah does not describe Gog and Magog here. Their character and their confinement are established in Sūrah 18, and this verse relies on that account.

The reliance is consistent with the sūrah's method. It names the figures and presumes the reader knows who they are and what was done to them.

**The Confinement and Its Ending**

The description of Gog and Magog as locked away by Dhu'l-Qarnayn establishes that their confinement was deliberate and constructed. A rampart was built to hold them in.

The unleashing is therefore the ending of a confinement that was imposed, and the ending is what the verse announces as a coming event.

The connection between the confinement and the unleashing is what gives the verse its force. What was locked away will be released, and the release is specified as a future occurrence.

The verse thus depends on the reader's knowledge of Sūrah 18 for its full significance, and the dependence is acknowledged by the commentators' cross-reference.

**Unleashed Renders Futiḥat**

Al-Ṭabarī notes that *unleashed* renders *futiḥat*, which can literally mean opened, referring to the opening of the rampart that was built to hold them in.

The literal sense is thus spatial: an opening is made in a structure. The translation as unleashed conveys the consequence rather than the literal action.

The reference to 18:95 confirms the connection to the rampart, and the reference indicates that the opening is of the structure Dhu'l-Qarnayn built.

The commentators' note preserves the literal sense alongside the translation, and the preservation shows what the Arabic says as distinct from what it implies.

**And They Rush Down from Every Hill**

The verse describes the manner of their emergence: they rush down from every hill. The description is of speed and of comprehensiveness.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that *hill* can mean any high place, so the reference is not confined to hills in the narrow sense but extends to elevated ground generally.

The gloss broadens the description. The rushing down is from every high place, and the broadening emphasizes the totality of the emergence.

The totality is what the verse conveys. Not some of them from some places, but they from every hill, and the universality is the description's point.

**The Manner of Emergence**

The rushing down indicates that the emergence is rapid and uncontrolled. The confinement ends, and what was confined moves quickly and in all directions.

The description is vivid without being elaborated. The verse states the action and its scope, and the commentators supply only the gloss on hill.

The vividness serves the eschatological purpose. The event is described so that its terror is conveyed, and the description is economical.

The economy is characteristic of the sūrah's eschatological references. The events are named and briefly described, and the fuller treatment is elsewhere.

**The Verse's Eschatological Function**

The verse introduces the eschatological sequence that verse 97 develops. The unleashing of Gog and Magog is the first of the signs the sequence records.

Al-Ṭabarī notes at verse 97 that the coming of Gog and Magog is a sign of the impending end of the world, which confirms the verse's function.

The function is thus to mark the beginning of the end. What precedes is prophetic history; what follows is the conclusion of the world.

The transition is abrupt. The verse moves from the impossibility of the destroyed returning to the release of Gog and Magog, and the movement is unexplained.

**The Connection to Verse 95**

The two verses form a single sentence, and the connection is temporal. The destroyed cannot return until the time when Gog and Magog are unleashed.

The temporal clause thus sets a limit. The impossibility is not eternal in the sense of unending but lasts until a specified event.

The specification is what links the two verses. The event that ends the impossibility is named, and the naming connects the account of the destroyed to the eschatological sequence.

The connection is grammatical as well as thematic, and the commentators' treatment of the two verses together reflects this.

**The Significance of the Sign**

The unleashing of Gog and Magog is a sign, and its character as a sign is what connects it to the true promise named at verse 97.

The sign is public and unmistakable. The rushing down from every hill is not a hidden event but a visible one, and its visibility is what makes it a sign.

The verse thus prepares for the confrontation at verse 97, where those who disbelieved recognize what they were heedless of.

The preparation is what gives the sequence its coherence. The sign comes, the promise draws near, and the heedless recognize both, and the recognition is recorded in their own words at the following verse. The unleashing is thus not an isolated marvel but the first term in an argument: what was confined is released, what was promised approaches, and those who paid no attention are confronted by what they neglected. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss that hill can mean any high place widens the scene from a particular landscape to elevated ground without exception, and the widening is what makes the emergence total rather than partial. The totality matters, since a sign that appeared in one place could be explained away or dismissed, while one that appears from every high place at once cannot be dismissed by anyone at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:97

> **and the true promise draws nigh. And, behold, the eyes of the disbelievers are upcast, "Oh, woe unto us! We have certainly been heedless of this; indeed, we have been wrongdoers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**And the True Promise Draws Nigh**

Al-Rāzī records that *the true promise* refers to the Day of Resurrection. The identification supplies what the phrase denotes, and the identification is preserved as the commentator's reading.

The designation of the promise as true distinguishes it from other promises. The Day of Resurrection is the promise that will not fail, and the truth is its defining characteristic.

The drawing near indicates imminence, and the imminence is what connects the verse to the preceding sign. Gog and Magog are unleashed, and the promise draws near.

The connection is sequential. The sign precedes the promise, and the sequence is what the two verses together establish.

**The Fixed Stare**

Al-Ṭabarī notes that the second phrase could also be read to mean that the stare of those who disbelieved will be fixed, and the alternative reading is preserved.

Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī explain that this will occur because of the fear they experience in the face of this horrible event. The stare is thus an effect of terror.

The cross-references to 14:42, where God grants reprieve till a day when eyes will stare transfixed, and to 80:34–37, on how people react to their doom, establish the idiom.

The accumulation of references shows that the fixed stare is a recurring eschatological image, and the image is associated specifically with the reaction of those confronted by judgment.

**The Two Readings of the Stare**

The verse's phrasing admits two readings, and the commentators preserve both. The stare may be described as there shall be the fixed stare, or as the stare will be fixed.

The difference is grammatical rather than substantive. Both readings describe the same phenomenon, and the preservation indicates that the parsing was discussed.

The commentators' note that the stare results from fear applies to either reading, so the interpretation is not affected by the choice between them.

The preservation is consistent with the commentators' method. Where the text admits alternative parsings, the alternatives are recorded without a preference being imposed.

**Oh, Woe unto Us**

The verse records the exclamation of those who disbelieved, and the exclamation is the first of three statements. The woe is acknowledged, and the acknowledgment is immediate.

The exclamation is not described but quoted. The verse gives the words, and the words are what convey the recognition.

The immediacy is significant. The stare is followed at once by the exclamation, and the sequence indicates that recognition and acknowledgment occur together.

The quotation is what makes the verse vivid. The reader is given the words of those confronted by the promise, and the words are their own.

**We Have Certainly Been Heedless of This**

Al-Rāzī explains that *we have certainly been heedless of this* reflects the fact that in the life of the world they paid no attention to such matters.

The confession is thus specific: the heedlessness was of this, that is, of the Day of Resurrection and what it involves. The neglect was directed at the very thing now confronting them.

The connection to the worldly life is what gives the confession its force. The heedlessness occurred then, and the recognition occurs now, and the gap between them is the verse's point.

The verse thus identifies the failure precisely. It was not ignorance but inattention, and the inattention was sustained through the whole of the worldly life.

**Indeed, We Have Been Wrongdoers**

The final confession is general: we have been wrongdoers. The specification of heedlessness is followed by an acknowledgment of wrongdoing without qualification.

The progression from specific to general is notable. The verse moves from heedlessness of this to wrongdoing generally, and the movement broadens the confession.

The broadening is what completes the acknowledgment. Those confronted do not limit their admission to a single failure but acknowledge wrongdoing as such.

The confession is thus complete. The woe is acknowledged, the heedlessness is specified, and the wrongdoing is admitted, and the three statements form a full recognition.

**Gog and Magog as Symbol**

The commentary notes that Gog and Magog represent, at the level of spiritual symbolism, the preoccupations, tumult, and impressions that assault the soul.

The symbolic reading is preserved alongside the literal account of their unleashing, and the preservation indicates that the figures were read on more than one level.

The reading connects the eschatological sign to the spiritual life. What is unleashed at the end is also what assails the soul, and the connection is recorded.

The commentators' inclusion of the symbolic reading shows the range of interpretation the verse generated, and the range is presented without the literal reading being displaced.

**The Verse's Function**
