The verse completes the eschatological sequence begun at verse 96. The sign comes, the promise draws near, and those who disbelieved recognize what they neglected.

The completion is the sūrah's warning. The heedlessness that characterized the worldly life is confronted by the event that was neglected, and the confrontation is described.

The verse thus closes the whole movement from prophetic history through communal fragmentation to eschatological judgment, and this closure is the point toward which the entire sequence has been moving.

The point is that the promise is true and draws near, and the recognition of those who neglected it comes too late to alter it. Al-Rāzī's identification of the true promise with the Day of Resurrection fixes the reference, and al-Ṭabarī's note that the coming of Gog and Magog is a sign of the impending end connects the two verses. The confession is what the whole sequence exists to record: not that they were ignorant of the promise, but that they were heedless of it, and the difference between mere ignorance and sustained inattention is the whole of the warning.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:98

> **Surely you and that which you worship apart from God shall be fuel for Hell. Unto it you shall come.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Surely You and That Which You Worship Apart from God**

The verse addresses the worshippers and their objects of worship together, and the address is direct rather than descriptive. Al-Rāzī records that it is said to be addressed to the Makkān idolaters.

The identification of the addressees matters for the verse's occasion, and the commentators preserve it as a reported identification rather than as a certainty.

The pairing of worshippers and worshipped is what the verse turns on. Both are addressed, and both are included in what follows, and the inclusion is the verse's assertion.

The direct address distinguishes this verse from the narrative that preceded. What came before was an account of the prophets; what comes now is a statement addressed to those who rejected the message.

**Shall Be Fuel for Hell**

The verse states that both the worshippers and their gods shall be fuel for the Fire, and al-Qushayrī notes that being fuel for the Fire is also mentioned in 2:24, 3:10, and 66:6.

The accumulation of references shows that the image is established in the Qur'an rather than introduced here. The Fire's fuel is a recurring designation.

The image's force lies in its reversal. The objects of worship were regarded as powerful, and the verse places them among the fuel rather than among those who deliver.

The reversal is what makes the verse polemical. It does not merely deny the gods' divinity but assigns them the lowest position in the eschatological order.

**The Quraysh's Objection**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Rāzī record that when the Quraysh heard this verse directed against their gods, they became extremely agitated and responded by pointing out that Jesus was worshipped by Christians.

They also pointed out that some people worshipped angels. The objection was thus not a denial of the verse but an application of it to figures the Muslims honoured.

The commentators record that the Prophet ﷺ remained silent until verse 101 was revealed, which according to one interpretation made clear that Jesus, the angels, and other figures who did not invite worship of themselves would not be fuel for the Fire.

The silence and the subsequent revelation form a sequence the commentators preserve as the occasion of verse 101, and the sequence connects the two verses historically.

**The Alternative Reading of the Exchange**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Rāzī record that some do not interpret the relationship between verses 98 and 101 in this way at all, and say rather that verse 98 makes clear that it is the worshippers who are addressed.

The alternative denies the narrative connection between the two verses. On this reading verse 101 is not a response to an objection but an independent statement about a different group.

The commentators preserve both readings without preferring one, and the preservation indicates that the connection between the verses was contested.

The contestation is itself informative. It shows that the relation between the two verses was not settled, and that the occasion narrative was one interpretation among others.

**Unto It You Shall Come**

The verse closes with a statement of arrival, and the arrival is addressed to the same plural as the fuel. Both the worshippers and their gods come to the Fire.

The certainty of the arrival is stated without qualification. There is no condition and no exception, and the statement is made in the second person.

The direct address makes the arrival immediate. It is not a prediction about others but a statement to those addressed, and the address includes them in its scope.

The closing clause thus completes the verse's movement: identification of the addressees, statement of their fate, and assertion of its certainty.

**The Verse's Polemical Function**

The verse functions as a refutation of idolatry, and the refutation takes the form of an eschatological assignment rather than an argument. The gods are not disproved but reclassified.

The reclassification is what gives the verse its force. The question of whether the gods exist is not addressed; their position in the Fire is stated.

This mode of refutation is characteristic of the Qur'an's treatment of idolatry. The gods' inability to deliver is demonstrated by their inclusion among those who need delivering.

The demonstration is completed at verse 99, which draws the inference explicitly, and the two verses together form the argument.

**The Occasion and Its Transmission**

The commentators' preservation of the Quraysh's objection and the Prophet's silence indicates that the occasion was transmitted as part of the verse's history.

The transmission is marked as reported rather than as established, and the commentators note that some reject the connection altogether.

The marking is methodologically significant. The occasion narrative is preserved with its status indicated, and the reader is given both the narrative and the denial of it.

The treatment allows the verse to be read with or without the occasion, and the two readings are both available.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

The verse follows the eschatological sequence at verses 96–97 and continues it. The fixed stare and the confession are followed by the assignment of fuel.

The continuation is from recognition to consequence. Those who disbelieved recognized their heedlessness, and the verse states what follows from it.

The sequence thus moves from the coming of the Hour to the fate of those confronted by it, and the movement is unbroken across the verses.

The verse's position is therefore continuous with what precedes, and the continuity is what gives the passage its cumulative force.

---

**The Pairing and Its Logic**

The verse's force depends on pairing the worshippers with their gods, and the pairing is not incidental. Both are addressed in the same breath and both are assigned to the same place, and the assignment is what the argument at verse 99 will draw out.

Al-Rāzī's note that the verse is said to be addressed to the Makkān idolaters fixes the occasion, and the fixing matters because the polemic is directed at a specific practice rather than at idolatry in the abstract.

The Quraysh's agitation on hearing the verse shows that the pairing was understood as an attack on the gods themselves and not only on those who worshipped them. Their objection was that Jesus and the angels were also worshipped, and the objection presumed that the verse included the objects of worship in its scope.

The commentators' record of the Prophet's silence until verse 101 was revealed preserves the exchange as part of the verses' history, and the record is marked as reported rather than established.

Al-Qushayrī's note that some reject the connection between verses 98 and 101 altogether is preserved alongside the occasion narrative, so the reader is given both the story and its denial.

The denial matters because it changes what verse 98 says. If the verse addresses only the worshippers, the objection about Jesus and the angels never arises, and verse 101 needs no exemption to supply.

The contested relation between the two verses is thus itself part of the tradition, and the commentators transmit the contest rather than resolving it in favour of either side.

What is not contested is the image of fuel. Al-Qushayrī's references to 2:24, 3:10, and 66:6 show it is established vocabulary, and its force lies in the reversal it performs on the status of the gods.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:99

> **Had these been gods, they would not have come unto it. But each shall abide therein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Had These Been Gods**

The verse states a counterfactual, and the counterfactual is the argument the preceding verse prepared. If these had been gods, a consequence would have followed, and it did not.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that if the gods had been actual divinities, they would have been able to help their worshippers and keep them out of the Fire.

The explanation supplies the consequence that the verse leaves implicit. The gods' inability to keep their worshippers out is what demonstrates that they were not gods.

The demonstration is thus from effect to cause. The worshippers are in the Fire, the gods did not prevent it, and the non-prevention is the evidence.

**They Would Not Have Come Unto It**

The verse's conclusion is that the gods would not have come to the Fire had they been gods, and the coming is what disproves the claim.

The argument is compact. Divinity implies the power to deliver, the power was not exercised, and the gods themselves are among those who come.

The inclusion of the gods among those who come is the argument's decisive point. They are not merely unable to deliver others; they share the fate of those they failed to deliver.

The sharing is what completes the refutation. A god who suffers with his worshippers is not a god, and the suffering is stated as a fact.

**But Each Shall Abide therein**

Al-Ṭabarī explains that *each shall abide therein* means both the worshipper and the object of worship will be fuel for the Fire.

The gloss confirms the pairing established at verse 98. The distributive *each* covers both parties, and the abiding is common to them.

The abiding is permanent in its description. The verse does not limit its duration, and the commentators do not supply a limit.

The permanence is what distinguishes the abiding from a temporary punishment. Both parties remain, and the remaining is what the verse states.

**The Argument's Structure**

The verse's argument is a modus tollens, and its compactness is notable. Divinity implies deliverance, deliverance did not occur, therefore divinity is denied.

The argument is stated in a single clause rather than developed. The Qur'an does not elaborate the inference but states the counterfactual and its failure.

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss supplies the middle term, and the gloss shows what the verse assumes rather than states.

The compactness is characteristic of Qur'anic argumentation. The inference is left to the reader, and the commentary supplies what is needed to make it explicit.

**The Gods' Inability**

The verse's central claim is that the gods could not help their worshippers, and the inability is demonstrated by the outcome rather than asserted.

The demonstration is empirical in form. What happened shows what the gods could do, and what happened is that both parties are in the Fire.

The form of the demonstration is what makes it effective. It does not require premises about the nature of divinity beyond the expectation that a god can deliver.

The expectation is what the counterfactual states, and the failure of the expectation is what the verse records.

**The Worshippers' Position**

The verse treats the worshippers and their gods together, and the treatment is identical. Both abide in the Fire, and the abiding is undifferentiated.

The undifferentiated treatment is significant. The verse does not distinguish between those who worshipped and those who were worshipped in respect of their fate.

The absence of distinction is what verse 101 will qualify, since some commentators read that verse as exempting certain objects of worship.

The qualification is thus prepared here, and the two verses together establish both the general rule and its contested exception.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse completes the refutation begun at verse 98 and prepares for the description of the Fire that follows. Its position is between assertion and description.

The completion is logical rather than narrative. The argument is finished, and what follows describes the condition of those who are in the Fire.

The transition is thus from proof to depiction, and the depiction occupies verses 100 and following.

The verse's brevity is appropriate to its function. The argument needs no elaboration once stated, and the statement is complete.

**The Refutation and the Catalogue**

The verse's refutation of idolatry connects it back to the catalogue of prophets. The prophets called to the worship of one God, and the verse demonstrates the futility of the alternative.

The connection is thematic rather than explicit. The sūrah does not draw it, but the sequence from prophetic mission to eschatological refutation implies it.

The implication is that the prophets' message is vindicated by the outcome. The gods failed, and the failure confirms what the prophets said.

The confirmation is what gives the passage its place in the sūrah's argument, and the argument is completed by the descriptions that follow.

---

**The Counterfactual and Its Force**

The verse's argument is compact enough to be stated in a line, and its compactness is what makes it effective. Divinity implies the power to deliver those who worship; the power was not exercised; therefore the claim to divinity fails.

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss supplies the middle term the verse leaves implicit: had they been actual divinities they would have been able to help their worshippers and keep them out of the Fire.

The gloss shows that the argument runs from effect to cause rather than from definition to conclusion. What happened is the evidence, and what happened is that the gods did not prevent the Fire.

The decisive point is that the gods are themselves among those who come unto it. They do not merely fail to deliver others; they share the fate of those they failed, and the sharing is stated as fact.

Al-Ṭabarī's explanation that each shall abide therein covers both worshipper and object of worship confirms the pairing established at verse 98, so the distributive each is not limited to one party.

The undifferentiated treatment is what verse 101 will qualify, since some commentators read that verse as exempting unwilling objects of worship such as Jesus and the angels.

The qualification is prepared here rather than announced, and the two verses together establish a general rule together with a contested exception to it.

The refutation's form is worth noting because it is not a disproof of the gods' existence. The question whether they exist is never raised; their position in the eschatological order is simply assigned.

That assignment is what makes the argument persuasive without needing premises about the nature of divinity beyond the ordinary expectation that a god can save his worshippers.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:100

> **Theirs shall be groaning therein, and therein they shall hear not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Theirs Shall Be Groaning Therein**

The verse describes the condition of those in the Fire, and the description begins with sound. Al-Qushayrī notes that groaning, *zafīr*, is also mentioned in 11:106.

The cross-reference establishes the term as part of the Qur'an's eschatological vocabulary. The groaning is a designated feature of the Fire rather than an incidental description.

The attribution of groaning to the inhabitants is stated as their portion: *theirs shall be*. The groaning belongs to them, and the belonging is permanent.

The verse thus begins the depiction of the Fire's condition, and the depiction is auditory before it becomes anything else.

**And Therein They Shall Hear Not**

The verse's second clause denies hearing, and the commentators offer two interpretations of the denial. Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī record that it means each person will experience the suffering utterly alone.

The interpretation is supported by accounts describing the denizens of the Fire being placed into a container that is placed into another that is placed into yet another.

The nesting of containers is described so that they are unable to sense anything other than their own suffering, and the description explains how the isolation is achieved.

The isolation is thus not merely social but perceptual. The sufferer hears nothing because nothing outside his own suffering reaches him.

**The Resurrection Reading**

Al-Rāzī records that for some, *they shall hear not* refers to the fact that they will be resurrected deaf, as described in 17:97.

The verse cited states that God will gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces, blind, dumb, and deaf, and the citation supplies the basis for the reading.

The reading locates the deafness in the resurrection itself rather than in the Fire's conditions. The inability to hear is a state of the resurrected rather than an effect of confinement.

The two readings are preserved without preference, and the difference between them concerns where the deafness originates rather than whether it occurs.

**Isolation and Its Meaning**

The first reading makes the isolation the essence of the punishment. The sufferer is alone with his suffering, and the aloneness is what the verse emphasizes.

The nested containers are a vivid image, and the image's function is to explain how complete the isolation is. Nothing outside reaches the sufferer.

The completeness is what distinguishes this punishment from ordinary suffering. Ordinary suffering occurs among others; this occurs in total perceptual isolation.

The verse's denial of hearing is thus a statement about the character of the punishment rather than a mere physical description.

**The Two Readings Together**

The isolation reading and the resurrection reading are compatible. The resurrected may be deaf, and the conditions of the Fire may complete the isolation.

The commentators' preservation of both indicates that the verse was understood in more than one way, and the plurality is recorded rather than reduced.

The plurality enriches the description. The deafness is both a state of the resurrected and a feature of their confinement, and both are presented.

The treatment is methodologically consistent. Where the text admits several senses, the senses are recorded with their sources and none is excluded.

**The Groaning and the Silence**

The verse pairs groaning with the inability to hear, and the pairing is striking. There is sound, and there is no hearing of it.

The apparent tension is resolved by the isolation reading. Each groans, and none hears another, so the groaning is simultaneous and unshared.

The unshared groaning is what the verse conveys. The Fire is full of sound, and the sound is not communicative, and the absence of communication is the point.

The pairing is thus not a contradiction but a description of isolation achieved through the coexistence of noise and deafness.

**The Verse's Depictive Function**

The verse begins the sustained depiction of the Fire that continues through the following verses, and the depiction is sensory rather than argumentative.

The shift from argument at verse 99 to depiction at verse 100 is deliberate. The refutation is complete, and what follows shows the condition it entails.

The depiction is brief but vivid. Two clauses establish sound and silence, and the commentators supply the accounts that elaborate them.

The elaboration is preserved as tradition, and the tradition's vividness is what gives the depiction its force.

**The Contrast Prepared**

The verse's depiction of the Fire prepares the contrast with verses 101–103, which describe those kept far from it.

The contrast is between groaning and silence on one side, and the absence of even the slightest sound on the other.

The two silences are different in kind. The inhabitants of the Fire hear nothing because they are isolated; those kept far from it hear nothing because the Fire does not reach them.

The distinction is what the following verses develop, and the preparation is what gives the contrast its precision.

---

**Two Kinds of Not-Hearing**

The verse pairs groaning with the inability to hear, and the pairing looks contradictory until the commentators' glosses are supplied. There is sound in the Fire, and no one hears it.

Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī read they shall hear not as meaning each person will experience the suffering utterly alone, and the reading dissolves the contradiction by making the deafness relational rather than physical.

The accounts they cite describe the denizens placed into a container set inside another set inside yet another, so that nothing outside their own suffering reaches them, and the nesting explains how total the isolation is.

Al-Rāzī's alternative locates the deafness in the resurrection itself, citing 17:97 where God gathers them upon their faces, blind, dumb, and deaf, so the inability to hear is a state of the resurrected rather than an effect of confinement.

The two readings are compatible and the commentators preserve both without preferring either, so the deafness is simultaneously a condition of the resurrected and a feature of their enclosure.

What distinguishes this silence from the silence of verse 102 is its cause. Here nothing is heard because nothing reaches the sufferer; there nothing is heard because the Fire is far away.

The distinction matters because it shows that isolation and distance are not the same thing, and the Qur'an's two silences are not two descriptions of one condition.

Al-Qushayrī's note that groaning, zafīr, also occurs at 11:106 establishes the term as eschatological vocabulary, so the sound is a designated feature of the Fire and not an incidental detail.

The result is a punishment whose essence is solitude: the Fire is full of groaning, and the groaning communicates nothing, and the absence of communication is itself part of what is being described.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:101

> **Surely those for whom what is most beautiful has already gone forth from Us; they shall be kept far from it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those for Whom What Is Most Beautiful Has Gone Forth**

The verse describes a group by reference to something that has already gone forth from God, and the description is the verse's central feature.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some understand this as a statement of predestination regarding those whose destination in the Garden has already gone forth, or has already been foreordained by God.

The reading makes the verse a statement about Divine foreordination, and the foreordination concerns the destination rather than the deeds.

The reading is preserved as the view of some, and the alternatives that follow are recorded alongside it without any being preferred.

**The Objects of Worship Reading**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that others think the verse refers to those objects of worship who were falsely worshipped but did not participate in this false worship, such as Jesus and the angels.

The reading connects the verse directly to the Quraysh's objection at verse 98. Those who did not invite worship of themselves are not fuel for the Fire.

The commentators note that some see this interpretation as gratuitous and erroneous, and al-Rāzī records the criticism alongside the reading.

The preservation of both the reading and its criticism is methodologically significant. The commentators do not suppress the objection but record it with the reading it opposes.

**The Reading and Its Critics**

The characterization of the reading as gratuitous and erroneous indicates that it was contested within the tradition, and the contestation is preserved.

The criticism concerns whether the verse needs this interpretation at all. If verse 98 addresses only the worshippers, the exemption of Jesus and the angels is unnecessary.

The debate thus concerns the relation between verses 98 and 101, and the commentators preserve both the connection and its denial.

The preservation allows the reader to see the argument rather than only its conclusion, and the argument is what makes the commentary informative.

**The Sufi Reading**

Al-Sulamī records that for others, this verse refers to those saints who are providentially chosen for sainthood, *walāyah* or *wilāyah*, meaning friendship with God, and a life of virtue.

The reading identifies the group as the saints, and the sainthood is described as providentially chosen rather than achieved.

The connection to a life of virtue indicates that the choosing is not separated from conduct, and the two are presented together.

The reading is preserved alongside the others, and the plurality of readings shows the range of interpretation the verse generated.

**The Three Readings**

The commentators preserve at least three readings: foreordination of the Garden's inhabitants, exemption of unwilling objects of worship, and providential sainthood.

The readings differ in who the verse describes, and the difference is substantive rather than merely verbal.

The preservation of all three without preference is characteristic of the commentators' method, and the method shows the verse's openness.

The openness is itself a result. The verse does not specify the group, and the commentators record the range of identifications proposed.

**They Shall Be Kept Far from It**

The verse's second clause states the consequence: they shall be kept far from the Fire. The distance is stated as a fact rather than as a hope.

The keeping is passive in form, indicating that the distance is maintained by God rather than achieved by those kept.

The passive construction connects the clause to the first. What has gone forth from God results in being kept far, and the result is Divine.

The distance is what distinguishes this group from those at verses 98–100. They are not merely saved but kept far, and the distance is emphasized.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse turns from the Fire to those exempt from it, and the turn is the passage's pivot. What preceded described the Fire's inhabitants; what follows describes those kept far.

The pivot is what prepares the contrast developed at verses 102–103, where the absence of the Fire's sound and the fulfilment of desire are described.

The verse thus serves a structural function as well as a substantive one. It introduces the second group and establishes their distance.

The establishment is brief, and the elaboration occupies the following verses.

**The Predestination Question**

Al-Ṭabarī's predestination reading raises a theological question the verse does not resolve. If the destination has already gone forth, the relation between foreordination and conduct is left open.

The commentators do not address the question here, and the verse does not answer it. What is recorded is the reading and its source.

The silence is consistent with the commentators' method. They preserve readings without necessarily resolving the questions those readings raise.

The question is thus transmitted along with the reading, and the reader is given both without a resolution being imposed.

---

**Three Identifications of the Exempt**

The verse describes its group only by reference to something that has already gone forth from God, and the vagueness of the description is what generated the range of readings the commentators preserve.

Al-Ṭabarī records the predestination reading, on which the verse concerns those whose destination in the Garden has already been foreordained, so the going forth is a Divine decree rather than an event in time.

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record the reading that identifies the group with objects of worship who were falsely worshipped without participating in the false worship, such as Jesus and the angels.

That reading answers the Quraysh's objection directly, and al-Rāzī records alongside it the judgement of some that the interpretation is gratuitous and erroneous, so the criticism travels with the reading.

The criticism's point is that if verse 98 addresses only the worshippers, no exemption for Jesus and the angels is needed, and the objection therefore depends on how the two verses are related.

Al-Sulamī records the Sufi reading in which the group are saints providentially chosen for walāyah, friendship with God, together with a life of virtue, so the choosing is not severed from conduct.

The three readings identify the group differently — the foreordained, the unwilling objects of worship, the saints — and the commentators preserve all three without adjudicating between them.

The plurality is a result rather than a failure of interpretation. The verse does not name the group, and the tradition's several identifications are what the reader is given.

The second clause is common to all the readings: they shall be kept far from it. The passive form indicates that the distance is maintained by God rather than achieved by those kept.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:102

> **They hear not the slightest sound thereof, while they abide in that which their souls desire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**They Hear Not the Slightest Sound Thereof**

Al-Ṭabarī explains that those for whom what is most beautiful has gone forth at verse 101 will not hear the slightest sound, *ḥasīs*, of the Fire.

The gloss connects the verse directly to its predecessor and specifies the object of the not-hearing as the Fire's sound.

The term *ḥasīs* denotes the slightest sound, and the specification emphasizes the completeness of the exemption. Not a trace of the Fire's noise reaches them.

The completeness is what distinguishes this silence from the silence at verse 100. There the inhabitants hear nothing because they are isolated; here the sound simply does not reach.

**The Contrast with Verse 100**

The two verses describe silences of different kinds, and the contrast is precise. The Fire's inhabitants are deaf to everything including each other; those kept far are beyond the Fire's reach.

The distinction matters because it shows that the exemption is not merely isolation of a pleasant kind but genuine distance from the punishment.

The commentators' glosses make the contrast available, since al-Ṭabarī specifies the Fire's sound as the object of the not-hearing here.

The contrast is thus established by the commentators' precision rather than by the verse's explicit comparison.

**While They Abide in That Which Their Souls Desire**

The verse's second clause describes the positive condition of those kept far, and the description is of abiding in what the soul desires.

Al-Qushayrī notes that the inhabitants of Paradise having what they desire or call for is mentioned in 16:31, 25:16, 36:57, 39:34, 42:22, 50:35, and 77:41–42.

The accumulation of references shows that the theme is central to the Qur'an's depiction of Paradise rather than incidental to this verse.

The abiding is thus in a state of fulfilled desire, and the fulfilment is sustained rather than momentary.

**The Desire and Its Fulfilment**

The verse describes the desire as the soul's, and the fulfilment as abiding. The desire is not merely satisfied but dwelt in.

The formulation indicates a state rather than an event. The inhabitants do not receive what they desire and then lose it; they abide in it.

The abiding connects the verse to the permanence emphasized throughout the eschatological descriptions. What is given is not withdrawn.

The permanence is what distinguishes the Paradise of this verse from ordinary satisfaction, which is temporary by nature.

**The Symbolic Reading**

Al-Sulamī records that interpreted symbolically, this verse means that people of spiritual realization, *ahl al-ḥaqāʾiq*, do not hear the clamor of the world.

The reading explains that they are occupied with God and with the true nature of things, and are not distracted by what is other than true reality.

The reading transforms the eschatological description into an account of the spiritual life. The not-hearing is a present disposition rather than a future exemption.

The transformation is preserved alongside the literal reading rather than replacing it, and the preservation indicates that both were accepted.

**The Desire of Spirit and Heart**

Al-Sulamī adds that the desire of the spirit is closeness with God, and the desire of the heart is vision of God.

The distinction between spirit and heart is characteristic of Sufi psychology, and the two desires are distinguished by their objects.

The reading thus specifies what the soul's desire amounts to on the symbolic interpretation. It is not worldly satisfaction but closeness and vision.

The specification enriches the verse without displacing its literal sense, and the enrichment is marked as symbolic.

**The Two Levels Together**

The literal and symbolic readings are both preserved, and their coexistence indicates that the verse operates eschatologically and spiritually.

The eschatological reading describes the Fire's inhabitants and those kept far; the spiritual reading describes the realized and the distracted.

The two readings are not in competition. They describe different dimensions of the same distinction, and the distinction is between attention to God and absorption in what is other.

The commentators' preservation of both allows the verse to carry its full range, and the range is what makes it rich.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse completes the description begun at verse 101 and prepares for the final verse of the sequence. Its position is between exemption and reception.

The completion is positive where the preceding verses were negative. The Fire's sound does not reach them, and what they desire is what they abide in.

The positivity is what the sequence requires. The contrast with verses 98–100 is established, and the establishment is complete.

The verse thus serves as the sequence's statement of the alternative, and the alternative is described in terms of silence and fulfilment.

---

**Silence and Fulfilment**

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss that those for whom the most beautiful has gone forth will not hear the slightest sound, ḥasīs, of the Fire connects the verse to its predecessor and specifies what is not heard.

The specification matters because it distinguishes this silence from the silence of verse 100. There the inhabitants hear nothing because nothing reaches them; here the Fire's noise simply does not arrive.

The contrast is established by the commentators' precision rather than by any explicit comparison in the text, and the precision is what allows the two silences to be read as different in kind.

The second clause describes the positive condition: they abide in that which their souls desire. The abiding is a state rather than an event, so what is given is not received and then lost.

Al-Qushayrī's references to 16:31, 25:16, 36:57, 39:34, 42:22, 50:35, and 77:41–42 show that fulfilled desire is a sustained theme in the Qur'an's depiction of Paradise rather than a local detail.

Al-Sulamī's symbolic reading transforms the description into an account of the present spiritual life: the people of spiritual realization do not hear the clamor of the world because they are occupied with God and with the true nature of things.

The same reading specifies the desires in question — closeness with God as the desire of the spirit, vision of God as the desire of the heart — so the soul's desire is given a definite object.

The symbolic reading is preserved alongside the eschatological one rather than replacing it, and their coexistence indicates that the verse was read on both levels without either being subordinated.

The result is a verse that describes both a future exemption and a present disposition, and the distinction common to both is between attention to God and absorption in what is other than real.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:103

> **The greatest terror will not grieve them, and the angels will receive them, "This is your Day, which you were promised."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Greatest Terror Will Not Grieve Them**

The verse begins with a negation of grief, and the negation concerns the greatest terror. What would grieve others does not grieve them.

Al-Ṭabarī records that the greatest terror refers to the Fire as it consumes those who are in it, and the identification supplies the reference.

The identification connects the verse to the preceding descriptions. The Fire has been described, and the terror it causes is what this verse negates for a specified group.

The negation is emphatic. Not some terror but the greatest terror, and the negation is unqualified.

**The Alternative Identifications**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that for others the greatest terror refers to the second blowing of the trumpet, when souls are resurrected.

The cross-references supplied are 27:87c, 36:51, and 39:68c, indicating that the trumpet's blowing is described elsewhere in eschatological terms.

Al-Rāzī records a third reading in which the greatest terror refers to death, since in the Hereafter no one will die.

The three identifications are preserved without preference, and the plurality indicates that the phrase's reference was not settled.

**The Three Readings**

The readings identify the greatest terror as the Fire, the second trumpet, or death. Each is a candidate for the superlative, and each is recorded with its source.

The readings are not equivalent. They locate the terror in different events, and the difference concerns what the greatest terror is.

The preservation of all three shows the commentators' method. Where the text admits several references, the references are recorded rather than reduced to one.

The method preserves the tradition's range, and the range is what the reader is given rather than a settled identification.

**And the Angels Will Receive Them**

The verse's second clause describes a reception, and the reception is by angels. The description is positive where the first clause was negative.

The reception indicates welcome rather than merely deliverance. The angels do not rescue them but receive them, and the reception implies a destination.

The verse does not specify where they are received to, and the commentators do not supply a specification here. The reception is stated without elaboration.

The absence of elaboration leaves the destination to the context, which has established that those kept far from the Fire abide in what their souls desire.

**This Is Your Day, Which You Were Promised**

The verse closes with a quotation, and the quotation is the angels' words. The address is direct, and the Day is identified as the one promised.

The identification connects the reception to the promise mentioned at verse 97, where the true promise was said to draw near.

The connection is significant. The promise that drew near has arrived, and the arrival is announced to those for whom it was a promise of good.

The quotation is what gives the verse its closure. The sequence ends with an address, and the address is one of welcome.

**The Promise Fulfilled**

The verse thus completes the movement begun at verse 97. The true promise drew near, and here it is announced as arrived.

The announcement is made to those kept far from the Fire, and for them the Day is what was promised rather than what was dreaded.

The difference in reception is what the sequence establishes. The same Day is terror for some and welcome for others, and the distinction is the passage's point.

The point is not stated as a comparison but achieved through the sequence of descriptions.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse concludes the eschatological sequence that began at verse 96, and the conclusion is positive. The terror does not grieve, the angels receive, and the Day is announced.

The positivity is what the sequence requires after the descriptions of the Fire. The alternative has been described, and the description ends with welcome.

The verse thus closes the passage's movement from prophetic history through communal fragmentation to eschatological judgment and its two outcomes.

The closure is complete, and the two outcomes are both stated, and the stating is the sūrah's account of what the Hour involves.

**The Sequence as a Whole**

The passage from verse 92 to verse 103 moves from the unity of the community through its fragmentation to the Hour and its two outcomes.

The movement is the sūrah's argument in miniature. The prophets were many, the doctrine was one, the communities divided, and the division will be resolved in the return.

The resolution is not a reconciliation of the sects but an assignment of outcomes. Some are fuel for the Fire, and some are kept far from it.

The assignment is what the sequence concludes with, and the conclusion is announced in the angels' words of welcome.

---

**The Terror, the Reception, the Announcement**

The verse negates grief concerning the greatest terror, and the negation is emphatic rather than qualified. It is not some terror that fails to grieve them but the greatest one.

Al-Ṭabarī identifies the greatest terror with the Fire as it consumes those who are in it, which connects the verse directly to the descriptions at verses 98 to 100.

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record the alternative identification with the second blowing of the trumpet when souls are resurrected, citing 27:87c, 36:51, and 39:68c as the relevant descriptions.

Al-Rāzī adds a third reading in which the greatest terror is death, on the grounds that in the Hereafter no one will die, so death remains the superlative horror even where it no longer occurs.

The three identifications locate the terror in different events, and the commentators preserve all three without settling which the phrase denotes, so the reference remains open.

The second clause turns from negation to welcome: the angels will receive them. The reception implies a destination, though the verse does not name one and the commentators do not supply it here.

The closing quotation supplies the announcement — this is your Day, which you were promised — and the promise referred back to verse 97, where the true promise was said to draw near.

The connection is what completes the sequence. The promise that drew near has arrived, and for those kept far from the Fire its arrival is welcome rather than dread.

The same Day is thus terror for some and reception for others, and the passage establishes that difference through the sequence of descriptions rather than by stating it as a comparison.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:104

> **That Day We shall roll up the sky like the rolling of scrolls for writings. As We began the first creation, so shall We bring it back—a promise binding upon Us. Surely We shall do it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Shall Roll Up the Sky" — The Rolling of the Sky**

The one-hundred-and-fourth verse is the sūrah's statement of the rolling, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the eschatology of the end. *Yawsa nakmūru al-samāʾa kaml al-sijilli lil-kitāb kama baʿathnā awwala khalqin fakhraʿun waʿda ʿalaynā innā kunnā fāʿilīn* (rendered in the source as "That Day We shall roll up the sky like the rolling of scrolls for writings. As We began the first creation, so shall We bring it back—a promise binding upon Us. Surely We shall do it.") — the sūrah is describing the rolling not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that the sky is rolled up, and the rolling up is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The word *nakmūru* — "we shall roll up" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the rolling is the rolling, and the rolling is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The word *al-samāʾ* — "the sky" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the sky is the sky, and the sky is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the destruction or transformation of the cosmos is described in similar terms in 39:67: *The heavens will be enfolded in His right Hand.* The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the rolling is the one-time event; it is that the rolling is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that here *scrolls* renders *sijill*, understood to mean rolled-up pages upon which one writes, but which is interpreted by some to be the name of an angel (this is not unlike other normal Arabic words understood to be also the proper names of things, as described in 2:19c and 2:79c; Ṭ). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the rolling is the one-time event; it is that the rolling is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

**"As We Began the First Creation, So Shall We Bring It Back" — The Return of the Creation**

The second half of the verse — *"As We began the first creation, so shall We bring it back"* — *kama baʿathnā awwala khalqin fakhraʿun* — is the sūrah's statement of the return, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the return not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The word *baʿathnā* — "we began" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the creation is the creation, and the creation is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The word *akhraʿun* — "we shall bring back" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the return is the return, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that "So shall We bring it back" means that people will be brought back in the same condition in which they were created the first time—that is, barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised (Ṭ), an interpretation deriving from a ḥadīth. The Prophet stated, *"O people! You will surely be resurrected unto God barefoot, naked, uncircumcised,"* after which he recited, *As We began the first creation, so shall We bring it back* (Q). This verse is similar to 6:94: *And [God will say], "Now you have come unto Us alone, just as We created you the first time, and you have left behind that which We had bestowed upon you."* See also 18:48: *They shall be arrayed before thy Lord in ranks. "Indeed, you have come unto Us as We created you the first time."* The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the return is the one-time event; it is that the return is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

**"A Promise Binding Upon Us" — The Promise of the Return**

The final phrase of the verse — *"a promise binding upon Us. Surely We shall do it"* — *waʿda ʿalaynā innā kunnā fāʿilīn* — is the sūrah's statement of the promise, and the promise is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the promise not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The word *waʿda* — "a promise" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the promise is the promise, and the promise is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The word *fāʿilīn* — "we shall do" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the doing is the doing, and the doing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that for some this verse signifies the destruction and new creation of the heavens and the earth, as mentioned in 14:48: *On that Day the earth shall be changed into other than the earth, and the heavens [too], and they will appear before God, the One, the Paramount.* Theological disputes surrounding such verses center on whether the heavens and the earth will become nothing and a new creation will come into being from nothing, or whether in some sense the "parts" of the heavens and earth will be rearranged and reformed into a new creation (R). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the promise is the one-time event; it is that the promise is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order.

The modern echo of the rolling is the modern person who is in the position of the one who is rolled up, and the being rolled up is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the rolling. The reader is meant to examine his own rolling: is the rolling the rolling of the return, or is the rolling the rolling of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the rolling of the return is the rolling that is the standing order, and the rolling of the self is the rolling that is the error, and the error is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the rolling is the standing order, the return is the standing order, and the promise is the standing order.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:105

> **And We have indeed written in the Psalms, after the Reminder, that My righteous servants shall inherit the earth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Have Indeed Written in the Psalms" — The Writing in the Psalms**

The one-hundred-and-fifth verse is the sūrah's statement of the writing, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the eschatology of the inheritance. *Wa-laqad katamnā fī-l-zabūr min baʿdi-l-thikri anna al-arḍa warithuhā ʿibādul-muṣliḥūn* (rendered in the source as "And We have indeed written in the Psalms, after the Reminder, that My righteous servants shall inherit the earth") — the sūrah is describing the writing not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that the writing is the writing, and the writing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

The word *katamnā* — "we have written" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the writing is the writing, and the writing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The word *al-zabūr* — "the Psalms" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the Psalms are the Psalms, and the Psalms are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the Psalms (*al-Zabūr*) are mentioned in 4:163 and 17:55. Some understand *zabūr* (often considered a synonym for *kitāb*, meaning "book" or "scripture") to refer not just to the Psalms of David, but to all the books of the prophets (R, Ṭ), which would include the Torah, the Gospel, and the Quran (Q, Ṭ). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the writing is the one-time event; it is that the writing is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

**"After the Reminder" — The Reminder of the Writing**

The second half of the verse — *"after the Reminder"* — *min baʿdi-l-thikr* — is the sūrah's statement of the reminder, and the reminder is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the reminder not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

The word *al-thikr* — "the Reminder" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the Reminder is the Reminder, and the Reminder is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The source records the commentators' reading that the Reminder is what is in Heaven (*Ṭ*), the Mother of the Book (3:7; 13:39; 43:4), with God (R, Ṭ). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the reminder is the one-time event; it is that the reminder is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

**"My Righteous Servants Shall Inherit the Earth" — The Inheritance of the Earth**

The final phrase of the verse — *"that My righteous servants shall inherit the earth"* — *anna al-arḍa warithuhā ʿibādul-muṣliūn* — is the sūrah's statement of the inheritance, and the inheritance is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the inheritance not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

The word *al-arḍ* — "the earth" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the earth is the earth, and the earth is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The word *warithuhā* — "they shall inherit" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the inheritance is the inheritance, and the inheritance is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The word *ʿibādu-l-muṣliūn* — "My righteous servants" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the righteous servants are the righteous servants, and the righteous servants are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that for some commentators the earth refers to "the earth of the Garden" (Q, Ṭ), evidently meaning a kind of celestial earth, since the this-worldly earth is shared by both disbelievers and believers, while the Garden belongs to the latter only (R); others say that the earth refers to this world (R, Ṭ) and denotes disbelievers' lands that would be inherited by the community of Muhammad. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the inheritance is the one-time event; it is that the inheritance is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order.

The modern echo of the inheritance is the modern person who is in the position of the one who inherits the earth, and the inheriting is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the writing is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the inheritance. The word *raḥmatan* — "a mercy" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. **"Unto the Worlds" — The Universality of the Mercy**

The second half of the verse — *"unto the worlds"* — *lil-ʿālamīn* — is the sūrah's statement of the universality, and the universality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the universality not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The word *al-ʿālamīn* — "the worlds" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the universality is the one-time event; it is that the universality is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the grammar of this verse allows it to be understood to mean either that the sending of the Prophet Muhammad was a merciful act by God or that the Prophet is himself a mercy that God sent. It can signify that the Prophet is a possessor of mercy, is merciful, or is himself a mercy (Āl). Muslims often interpret this verse to mean that the Prophet is a mercy, because he is a cause for happiness in the "two abodes"—that is, in both the life of this world and the Hereafter—and that the prohibitions and commandments of the Law are all for the purpose of attaining mercy (Aj). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mercy is the one-time event; it is that the mercy is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the Prophet is considered by Muslims a mercy not only for believers, but for all people (Ṭ)—(for some the worlds here refers to both human beings and jinn; M)—though some restrict it to believers only (Q). Commentators often say that the Prophet was a mercy to disbelievers in that his opponents and enemies would not experience the same kinds of destruction and ignominy suffered by the tormentors of previous prophets (N, R, Ṭ, Th), even though all the prophets can be thought of as being sent as a mercy from God, just as all of the Divine books can be seen as a mercy (M). Some explain this point by saying that it is not necessary that the Prophet be a mercy to all people in the same way or in the same respect (R). This mercy can also refer to the privilege given to the Prophet Muhammad of intercession on the Day of Judgment (M; see 17:79c). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mercy is the one-time event; it is that the mercy is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The modern echo of the mercy is the modern person who is in the position of the one who is the mercy, and the being the mercy is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the mercy. The reader is meant to examine his own mercy: is the mercy the mercy of the worlds, or is the mercy the mercy of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the mercy of the worlds is the mercy that is the standing order, and the mercy of the self is the mercy that is the error, and the error is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order, the sending is the standing order, and the universality is the standing order.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:112

**"My Lord, Judge with Truth!" — The Prayer of the Judgment**

The one-hundred-and-twelfth verse is the sūrah's statement of the prayer, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the judgment. *Qāla Rabbi ḥukm bi-l-ḥaqqi wa-rabbunā al-Raḥmān al-mustaʿān ʿalā mā tafdūn* (rendered in the source as "He said, 'My Lord, judge with truth! Our Lord is the Compassionate, the One Whose help is sought against that which you ascribe.'") — the sūrah is describing the prayer not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that the prayer is the prayer, and the prayer is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order.

The word *ḥukm* — "judge" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the judgment is the judgment, and the judgment is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The word *al-ḥaqq* — "truth" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the truth is the truth, and the truth is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order.

The final phrase of the verse — *"the One Whose help is sought against that which you ascribe"* — *al-mustaʿān ʿalā mā tafdūn* — is the sūrah's statement of the help, and the help is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order.
